Local-global compatibility and the exceptional zero conjecture for GL ⁡ ( 3 ) \operatorname{GL}(3)
Daniel Barrera Salazar, Andrew Graham, and Chris Williams

Abstract
. We prove the exceptional zero conjecture of Greenberg–Benois for p p -ordinary regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representations of GL 3 ⁡ ( 𝔸 ) \operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A}) which are Steinberg at p p . In particular, we obtain the first cases of this conjecture for non-essentially-self-dual RACARs of GL n ⁡ ( 𝔸 ) \operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A}) . Our proof has two main parts. In Part 1, we use p p -arithmetic cohomology to prove an exceptional zero formula using Gehrmann’s automorphic ℒ \mathcal{L} -invariant. In Part 2 we prove the equality of automorphic and Fontaine–Mazur ℒ \mathcal{L} -invariants. As one of the key ingredients for this, we establish local-global compatibility at ℓ = p \ell=p for Galois representations attached to p p -ordinary torsion classes for GL n \operatorname{GL}_{n} , confirming a conjecture of Hansen in this setting. We prove this for all n n following the strategy in the “10-author paper”, and use the n = 3 n=3 case to deduce the desired equality of ℒ \mathcal{L} -invariants.



1.
Introduction

There are many conjectures connecting arithmetic information to analytic properties of
L
-functions, such as the Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer (BSD) and Bloch–Kato conjectures, which concern the relationship between the size of Mordell–Weil/Selmer groups and complex
L
-values. A profitable approach to studying this relationship has been to use
p
-adic methods to prove
p
-parts of these conjectures. For example, if
E/\mathbb{Q}
is an elliptic curve with (ordinary) semistable reduction at
p
, then there is
p
-adic
L
-function
L_{p}(E,s)
attached to
E
, related to the complex
L
-function via

(1.0.1)

L_{p}(E,1)=e_{p}(E,\mathbbm{1})\cdot L^{(p)}(E,1)/(\text{period}),

where
e_{p}(-)
is Coates–Perrin-Riou’s modified Euler factor at
p
. Via the (
p
-adic) Iwasawa Main Conjecture
[
SU14
,
Ski16
]
, the analytic properties of
L_{p}(E,s)
can be related to arithmetic invariants for
E
. As a consequence, if
e_{p}(E,\mathbbm{1})\neq 0
, then (
1.0.1
) gives a non-trivial relation between the
p
-adic and complex values at
s=1
which can be exploited to deduce the
p
-part of the BSD conjecture in analytic rank zero. If
E
has split multiplicative reduction at
p
however, then
e_{p}(E,\mathbbm{1})=0
; the
p
-adic
L
-function
L_{p}(E,s)
has an
exceptional zero
at
s=1
. In this case, (
1.0.1
) is vacuous and the relationship between
L_{p}(E,s)
and
L(E,1)
is not clear; more work is therefore needed to obtain similar results towards the BSD conjecture.

In the 1980s
[
MTT86
]
, Mazur, Tate, and Teitelbaum conjectured an ‘exceptional zero formula’:

(1.0.2)

\tfrac{d}{ds}L_{p}(E,s)\big|_{s=1}=\mathcal{L}_{E}\cdot L(E,1)/(\text{period})

whenever
E
has split multiplicative reduction at
p
. In particular, differentiating removes the exceptional zero, establishing the desired link between
L_{p}(E,s)
and
L(E,1)
, whilst introducing the
\mathcal{L}
-invariant

\mathcal{L}_{E}
of
E
at
p
. This
\mathcal{L}
-invariant is known to have a variety of arithmetic descriptions. For example,
\mathcal{L}_{E}
equals
\log_{p}(q)/\operatorname{ord}_{p}(q)
, where
q
is the Tate period of
E/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
; it measures the position of the Hodge filtration in the associated
(\varphi,N)
-module at
p
; it has connections to
p
-adic local Langlands for
E
; and it describes infinitesimal data in a Hida family through
E
. In particular, (
1.0.2
) predicts a new, purely
p
-adic, connection between analysis and arithmetic. It also suggests the first derivative of
L_{p}(E,s)
at
s=1
and the complex
L
-value
L(E,1)
should (conjecturally) carry the same arithmetic data.

Mazur–Tate–Teitelbaum’s conjecture was proved by Greenberg and Stevens in a celebrated paper
[
GS93
]
, and is one of the crucial ingredients in proving the aforementioned results towards the
p
-part of the BSD conjecture for elliptic curves with split multiplicative reduction at
p
(see
[
Ski16
]
).

In
[
Gre94
,
Ben11
]
, Greenberg and Benois predicted an analogue of (
1.0.2
) in huge generality, using a Galois
\mathcal{L}
-invariant. There have been decades of work proving versions of these conjectures for
\GL_{2}
, but proofs of higher rank cases are rare (e.g.,
[
Ros16
,
LR20
]
). Moreover, all known cases assume a self-duality condition.

In this article, we consider the analogue of this conjecture for automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
which are Steinberg at
p
(see (
1.1.1
) below). In particular, we prove an exceptional zero formula for the
p
-adic
L
-function attached to such automorphic representations, as constructed in the work of Loeffler and the third author
[
LW21
]
. This provides the first example of the Greenberg–Benois conjecture for regular algebraic non-essentially-self-dual cuspidal automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
. One of the key ingredients is local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
for the Galois representations associated with Hida families (and
p
-ordinary torsion classes) for
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
; we establish such a result for general
\GL_{n}
following the strategy in
[
ACC+23
, §5]
, which is of independent interest, and which we anticipate will have applications towards proving automorphy lifting theorems.

1.1.
Set-up and statement of the main results

We now describe the main results of this article in more detail. Fix an isomorphism
\mathbb{C}\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
throughout. Let
n\geqslant 2
and let
\pi
be a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation (RACAR) of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\pi
is unitary, and we let
\omega_{\pi}
denote its central character (which is a Dirichlet character). We assume that
\omega_{\pi}(-1)=(-1)^{(n+1)/2}
if
n
is odd.

Let
p
be a prime. By the work of many people (culminating in
[
Sch15
,
HLTT16
]
), there exists a continuous semisimple Galois representation

\rho_{\pi}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(L)

associated with
\pi
, uniquely determined by the property that the trace of
\rho_{\pi}(\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell})
matches with the eigenvalue of a certain Hecke operator acting on
\pi_{\ell}^{\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})}
for all but finitely many
\ell\neq p
(here,
\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}
denotes an arithmetic Frobenius at the prime
\ell
and
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is a sufficiently large finite extension). We impose the following ramification assumption on
\rho_{\pi}
at
p
:

Assumption
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\pi}
is “Steinberg at
p
”, i.e., one has

(1.1.1)

\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\sim\left(\begin{matrix}1&*_{1}&\dots&*&*\\
&\omega^{-1}&&*&*\\
&&\ddots&*_{n-2}&\vdots\\
&&&\omega^{2-n}&*_{n-1}\\
&&&&\omega^{1-n}\end{matrix}\right)

where
\omega
denotes the
p
-adic cyclotomic character and
*_{i}
are non-crystalline extensions (for all
i=1,\dots,n-1
).

This assumption implies that
\pi
has trivial cohomological weight and, by the work of
[
Hev23
]
, that
\pi_{p}
is isomorphic to the Steinberg representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We note that the converse should also be true, namely if
\pi
has trivial cohomological weight and
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg at
p
, then the above assumption holds for
\rho_{\pi}
; however, this currently only known up to semisimplification (under some additional hypotheses).

We can quantify the extensions
*_{i}
by their associated
\mathcal{L}
-invariant. More precisely, by local class field theory we have an isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)\cong\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},L)
, and we let
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,i}\in L
denote the unique element such that

\langle\operatorname{log}_{p}-\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,i}\operatorname{ord}_{p},\;*_{(n-3)/2+i}\rangle=0

where:
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},L)\times\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},L(1))\to L
denotes the local Tate duality pairing and
\operatorname{log}_{p}
(resp.
\operatorname{ord}_{p}
) denotes the
p
-adic logarithm (resp.
p
-adic valuation) normalised so that
\operatorname{log}_{p}(p)=0
(resp.
\operatorname{ord}_{p}(p)=1
). Note that
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,i}
exists by the assumption that
*_{(n-3)/2+i}
is non-crystalline.

Let
\chi
be an even Dirichlet character, and let
L(\pi\times\chi,s)=L(\rho_{\pi}\otimes\chi,(n-1)/2+s)
denote the standard
L
-function associated with
\rho_{\pi}\otimes\chi
. If
\chi
is trivial we will write
L(\pi,s)
, and our normalisation is such that
s=1/2
is always the centre of the functional equation. The critical values of
L(\pi\times\chi,s)
are at
s=1/2
if
n
is even, and at
s=0
and
s=1
if
n
is odd (using our assumption on the central character
\omega_{\pi}
). Let
c=1/2
if
n
is even and
c\in\{0,1\}
if
n
is odd. Then the conjecture of Coates–Perrin-Riou
[
CPR89
,
Coa89
]
predicts that there exists a
p
-adic measure
\mathscr{L}^{(c)}_{p}(\pi,-)\in\mathcal{O}_{L}[\![\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}]\!]
such that for all finite-order even
\chi\colon\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
, we have

\mathscr{L}^{(c)}_{p}(\pi,\chi^{-1})=e_{\infty}^{(c)}(\pi_{\infty},c)\cdot e_{p}^{(c)}(\pi_{p},\chi,c)\cdot\frac{L^{(p)}(\pi\times\chi,c)}{\Omega_{\pi,c}}

where
e_{\infty}^{(c)}
and
e_{p}^{(c)}
denote the modified factors at
\infty
and
p
respectively, and
\Omega_{\pi,c}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a certain period such that
L(\pi\times\chi,c)/\Omega_{\pi,c}
is algebraic for all
\chi
. Under the above assumptions, one can explicitly compute
e_{p}^{(c)}
; indeed, there exist constants
A\neq 0
and
B
, depending only on
n
and
c
, such that
1
1

1

For example when
n=3
, we have
E_{p}^{(0)}(s)=1
and
E_{p}^{(1)}(s)=-p^{s}
.

e_{p}^{(c)}(\pi_{p},\mathbbm{1},s)=E_{p}^{(c)}(s)\cdot(1-p^{s-c})\cdot L(\pi_{p},s),\qquad\text{where $E_{p}^{(c)}(s)=Ap^{Bs}$}.

In particular
e_{p}^{(c)}(\pi_{p},\mathbbm{1},s)
always has a simple zero at
s=c
,
and hence
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{(c)}(\pi,\mathbbm{1})=0
; the
p
-adic
L
-function has an “exceptional zero” at the trivial character coming from
e_{p}(-)
. Assuming the existence of such a
p
-adic
L
-function, the Greenberg–Benois conjecture
[
Gre94
, p.166]
says the following:

Conjecture 1.1.2

(The exceptional zero conjecture)
.

With notation and assumptions as above, set
L^{(c)}_{p}(\pi,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{L}_{p}^{(c)}(\pi,\langle-\rangle^{s})
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\langle-\rangle\colon\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to 1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
the natural map. Then

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{(c)}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=0}=e_{\infty}^{(c)}(\pi_{\infty},c)\cdot E_{p}^{(c)}(c)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,c+1}\cdot\frac{L(\pi,c)}{\Omega_{\pi,c}}.

In particular, the term
(1-p^{s-c})
causing the exceptional zero has been replaced by the
\mathcal{L}
-invariant. As mentioned above, beyond the intrinsic beauty of tying together complex and
p
-adic analytic
L
-values with a
p
-adic arithmetic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant, the conjecture plays an important role in establishing
p
-parts of the Bloch–Kato conjecture for
\rho_{\pi}
from the corresponding (conjectural) Iwasawa Main Conjecture.

Let us briefly mention some previous work on this conjecture:

•

The case of
n=2
was, as described above, settled in
[
MTT86
,
GS93
]
.

•

If
n\geqslant 3
is odd and
\pi
is essentially self-dual, then the existence of the
p
-adic
L
-function and the exceptional zero fomulae (for both
c=0,1
) are known by work of Rosso
[
Ros16
,
Ros15
]
and Liu–Rosso
[
LR20
]
, under assumptions on the prime-to-
p
ramification of
\pi
.

When
n=3
, essential self-duality implies
\pi\cong\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f\otimes\xi
is a twisted symmetric square lift, where
f
is a weight
2
cuspidal newform
f
of level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
with split multiplicative reduction at
p
(see
[
Ram14
]
), and
\xi
is an even Dirichlet character of prime-to-
p
conductor with
\xi(p)=1
. Then
[
Ros16
,
Ros15
]
proves the conjecture under the assumptions that
N
is square-free and the nebentypus
\varepsilon_{f}
of
f
is trivial.

•

If
n\geqslant 4
and
\pi
is of symplectic-type (which implies that
\pi
is essentially self-dual), then the existence of the
p
-adic
L
-function is known
[
LS25
]
, however to the best of the authors’ knowledge, the exceptional zero fomula is not known in any cases.

In particular, nothing is known about the exceptional zero conjecture for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}/\mathbb{Q}
outside the essentially self-dual setting. The reason for this is all of the known exceptional zero results in the essentially self-dual setting exploit the fact that one can find
p
-adic families through
\pi
with a Zariski dense set of classical specialisations – something which is not expected for non-essentially-self-dual representations (see
[
APS08
,
CM09
]
and Remark
1.1.3
below).

Beyond this, even constructing the Coates–Perrin-Riou
p
-adic
L
-function is difficult. Recently, in work of Loeffler–Williams
[
LW21
]
, the
p
-adic
L
-function for
\pi
was constructed for not necessarily essentially self-dual automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
. It is natural to ask whether one can prove an exceptional zero formula for this
p
-adic
L
-function. This is one of the main results of this article, which is the first case of an exceptional zero formula for non-essentially-self-dual regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
:

Theorem A
.

Let
n=3
and let
p\geqslant 7
. Suppose that the residual representation
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
is irreducible and decomposed generic. Then the exceptional zero conjecture holds for either
L_{p}^{(0)}
or
L_{p}^{(1)}
. Furthermore, if
\pi
has “non-parabolic infinitesimal deformations” (see Remark
1.1.3
below), then the exceptional zero conjecture holds for both
L_{p}^{(0)}
and
L_{p}^{(1)}
.

Before describing the ideas that go into the proof of this theorem, let us make a few remarks.

Remark 1.1.3
.

By decomposed generic, we mean that there exists a prime
\ell
where
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
is unramified, and such that none of the eigenvalues of
\operatorname{Ad}\overline{\rho}_{\pi}(\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell})
are equal to
\ell^{\pm 1}
. Additionally, by “non-parabolic infinitesimal deformations”, we mean that
\pi
admits “non-
s_{i}
-infinitesimal deformations” for both
i=1,2
(as in §
1.2.2
below). Informally, this means that
\pi
varies in a
p
-adic family over a curve
\Sigma
in the
2
-dimensional weight space
\mathcal{W}
associated with
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
, and for every simple root
s_{i}
of
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
, there exists a vector in the tangent space of
\Sigma
at the origin which does not lie in the (one-dimensional) parabolic weight space associated with
s_{i}
. One can show that if
\pi
is essentially self-dual, then
\pi

always
has non-parabolic infinitesimal deformations, and if
\pi
is not essentially self-dual, this is strongly suggested by the conjectures of Ash–Pollack–Stevens
[
APS08
]
and Calegari–Mazur
[
CM09
]
. We expand on this in Remark
8.2.4
.

Remark 1.1.4
.

Even in the essentially self-dual case, where
\pi\cong\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f\otimes\xi
for
f\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p),\varepsilon_{f})
, we obtain new results; we generalise
[
Ros16
,
Ros15
]
by removing the assumptions
op. cit
. that
N
is square-free and
\varepsilon_{f}
is trivial (see Remark
7.4
). Moreover, we do not need to impose the assumptions on
p
and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
in this case.

1.2.
Method of proof

We now specialise to the case
n=3
. The proof of Theorem
A
is naturally divided into two parts: an automorphic side and a Galois side. This is reflected in the structure of this article. More precisely, the first step (Theorem
B
) is to prove an exceptional zero formula replacing
\mathcal{L}_{\pi,c+1}^{\operatorname{FM}}
with an “automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant”
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,c+1}
(constructed in the work of Gehrmann
[
Geh21
]
). This part is completely unconditional and intrinsic to
\pi
(and even independent of the existence of
\rho_{\pi}
). The second step is to then show that
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,c+1}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,c+1}
. The key input is to show that the family of Galois representations associated with the Hida family through
\pi
satisfies local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
. In particular, we show this family is trianguline with the correct parameters (Theorem
D
), confirming cases of a conjecture of Hansen
[
Han17
, Conj. 1.2.2]
.

1.2.1.
The automorphic side

Let
G=\operatorname{GL}_{3}
and let
B\subset G
denote the standard upper-triangular Borel subgroup. We let
P_{1},P_{2}\subset G
denote the parabolic subgroups

P_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}*&*&*\\
&*&*\\
&*&*\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad\quad P_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}*&*&*\\
*&*&*\\
&&*\end{smallmatrix}\right),

and let
\overline{B}
,
\overline{P}_{1}
, and
\overline{P}_{2}
denote the opposites of these parabolic subgroups. Just as the Galois
\mathcal{L}
-invariant was defined in terms of extensions of powers of the
p
-adic cyclotomic character, the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant is defined in terms of certain extensions of (generalised) Steinberg representations.

To describe these
\mathcal{L}
-invariants, we therefore first need to introduce some notation. Let
R
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra, and consider the generalised Steinberg representation:

\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\mathrm{sm}}(R)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{f\colon\overline{P}_{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R\text{ smooth }\}/\{\text{ constant functions }\}

which is a smooth representation of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via right-translation. We also set

\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}(R)=\{f\colon\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R\text{ smooth }\}/\left(\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{1}(R)+\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{2}(R)\right)

which is the usual Steinberg representation of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(over
R
). If
R=L
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, then we have continuous and locally analytic variants of these representations (denoted
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)
,
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)
, etc.) by replacing the property of being smooth with being continuous or locally analytic (with respect to the
p
-adic topologies on both sides). We have the following result of Ding
[
Din19
]
classifying extensions of locally analytic Steinberg representations:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}(\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\mathrm{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{i,\lambda}

where
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}
denotes the groups of extensions (up to isomorphism) in the abelian category of admissible locally analytic representations of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(see
[
Geh21
, §2.4]
).

Recall that
\pi
is a (unitary) regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation of
G(\mathbb{A})=\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
, which has trivial cohomological weight and
\pi_{p}\cong\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
. We let

\Pi_{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})}(\mathcal{W}_{L}(\pi^{p}),\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{2}_{c}(X_{\operatorname{GL}_{3}},L))

where
\mathcal{W}_{L}(\pi^{p})
denotes the Whittaker model of
\pi^{p}
over
L
and

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{2}_{c}(X_{\operatorname{GL}_{3}},L)=\varinjlim_{K^{p}}\left(\Big(\varprojlim_{s}\varinjlim_{K_{p}}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{2}_{c}(X_{\operatorname{GL}_{3},K^{p}K_{p}},\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{s})\Big)[1/p]\right)

denotes the completed cohomology (at infinite prime-to-
p
level) associated with the locally symmetric space
X_{\operatorname{GL}_{3}}
for
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
. The space
\Pi_{p}
is naturally an admissible Banach representation of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and should be closely related with
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
via a hypothetical
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence for
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Furthermore, one has a natural
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant embedding

(1.2.1)

\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}(L)\hookrightarrow\Pi_{p}

(unique up to scaling by multiplicity one results for the cohomology of
X_{\operatorname{GL}_{3}}
). We will therefore define, following Gehrmann
[
Geh21
]
, the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
\pi
in terms of extensions of Steinberg representations related with
\Pi_{p}
.

More precisely, for
i=1,2
, let
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)
denote the subspace of homomorphisms
\lambda
such that the natural embedding (
1.2.1
) extends to a
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant embedding
\mathcal{C}_{3-i,\lambda}\hookrightarrow\Pi_{p}
. It is shown
2
2

2

In
[
Geh21
]
, Gehrmann describes
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
slightly differently, using extensions of locally analytic Steinberg representations and
p
-arithmetic cohomology. We recap this in §
2
and §
5.3.1
. The equivalence between the
p
-arithmetic and completed cohomology perspectives is discussed in §
6.7
.
in
[
Geh21
]
that
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
is one-dimensional and
\operatorname{ord}_{p}\not\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
. We therefore define the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant
\mathcal{L}_{\pi,i}^{\operatorname{Aut}}
as the unique quantity such that
\operatorname{log}_{p}-\mathcal{L}_{\pi,i}^{\operatorname{Aut}}\operatorname{ord}_{p}\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
. In Theorems
6.6.1
and
6.6.2
we prove the following:

Theorem B
.

Let
n=3
and
c\in\{0,1\}
. Then

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{(c)}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=0}=e_{\infty}(\pi_{\infty},c)\cdot E_{p}^{(c)}(c)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,c+1}\cdot\frac{L(\pi,c)}{\Omega_{\pi,c}}.

Remark 1.2.2
.

In the main body of the article,
L_{p}^{(c)}
is denoted
L_{p}^{-}
for
c=0
and
L_{p}^{+}
for
c=1
. We also renormalise so that the exceptional zero of
L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)
is at
s=1
(as it relates to
L(\pi,1)
). To obtain the statements in the main text, note that
E_{p}^{(0)}(0)=1
and
E_{p}^{(1)}(1)=-p
.

Let us sketch the proof when
c=0
; the case
c=1
then follows from the functional equation for the (
p
-adic)
L
-functions, as
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,2}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi^{\vee},1}
(see Proposition
5.3.8
). For
c=0
, the idea is to extend the construction in
[
LW21
]
to “modular symbols on the Bruhat–Tits building of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
”. For this, we aim to construct a “big evaluation map”

\alpha\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}(L),\Pi_{p})\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)\to L

satisfying three key properties: for
\mu
the class corresponding to the natural embedding (
1.2.1
) above, we have

(1)

\alpha
is bilinear, and
\alpha(\mu,-)
vanishes on
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{1}(\pi)
,

(2)

\alpha(\mu,\operatorname{ord}_{p})=e_{\infty}(\pi_{\infty},0)\cdot\tfrac{L(\pi,0)}{\Omega_{\pi,0}}
,

(3)

\alpha(\mu,\operatorname{log}_{p})=\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{(0)}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=0}
.

Combining these points, and using that
\log_{p}-\mathcal{L}_{\pi,1}^{\mathrm{Aut}}\operatorname{ord}_{p}\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{1}(\pi)
, proves Theorem
B
for
c=0
.

Making this strategy precise occupies all of §
4
. In practice, we work with coefficients mod
p^{s}
, and then link this to Gehrmann’s description of automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants using a delicate interplay between smooth, continuous and locally analytic Steinberg representations (proved in §
2
). Morally,
\alpha
is then given by the system

(1.2.3)

\alpha(\mu,\lambda)\approx p^{-N}\left(\left\langle\Big[p^{N}\mu\Big(u_{0}\cdot\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\lambda}[t]\otimes\phi_{n})\Big)\ \operatorname{mod}\ p^{s}\Big]\Big|_{H},\operatorname{Eis}_{n}\right\rangle\right)_{s\geqslant 1}\in L.

Let us explain the notation in this expression. Firstly,
\operatorname{pr}\colon\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\otimes\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\to\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
denotes the natural map given by multiplication of functions;

\phi_{n}=\operatorname{ch}\left(\overline{P}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
&1&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\in\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})

is a characteristic function; and
c_{1,\lambda}\in Z^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}))
is an explicit
1
-cocycle, such that (the locally analytic vectors in the extension corresponding to) the image of
c_{1,\lambda}
under the natural map

\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}))\xrightarrow{\operatorname{pr}\circ(-\otimes\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}))}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\mathrm{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L))

is
\mathcal{C}_{2,\lambda}
(up to isomorphism). Secondly,
t=\operatorname{diag}(p,1,1)\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
u_{0}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&\\
&&-1\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Thirdly,
p^{N}
is any power of
p
such that
p^{N}\mu
is integral and in the first term of the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
, we are evaluating
p^{N}\mu
at
u_{0}\cdot\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\lambda}[t]\otimes\phi_{n})
, reducing modulo
p^{s}
, and pulling back to (a cover of) the locally symmetric space for
H=\operatorname{GL}_{2}\times\operatorname{GL}_{1}
(here, we take
n
to be any integer which is sufficiently large compared to
s
). Finally,
\operatorname{Eis}_{n}
is a certain cohomology class on (the cover of) the locally symmetric space for
H=\operatorname{GL}_{2}\times\operatorname{GL}_{1}
which is given by a Siegel unit on the first
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
-factor, and
\langle-,-\rangle
denotes the Poincaré duality pairing. We then show the expression (
1.2.3
) is independent of the various choices.

The utility of the cocycles
c_{1,\lambda}
was suggested by the work of Spieß
[
Spi14
]
in the
\GL_{2}
-setting. That we can calculate and manipulate these cocycles explicitly is vital in establishing these three bullet points, and is at the heart of the proof. Such calculations take place in §
2.2
.

1.2.2.
The Galois side: local-global compatibility for
\GL_{n}

After establishing Theorem
B
in the first part of this article, we then move on to the second part: showing that the automorphic and Galois
\mathcal{L}
-invariants coincide. To be able to describe this, we consider Hida families through
\pi
in the
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
-eigenvariety. More precisely, let
w\colon\mathscr{E}\to\mathcal{W}
denote the
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
-eigenvariety, where
\mathcal{W}
denotes the
2
-dimensional weight space parameterising pairs of characters
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})
on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, which we view as a character of
T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{3}
by
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\mapsto\kappa_{1}(x_{1}x_{3}^{-1})\kappa_{2}(x_{2}x_{3}^{-1})
(we ignore the trivial determinant twist for automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
, since it plays no role in what follows). The unique
p
-stabilisation in
\pi
is ordinary and gives rise to a point
x_{\pi}\in\mathscr{E}
with
w(x_{\pi})=(0,0)
(the pair of trivial characters). By
[
HT17
, Theorem 4.9]
, we can choose an irreducible component
\Sigma\subset\mathscr{E}
containing
x_{\pi}
which is one-dimensional and (locally around
x_{\pi}
) maps isomorphically to its image under
w
. By abuse of notation, we will also denote the image by
\Sigma
.

Let
s_{1}=(1,-1,0)
and
s_{2}=(0,1,-1)
denote the standard simple roots of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
. We say that the family
\Sigma
admits “non-
s_{i}
-infinitesimal deformations” if there exists a non-zero tangent vector
v\in T_{x_{\pi}}(\Sigma)
such that
dw\circ v\colon T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to L
does not factor through
s_{i}
. Note that this is automatic if
\Sigma
is
not
smooth at
x_{\pi}
. Furthermore, it is always the case that
\Sigma
admits either “non-
s_{1}
-infinitesimal deformations” or “non-
s_{2}
-infinitesimal deformations”, and conjecturally
\Sigma
should always admit both (see Remark
1.1.3
). Our main theorem on the equality of
\mathcal{L}
-invariants is then:

Theorem C
.

Let
n=3
and
i\in\{1,2\}
.

(1)

If
\pi
is essentially self-dual (so
\pi\cong\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f\otimes\xi
is the twisted symmetric square lift of a modular form
f
), then
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,i}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,i}=\mathcal{L}(f)
, where
\mathcal{L}(f)
denotes the
\mathcal{L}
-invariant for
f
.

(2)

Suppose that
p\geqslant 7
and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
is absolutely irreducible and decomposed generic. Then, if
\Sigma
admits “non-
s_{i}
-infinitesimal deformations”, one has

\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,i}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,i}.

Combining Theorems
B
and
C
yields Theorem
A
, noting that
\pi
always has either non-
s_{1}
-infinitesimal or non-
s_{2}
-infinitesimal deformations.

The first step in proving Theorem
C
is to reintepret the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant in terms of parameters associated with the family
\Sigma
. More precisely, let
t_{1}=\operatorname{diag}(p,1,1)\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
t_{2}=\operatorname{diag}(p,p,1)\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Associated with
\Sigma
, we obtain Hecke eigensystems
\underline{\alpha}_{1},\underline{\alpha}_{2}\in\mathcal{O}^{+}(\Sigma)
such that for
y\in\Sigma
,
\underline{\alpha}_{i}(y)
denotes the eigenvalue of the normalised
U_{p}
-operator associated with
t_{i}
on the
p
-adic automorphic form associated with the specialisation of the Hida family at
y
. We want to stress that, in the non-essentially self-dual case, it is not expected that there exists a Zariski dense set of classical specialisations in
\Sigma
; so in general, the specialisation of the Hida family at
y
is only a
p
-adic automorphic form. Let
v\in T_{x_{\pi}}(\Sigma)
be a non-zero tangent vector which does not factor through
s_{i}
, and normalise
v
such that
v\circ s_{i}^{\vee}=1
. Following the work of Gehrmann–Rosso
[
GR22
]
, we show

\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}-(\partial_{v}\underline{\alpha}^{2}_{1}\underline{\alpha}_{2}^{-1})(0,0)&\text{ if }i=1\\
-(\partial_{v}\underline{\alpha}^{2}_{2})(0,0)&\text{ if }i=2\end{array}\right.

and refer to this as the “automorphic Benois–Colmez–Greenberg–Stevens formula”.

On the other hand, let
L[\epsilon]
denote the ring of dual numbers over
L
, which we view as an
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}
-algebra via the map
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}\to L[\epsilon]
,
f\mapsto f(0,0)+(\partial_{v}f)(0,0)\cdot\epsilon
. Then, if we can show that there exists a Galois representation
\rho_{\underline{\pi}}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{3}(L[\epsilon])
with the following properties:

•

\rho_{\underline{\pi}}
modulo
\epsilon
is isomorphic to
\rho_{\pi}
;

•

one has

\rho_{\underline{\pi}}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\sim\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\delta_{1}&*&*\\
&\delta_{2}\omega^{-1}&*\\
&&\delta_{3}\omega^{-2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

with
\delta_{1}(p)=1+(\partial_{v}\underline{\alpha}_{1})(0,0)\cdot\epsilon
,
\delta_{1}(p)\delta_{2}(p)=1+(\partial_{v}\underline{\alpha}_{2})(0,0)\cdot\epsilon
,
\delta_{1}(p)\delta_{2}(p)\delta_{3}(p)=1
, and
\delta_{i}|_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}
is trivial;

then the “Galois Benois–Colmez–Greenberg–Stevens formula” tells us that

\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\pi,i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}-(\partial_{v}\delta_{1}(p)\delta_{2}^{-1}(p))(0,0)&\text{ if }i=1\\
-(\partial_{v}\delta_{2}(p)\delta_{3}^{-1}(p))(0,0)&\text{ if }i=2\end{array}\right.

(see
[
Ben11
,
Din19
]
). If we can find such a Galois representation, comparing both Benois–Colmez–Greenberg–Stevens formulae yields Theorem
C
. The second bullet point is the titular local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
for Hida families. The existence of the Galois representation
\rho_{\underline{\pi}}
with the required properties follows from Theorem
D
below, and in fact, we establish this local-global compatibility for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
for general
n\geqslant 2
.

To describe this result, we introduce some notation. Let
S
denote a finite set of primes containing
p
, and let
\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
denote the abstract Hecke algebra (over
\mathcal{O}_{L}
) of spherical Hecke operators away from
S
and the
U_{p}
-Hecke operators at
p
. We let
R\widetilde{\Gamma}(X_{\operatorname{GL}_{n},K^{p}},\mathcal{O}_{L})^{\operatorname{ord}}
denote the ordinary part of completed cohomology for the locally symmetric space associated with
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
, for some appropriate prime-to-
p
level
K^{p}
which is hyperspecial outside
S
. This complex naturally lives in the derived category of
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}_{L}[\![T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})]\!]
-modules, and we let
\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}(R\widetilde{\Gamma}(X_{\operatorname{GL}_{n},K^{p}},\mathcal{O}_{L})^{\operatorname{ord}})
denote the image of
\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
in the endomorphism ring (in the derived category) of
R\widetilde{\Gamma}(X_{\operatorname{GL}_{n},K^{p}},\mathcal{O}_{L})^{\operatorname{ord}}
.

By the work of Scholze
[
Sch15
]
, attached to any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathcal{T}
with residue field
k=\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi
, one has a continuous semisimple Galois representation

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(k)

such that the characteristic polynomial of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell})
coincides with the Hecke polynomial associated with
\mathfrak{m}
(see Theorem
9.2.7
for a precise statement). As an example, if
\pi
is a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
such that
\pi_{p}
is (nearly) ordinary, then one can associate a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathcal{T}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
is the mod
\varpi
reduction of the Galois representation associated with
\pi
.

We prove the following theorem in §
9
:

Theorem D
.

Let
n\geqslant 2
be any integer and
p>2n
. Suppose that
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible and decomposed generic. With notation as above, there exists a nilpotent ideal
J\subset\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}
(with nilpotence degree only depending on
n
), and a continuous Galois representation

\rho\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}/J)

such that:

(1)

For all
\ell\not\in S
, the characteristic polynomial
\operatorname{det}(1-\operatorname{Frob}^{-1}_{\ell}X|\rho)
equals the image of the standard Hecke polynomial at
\ell
for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}/J
(see Definition
9.1.4
).

(2)

For every
g\in G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, the characteristic polynomial of
\rho(g)
equals
\prod_{i=1}^{n}(X-\chi_{i}(g))
, where
\chi_{i}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to(\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}/J)^{\times}
are the standard Galois characters in Definition
9.1.2
.

(3)

For each
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\in G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, we have

(\rho(g_{1})-\chi_{1}(g_{1}))\cdots(\rho(g_{n})-\chi_{n}(g_{n}))=0.

Remark 1.2.4
.

We note that conditions (2) and (3) are a way to encode the local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
purely in terms of the continuous determinant associated with
\rho
. Furthermore, the existence of such a Galois representation satisfying (1) essentially follows from
[
CGH+20
, Theorem 1.1.6]
(see also
[
Sch15
,
NT16
]
) – our main contribution is parts (2) and (3). In fact,
[
CGH+20
, Theorem 1.1.6]
implies that there exists a Galois representation satisfying (1) with
J=0
, however it does not seem clear (to the authors) how to remove the nilpotent ideal when establishing properties (2) and (3).

The proof of Theorem
D
occupies all of §
9
, and follows the strategy of
[
ACC+23
, §5]
. More precisely,
\rho
is constructed by realising the Hecke eigensystem for
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}
in the boundary cohomology of the Shimura variety associated with
\operatorname{GSp}_{2n}
, and using known local-global compatibility results at
\ell=p
for standard Galois representations associated with automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GSp}_{2n}
(i.e., known local-global compatibility results for essentially self-dual cuspidal automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}
in, e.g.,
[
BLGGT14a
]
). One of the key ingredients is the recent vanishing results for the mod
p
cohomology of abelian-type Shimura varieties in
[
YZ25
]
.

Remark 1.2.5
.

The theorem proves cases of a conjecture of Hansen
[
Han17
, Conj. 1.1.2(iii)]
, which says that the Galois representation associated to (possibly non-classical) points in eigenvarieties are trianguline. In particular, it shows this for any non-Eisenstein, decomposed generic ordinary point in the
\GL_{n}
-eigenvariety. We remark that, over imaginary CM fields, cases of Hansen’s conjecture have been obtained in
[
ACC+23
]
(in the ordinary setting) and
[
McD25
]
(in the finite-slope setting), however it is not possible to deduce the cases obtained from Theorem
D
from these works.

Remark 1.2.6
.

The proof of Theorem
D
relies on the endoscopic classification for symplectic groups in
[
Art13
]
. Although
op.cit.
is still conditional on forthcoming work, there has been a tremendous amount of progress towards making this unconditional in
[
ATI+24
]
. At present, the only thing that remains is the proof of the twisted weighted fundamental lemma.

1.3.
Structure of the paper

In Part 1 (§
2
–§
6
), we prove Theorem
B
. In §
2
, we recall the extensions of Steinberg representations, and describe the interplay between smooth, continuous, and locally analytic extensions. This section also contains the crucial explicit computation of the cocycle
c_{1,\lambda}
(in §
2.3
). In §
3
we recap
p
-arithmetic cohomology and fix conventions for level subgroups and Eisenstein classes. The technical heart of Part 1 is §
4
, where we construct our evaluation maps, and use them to attach a
p
-adic measure and period to an appropriate
p
-arithmetic class. The main result of this section is an abstract ‘exceptional zero formula’ relating this measure and period (Theorem
4.7.6
). In §
5
we recall the
p
-adic
L
-functions and automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
\pi
, and prove the duality
\mathcal{L}_{\pi,2}^{\operatorname{Aut}}=\mathcal{L}_{\pi^{\vee},1}^{\operatorname{Aut}}
. We conclude Part 1 in §
6
, where we draw all of this together in our specific context to deduce Theorem
B
.

In Part 2, we prove Theorems
C
and
D
. In §
7
we first prove Theorem
C
in essentially self-dual cases via a comparison to Rosso’s work
[
Ros16
,
Ros15
]
. In §
8
, we study the
\GL(3)
-eigenvariety and Kozsul complexes of
p
-adic distributions, implementing the strategy of Gehrmann–Rosso
[
GR22
]
to prove Theorem
C
in general, up to a crucial trianguline hypothesis (Theorem
8.3.5
). In §
9
, the most important section in Part 2, we prove Theorem
D
and show that it implies Theorem
8.3.5
, thus completing the proof of Theorem
C
(and hence Theorem
A
).

. The influence of the work of Lennart Gehrmann on Part I of this paper will be evident to the reader, and we thank him for many conversations about his work. We would also like to thank Vaughan McDonald for comments on an earlier version of this article.

1.5.
Notation

Let
G=\GL_{3}
, and let
H=\GL_{2}\times\GL_{1}
, diagonally embedded in
G
. Let
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{L}
and uniformiser
\varpi\in\mathcal{O}_{L}
. We let
I_{n}
denote the
(n\times n)
identity matrix. For a reductive group
G/\mathbb{Q}
, let
G(\mathbb{Q})_{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=G(\mathbb{Q})\cap G(\mathbb{R})_{+}
, where
G(\mathbb{R})_{+}\subset G(\mathbb{R})
denotes the connected component of the identity.

Part I
Automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants

2.
Extensions of Steinberg representations

In this section, we study extensions of Steinberg representations, developing and extending
[
Geh21
, §2]
in the specific setting of
\GL_{3}
. Crucially, in Proposition

2.3.3
, we explicitly compute values of a cocycle attached to a particular extension. We also prove compatibilities between locally analytic, continuous and smooth extension classes, with coefficients in
L,\mathcal{O}_{L}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}
.

2.1.
Generalised Steinberg representations

Let
R
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra. We denote the standard upper triangular Borel subgroup of
G
by
B
. We let
P_{1}
(resp.
P_{2}
) denote the standard parabolic of
G=\operatorname{GL}_{3}
of type
(1,2)
(resp.
(2,1)
). Let
M_{1}
(resp.
M_{2}
) be its Levi subgroup.

For any parabolic
P\subset\GL_{3}
, we write
\overline{P}
for the opposite parabolic.

Definition 2.1.1
.

For
i=1,2
, let
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(R)
denote the
R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-module of smooth functions
f\colon\overline{P}_{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R
, with
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
acting by right translation. Let
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{i}(R)
denote the quotient of
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(R)
by the constant functions.

Let
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{B}}(R)
denote the
R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-module of smooth functions
f\colon\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R
. We set

\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{B}}(R)/\left(\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(R)+\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{2}}(R)\right).

We have the following:

Lemma 2.1.2
.

For
i=1,2
, the representations
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(R)
and
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)
are projective
R
-modules which satisfy
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(R)=\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R
and
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)=\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R
.

Proof.

Since
\overline{P}_{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is compact, one can easily verify that
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(R)=\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R
and
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)=\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R
. Furthermore, one has
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Z})\oplus\mathbb{Z}
as
\mathbb{Z}
-modules, so it is enough to show that
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Z})
is a projective
\mathbb{Z}
-module. But this follows from
[
Geh21
, Thm. 2.6]
.
∎

We also need continuous and locally analytic variants. For
P\in\{P_{1},P_{2},B\}
, let
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{cts}}_{\overline{P}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
(resp.
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{cts}}_{\overline{P}}(L)
, resp.
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{la}}_{\overline{P}}(L)
) denote the space of continuous functions
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\mathcal{O}_{L}
(resp. continuous functions
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to L
, resp. locally analytic functions
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to L
) which are left-invariant under the action of
\overline{P}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. These representations have a continuous action of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
by right-translation; this action is locally analytic on
\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

For
i=1,2
and
(R,\bullet)\in\{(\mathcal{O}_{L},\operatorname{cts}),(L,\operatorname{cts}),(L,\operatorname{la})\}
, let
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\bullet}(R)
denote the quotient of
\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{i}}^{\bullet}(R)
by the submodule of constant functions. We also set

\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\bullet}(R)/\left(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(R)+\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{2}}^{\bullet}(R)\right).

Remark 2.1.4
.

Note that
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{la}}_{\overline{P}}(L)
can be seen as the locally analytic vectors in the admissible Banach representation
\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)
(in the sense of
[
ST02
, §3]
and
[
Eme17
]
).

Additionally,
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)
(resp.
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)
) can also be seen as the locally analytic vectors in
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)
(resp.
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)
), since passing to locally analytic vectors is an exact functor on the category of admissible Banach representations (see
[
ST03
, Thm. 7.1]
, or more precisely
[
Pan22
, Thm. 2.2.3]
).

Notation 2.1.5
.

In what follows, when we write
(\bullet,R,\lambda)
, we mean one of the following cases:

(1)

\bullet=\operatorname{sm}
, we take
R
to be a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra, and we let
\lambda\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to R
be a smooth homomorphism (where the target is equipped with the additive group structure).

(2)

\bullet=\operatorname{cts}
, we take
R=\mathcal{O}_{L}
or
R=L
, and we let
\lambda\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to R
be a continuous homomorphism.

(3)

\bullet=\operatorname{la}
, we take
R=L
, and we let
\lambda\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to R
be a continuous homomorphism (which is automatically locally analytic).

When we write
\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)^{*}
and
\operatorname{St}_{i}^{\bullet}(R)^{*}
, we mean the
R
-linear dual in case (1), and the continuous
R
-linear dual in cases (2) and (3) (for the topologies on both the target and source).

We have the following analogue of Lemma
2.1.2
:

Lemma 2.1.6
.

Let
\ast\in\{1,2,\varnothing\}
.

(i)

For
s\geqslant 1
a positive integer, we have
\operatorname{St}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}\cong\operatorname{St}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})
.

(ii)

We have
\operatorname{St}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}L\cong\operatorname{St}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{cts}}(L)
.

Proof.

Let
P\in\{P_{1},P_{2},B\}
. If
f\colon\overline{P}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\mathcal{O}_{L}
is continuous, then its reduction modulo
\varpi^{s}
is locally constant (i.e., smooth), so

(2.1.7)

\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}\cong\operatorname{I}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\overline{P}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}).

Also any continuous
g:\overline{P}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to L
is bounded by compactness, so we have

(2.1.8)

\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}L\cong\operatorname{I}^{\mathrm{cts}}_{\overline{P}}(L).

If
\ast=i=1,2
, then
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
is defined by the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{L}\to\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{cts}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\to 0.

Tensoring with
\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}
and using (
2.1.7
) yields a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}\to\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{i}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}\to 0,

noting this remains exact since

\mathrm{Tor}_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{L}}(\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{cts}}_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{L}),\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})=\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{cts}}_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{L})[\varpi^{s}]=0.

Similarly for
\ast=\varnothing
we obtain an exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})+\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{2}}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})\to\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{B}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}\to 0.

As these sequences define
\operatorname{St}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\ast}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})
, we deduce (i). Part (ii) follows similarly after tensoring the sequences defining
\mathrm{St}_{\ast}^{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
with
L
, noting we retain exactness as
L
is
\mathcal{O}_{L}
-flat.
∎

2.2.
Extensions I

Let
(\bullet,R,\lambda)
be as in Notation
2.1.5
, so
\lambda
is an additive character of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
. We now construct extensions of Steinberg representations (and, ultimately, 1-cocycles) attached to such characters. Our chief interest will later be in the characters
\lambda=\log_{p}
and
\mathrm{ord}_{p}
.

Let
v_{1}
be the character of
M_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=\GL_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
defined by

v_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&&\\
&b&c\\
&d&e\end{smallmatrix}\right)=a^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{det}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b&c\\
d&e\end{smallmatrix}\right).

This naturally extends to a character of
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via projection to the Levi
M_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Denote by
\tau_{\lambda}
the two dimensional representation of
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
given by

\tau_{\lambda}:p\longmapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&(\lambda\circ v_{1})(p)\\
&1\end{array}\right).

We let
\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(\tau_{\lambda})
denote the
\bullet
-induction of this representation, that is

\displaystyle\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(\tau_{\lambda})=\Big\{f:G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R^{\oplus 2}:f

satisfies property
\bullet
, and

\displaystyle f(p\cdot-)=\tau_{\lambda}(p)\cdot f(-)\ \forall p\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\Big\}.

Note that:

•

the map
R\to R^{\oplus 2}
,
r\mapsto(r,0)
induces an
R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-module map
\jmath_{1}:\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(R)\to\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\tau_{\lambda})
, and

•

the map
R^{\oplus 2}\to R
,
(r_{1},r_{2})\mapsto r_{2}
induces an
R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-module map
\jmath_{2}:\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\tau_{\lambda})\to\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(R)
.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

We have a short exact sequence

(2.2.2)

0\to\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(R)\xrightarrow{\ \ \jmath_{1}\ \ }\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(\tau_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\ \ \jmath_{2}\ \ }\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(R)\to 0.

Proof.

The map
\jmath_{1}
is visibly injective, and it is clear from the definitions that the sequence is exact at
\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(\tau_{\lambda})
. It remains to show that
\jmath_{2}
is surjective.

Let
s\colon G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be a continuous, left
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant map; we will fix a specific choice, hence showing existence of such a map, in Lemma
2.3.2
. Now, given any
F\in\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(R)
, define
\widetilde{F}:G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R^{\oplus 2}
by

\widetilde{F}(g)=\begin{pmatrix}(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(g)F(g)\\
F(g)\end{pmatrix}.

For any
p\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, we have
F(pg)=F(g)
and
s(pg)=ps(g)
, so

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}(pg)=\begin{pmatrix}(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(pg)F(pg)\\
F(pg)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(g)F(g)+(\lambda\circ v_{1})(p)F(g)\\
F(g)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\tau_{\lambda}(p)\begin{pmatrix}(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(g)F(g)\\
F(g)\end{pmatrix}.

Hence
\widetilde{F}\in\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(\tau_{\lambda})
. As visibly
\jmath_{2}(\widetilde{F})=F
, we deduce surjectivity.
∎

We can pullback and pushforward the exact sequence (
2.2.2
) along the maps
R\hookrightarrow\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(R)
and
\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(R)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}_{1}(R)
to obtain an exact sequence
3
3

3

Explicitly,
\mathcal{E}_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}(R)
is the quotient

\mathcal{E}_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}(R)=\{f\in\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\tau_{\lambda}):\jmath_{2}(f)\in R\}/\jmath_{1}(R)\subset\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\tau_{\lambda})/\jmath_{1}(R).

(2.2.3)

0\to\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}_{1}(R)\to\mathcal{E}_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}(R)\to R\to 0.

We can describe this extension by a cocycle as follows. Let
s
be a choice of splitting as in the proof of Lemma
2.2.1
. Then we obtain a cocycle
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}\in Z^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}_{1}(R))
by the formula

(2.2.4)

c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[x]=\Big[g\longmapsto(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(gx)-(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(g)\Big].

Here note that
(g\mapsto\lambda v_{1}s(gx)-\lambda v_{1}s(g))\in\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(R)
, and we take its image in
\mathrm{St}^{\bullet}_{1}(R)
.

Remark 2.2.5
.

Of course, this cocycle depends on the choice of splitting
s\colon G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. However, its image in
\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}_{1}(R))
does not; it is the image of
1\in R=\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),R)
under the boundary map

\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),R)\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R))

in the long exact sequence attached to (
2.2.3
).

2.3.
Explicit computation of
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}

For our later results, we must explicitly describe a value of the cocycle
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}
defined above. We do this in Proposition
2.3.3
. For this, we now introduce an explicit section
s
(which we fix for the rest of the article, unless specified otherwise).

Notation 2.3.1
.

Let
{{}^{M_{1}}W}
denote the set of minimal length representatives for
W_{M_{1}}\backslash W_{G}
, where
M_{1}
is the Levi of
P_{1}
. Explicitly, we take:

{{}^{M_{1}}W}=\left\{\operatorname{id},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1&\\
1&&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
1&&\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

For any
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, there exist unique

\overline{p}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\qquad w\in{{}^{M_{1}}}W,\qquad\text{and}\qquad k\in\operatorname{Iw}\cap w^{-1}N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})w

such that

g=\overline{p}\cdot w\cdot k.

In particular, there is a well-defined left-
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant continuous map
s\colon G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
defined by
s(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\overline{p}
.

Although this is a standard result, we provide an explicit proof; the notation introduced will also be useful in the proof of Proposition
2.3.3
. Note that
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via the map

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b&c\\
\star&\star&\star\\
\star&\star&\star\end{smallmatrix}\right)\longmapsto[a:b:c].

Proof.

Suppose
g=\overline{p}wk=\overline{p}^{\prime}w^{\prime}k^{\prime}
are two such expressions. Then
w=w^{\prime}
by disjointness in the Bruhat decomposition; and rearranging gives

(\overline{p})^{-1}\overline{p}^{\prime}=wk(k^{\prime})^{-1}w^{-1}.

The right-hand side is in
N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, whilst the left is in
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. As the intersection of these is trivial, we deduce
\overline{p}=\overline{p}^{\prime}
and
k=k^{\prime}
. In particular, any such expression as in the lemma must be unique, and (provided we have existence) the association
g\mapsto s(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\overline{p}
is well-defined.

It remains to prove existence. It is equivalent to show
g=\overline{p}nw
, for
n\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cap w\operatorname{Iw}w^{-1}
. Let

g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b&c\\
\star&\star&\star\\
\star&\star&\star\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

We break into a series of cases, corresponding to different subsets of
\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

(a)

If
a\neq 0
, then without loss of generality scale by
a^{-1}I_{3}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
to assume
a=1
. Then:

(a-1)

If
b,c\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, then let
w=\mathrm{id}
, and

k=n=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&b&c\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{Iw}\cap N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Then

s(g)=\overline{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=gn^{-1}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),

and
g=\overline{p}n=\overline{p}k
is the desired expression.

(a-2)

If
v_{p}(b)<0
and
v_{p}(b)\leqslant v_{p}(c)
, then let
w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1&\\
1&&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

n=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&b^{-1}&b^{-1}c\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\qquad k=w^{-1}nw=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&\\
b^{-1}&1&b^{-1}c\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{Iw}\cap w^{-1}N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})w.

Then
s(g)=\overline{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=gw^{-1}n^{-1}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),
and
g=\overline{p}nw=\overline{p}wk.

(a-3)

If
v_{p}(c)<0
and
v_{p}(c)<v_{p}(b)
, then let
w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
1&&\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

n=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&c^{-1}&bc^{-1}\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\qquad k=w^{-1}nw=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&\\
&1&\\
c^{-1}&bc^{-1}&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{Iw}\cap w^{-1}N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})w.

Then
s(g)=\overline{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=gw^{-1}n^{-1}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),
and
g=\overline{p}nw=\overline{p}wk.

Note (a-1), (a-2) and (a-3) cover all possible cases for
(b,c)\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2}
.

(b)

If
a=0
and
b\neq 0
, then without loss of generality we may assume
b=1
. Then:

(b-1)

If
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, then let
w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1&\\
1&&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and

n=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&c\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\qquad k=w^{-1}nw=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&\\
&1&c\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{Iw}\cap w^{-1}N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})w.

Then
s(g)=\overline{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=gw^{-1}n^{-1}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),
and
g=\overline{p}nw=\overline{p}wk
.

(b-2)

If
v_{p}(c)<0
, then let
w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
1&&\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and

n=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&c^{-1}\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\qquad k=w^{-1}nw=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&\\
&1&\\
&c^{-1}&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{Iw}\cap w^{-1}N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})w.

Then
s(g)=\overline{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=gw^{-1}n^{-1}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),
and
g=\overline{p}nw=\overline{p}wk
.

(c)

Finally if
a=b=0
, then without loss of generality we may assume
c=1
. Then

s(g)=\overline{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=g\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
1&&\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{-1}\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),

so
w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
1&&\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
g=\overline{p}w
is the desired expression (so
k=I_{3}
).

As this covers all possible
a,b,c
, it covers all
g
, and every
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
can be written uniquely as
g=\overline{p}wk
. The function
g\mapsto s(g)=\overline{p}
is visibly continuous and left-
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant.
∎

With this choice of
s
, we can explicitly calculate the cocycle
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}
evaluated at the element
t\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{diag}(p,1,1)\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Proposition 2.3.3
.

The element
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[t]\in\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R)
can be represented by a function

F\colon\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R\qquad\in\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(R)

which has support in
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and satisfies

F(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}-2\lambda(p)&\text{ if }x,y\in p\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
-2\lambda(x)&\text{ if }x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times},y\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
-2\lambda(y)&\text{ if }x\in p\mathbb{Z}_{p},y\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times},\end{array}\right.

where we write
g=\overline{p}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x&y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Proof.

By above
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[t]\in\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R)
is represented by the function
F^{\prime}\in\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(R)
defined by

F^{\prime}(g)=(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(gt)-(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(g),\qquad g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

As
\lambda(p)\in R\subset\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{1}}^{\bullet}(R)
is constant,
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[t]
is also represented by the function

F(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=F^{\prime}(g)-\lambda(p).

We now calculate this explicitly. By Lemma
2.3.2
, any
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
has the form
g=s(g)nw
, for unique
s(g)\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
,
n=wkw^{-1}\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cap w\operatorname{Iw}w^{-1}
and
w\in{{}^{M_{1}}}W
. By left-
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-invariance, we may without loss of generality assume
s(g)=1
, so

g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x&y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)w\in(N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cap w\operatorname{Iw}w^{-1})\cdot w.

Thus
(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(g)=\lambda(1)=0
, and

(2.3.4)

F^{\prime}(g)=(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(gt)=(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(nwt).

We break the computation into cases based on
n
and
w
.

Case 1:
First suppose
w\neq\mathrm{id}
. Then
w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1&\\
1&&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
or
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
1&&\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and in either case, we have

wtw^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&\\
&p&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)=:t^{\prime}.

Let also

n^{\prime}=(t^{\prime})^{-1}nt^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&px&y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cap w\operatorname{Iw}w^{-1}.

Then we have

gt=nwt=nt^{\prime}w=t^{\prime}n^{\prime}w.

In particular,

s(gt)=t^{\prime}.

As
v_{1}(t^{\prime})=p
, we have

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(g)=\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s(gt)=\lambda\circ v_{1}\left(t^{\prime}\right)=\lambda(p).

It follows that
F(g)=F^{\prime}(g)-\lambda(p)=0
.
In particular,
F
vanishes identically on both cells

\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cdot(N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cap w\operatorname{Iw}w^{-1})\cdot w=\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cdot w\cdot\operatorname{Iw},\quad\quad w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1&\\
1&&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
1&&\\
&1&\end{smallmatrix}\right);

hence
F
is supported on the remaining cell
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Case 2:
Now suppose
w=\mathrm{id}
, so
g=n=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x&y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in N_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. We wish to compute
F^{\prime}(g)=(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s)(gt).
We have

gt=nt=t\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{-1}x&p^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),

so as
t\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
s
is left-
\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant, we have

(2.3.6)

s(gt)=t\cdot s\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{-1}x&p^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right).

In the proof of Lemma
2.3.2
, we explicitly computed
s\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&b&c\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)
for arbitrary
b,c\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We now break into subcases, depending on the valuations of
x
and
y
.

(a-1)

If
x,y\in p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, then we have

s\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{-1}x&p^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=1.

Hence
s(gt)=t
. As
v_{1}(t)=p^{-1}
, by (
2.3.4
) we see

F^{\prime}(g)=\lambda(p^{-1})=-\lambda(p),

so
F(g)=-2\lambda(p)
.

(a-2)

If
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, then as in Lemma
2.3.2
(a-2) we have

\displaystyle s\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{-1}x&p^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{-1}x&p^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1&\\
1&&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-px^{-1}&-x^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}x&&\\
1&-px^{-1}&-x^{-1}y\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),

so by (
2.3.6
),
s(gt)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&&\\
1&-px^{-1}&-x^{-1}y\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Thus

F^{\prime}(g)=\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s(gt)=\lambda(px^{-2})=\lambda(p)-2\lambda(x),

so
F(g)=-2\lambda(x)
.

(a-3)

If
x\in p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, then as in Lemma
2.3.2
(a-3) we have

\displaystyle s\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{-1}x&p^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&p^{-1}x&p^{-1}y\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1&\\
&&1\\
1&&\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-py^{-1}&-xy^{-1}\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}y&&\\
&&1\\
1&-py^{-1}&-xy^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),

so by (
2.3.6
),
s(gt)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y&&\\
&&1\\
1&-py^{-1}&-xy^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Thus

F^{\prime}(g)=\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s(gt)=\lambda(py^{-2})=\lambda(p)-2\lambda(y),

so
F(g)=-2\lambda(y)
.

This covers all cases, thus completes the proof.
∎

We introduce the following notation:

Notation 2.3.7
.

Let

\delta^{\bullet}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},R)\to\operatorname{Ext}_{R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}^{1}\left(R,\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}_{1}(R)\right)

denote the map
\delta^{\bullet}(\lambda)=\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}_{1,\lambda}
, where
\operatorname{Ext}_{R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}^{1}
denotes the
R
-module of extensions in the category of abstract
R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-modules (i.e., we are forgetting any topology that these representations may carry). One can verify, using the description of
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}_{1,\lambda}
via the cocycle
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}
, that
\delta^{\bullet}
is
R
-linear.

Lemma 2.3.8
.

Let
s\geqslant 1
be a positive integer. We have the following commutative diagram:

(2.3.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s},\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},\mathcal{O}_{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},\mathcal{O}_{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(\mathcal{O}_{L},\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{L[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(L,\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{L[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(L,\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\operatorname{sm}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pmod{\varpi^{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\operatorname{cts}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\pmod{\varpi^{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\operatorname{cts}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\operatorname{la}}}

Proof.

Note any continuous homomorphism
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathcal{O}_{L}
is smooth modulo
\varpi^{s}
, so the curved map is well-defined. The right-hand maps can be described using the identification
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}(R,M)\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),M)
and, via Lemma
2.1.6
, natural functorialities in
R
and
M
. We can check commutativity at the level of cocycles, whence it follows from the description of
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}
.
∎

2.4.
Extensions II

We now construct certain extensions of
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{2}(R)
. Firstly, we note that there is a natural
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant map

\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{P}_{1}}(R)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{2}}(R)\to\operatorname{I}^{\bullet}_{\overline{B}}(R)

given by
(f_{1},f_{2})\mapsto f_{1}\cdot f_{2}
. This induces a well-defined
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant morphism
\operatorname{pr}\colon\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)\to\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)
. We consider the following composition (denoted
\Delta^{\bullet}
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},R)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\delta^{\bullet}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}(R,\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{-\otimes\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{pr}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R))

where the second map is well-defined because
\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)
is a projective (and hence flat)
R
-module (see Lemma
2.1.2
). By the diagram (
2.3.9
), we see that we have a commutative diagram:

(2.4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},\mathcal{O}_{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},\mathcal{O}_{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathcal{O}_{L}),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{L[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{L[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}\left(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\operatorname{sm}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pmod{\varpi^{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\operatorname{cts}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\pmod{\varpi^{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\operatorname{cts}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\operatorname{la}}}

Note that the vertical maps given by tensor products are well-defined by Lemma
2.1.6
.

Remark 2.4.2
.

Since
\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R)
is a projective
R
-module, we have an identification

(2.4.3)

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R))=\operatorname{H}^{1}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R))\right).

Under this identification, for
\lambda\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},R)
the element
\Delta^{\bullet}_{1,\lambda}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Delta^{\bullet}(\lambda)
can be explicitly described by the cocycle

(2.4.4)

\Delta_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}\colon x\mapsto\left[f\mapsto\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[x]\otimes f)\right].

The following proposition describes the relation with the extensions considered in
[
Geh21
, §2]
.

Proposition 2.4.5
.

The
L
-linear morphism
\Delta^{\operatorname{la}}
factors as

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{L[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))

where the first map is the isomorphism in
[
Geh21
, Theorem 2.15]
(well-defined up to scalar) and the second map is the natural one given by “forgetting topologies”. Here
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}
denotes the group of extensions of locally analytic
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-representations as in
[
Geh21
, §2.4]
.

Proof.

Let us recall how the isomorphism in
[
Geh21
, Theorem 2.15]
is constructed. Let
\Lambda\colon\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to L
be a continuous homomorphism, and let
\sigma_{\Lambda}
denote the two-dimensional representation of
\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
over
L
given by

b\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\Lambda(b)\\
&1\end{array}\right).

Let
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{la}}_{\overline{B}}(\sigma_{\Lambda})
denote the locally analytic induction of this representation. One has an exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{la}}_{\overline{B}}(L)\to\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{la}}_{\overline{B}}(\sigma_{\Lambda})\to\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{la}}_{\overline{B}}(L)\to 0

and by pulling back along
\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{2}}(L)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)
and pushing out along
\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)
, we obtain a
L
-linear morphism
4
4

4

Note the strong analogy with Lemma
2.2.1
.

\mathcal{C}_{-}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}\left(\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{2}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)\right),\qquad\Lambda\longmapsto\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}.

Restriction from
\overline{P}_{2}
to
\overline{B}
realises
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{P}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)
. As explained in
[
Geh21
, Lemma 2.13]
, if
\Lambda\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{P}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)
lies in this subspace, then
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}=0
; and as in
[
Geh21
, Corollary 2.14, Theorem 2.15]
, one has isomorphisms

(2.4.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}

\displaystyle(\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)/\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{P}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\,\smash{\raisebox{1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,]{\ \ \mathcal{C}_{-}\ \ }\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}(\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{2}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))\xleftarrow[\,\smash{\raisebox{1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,]{\ \ \ }\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)),

where the right-hand map is the natural one arising from the map
\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{2}}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)
.

In
[
Geh21
]
, the first isomorphism is taken to be
\lambda\mapsto\Lambda^{\prime}_{\lambda}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[b\mapsto\lambda(t_{2}t_{1}^{-1})]
, where
\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
denotes the projection of
b
to the torus. We set
\Lambda_{\lambda}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[b\mapsto(\lambda\circ v_{1})(t)=\lambda(t_{2}t_{3}t_{1}^{-1})]
and note

\Lambda_{\lambda}(b)=\Lambda^{\prime}_{\lambda}(b)+\Xi_{\lambda}(b)

where
\Xi_{\lambda}(b)=\lambda(t_{3})
. Since
\Xi_{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{P}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)
, we see that
\Lambda^{\prime}_{\lambda}
and
\Lambda_{\lambda}
represent the same homomorphism in the quotient; therefore, we can (and do) take the first isomorphism to be

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)/\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{P}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Lambda_{\lambda},

and this does not change the isomorphism in
[
Geh21
, Theorem 2.15]
.

Since
\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))=0
, one sees that the natural map

(2.4.7)

\gamma\colon\operatorname{H}^{1}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))\right)\to\operatorname{H}^{1}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{2}}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))\right)

is injective. Combining with (
2.4.6
), we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)/\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\overline{P}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}(\operatorname{I}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\overline{P}_{2}},\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{an}}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}},\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{P}_{2}}^{\operatorname{sm}},\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{2},\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda_{\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
