R=\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}
. By that lemma, we have
\operatorname{Ev}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}^{\operatorname{big}}\left(\Upsilon^{\operatorname{sm}}_{p^{k}\lambda}(\mu_{s})\right)=0
, hence by linearity, we see that

(4.7.8)

p^{k}\operatorname{Ev}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}^{\operatorname{big}}\left(\Upsilon^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\operatorname{log}_{p}}(\mu_{s})\right)=p^{k}\mathcal{L}_{\mu}\cdot\operatorname{Ev}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}^{\operatorname{big}}\left(\Upsilon^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\operatorname{ord}_{p}}(\mu_{s})\right).

Then for
n\gg 1
, we see that (by Lemma
4.6.1
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ev}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}^{\operatorname{big}}\left(\Upsilon^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\operatorname{log}_{p}}(\mu_{s})\right)=\widetilde{\Xi}_{n}(\Upsilon^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\operatorname{log}_{p}}(\mu_{s})[t])=\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}\Big(\mu_{s},\ u_{0}\cdot\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\operatorname{log}_{p}}^{\operatorname{sm}}[t]\otimes\phi_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ev}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}^{\operatorname{big}}\left(\Upsilon^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\operatorname{ord}_{p}}(\mu_{s})\right)=\widetilde{\Xi}_{n}(\Upsilon^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\operatorname{ord}_{p}}(\mu_{s})[t])=\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}\Big(\mu_{s},\ u_{0}\cdot\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\operatorname{ord}_{p}}^{\operatorname{sm}}[t]\otimes\phi_{n})\Big).

Using Proposition
2.3.3
, we see that for
n\gg 0
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\operatorname{log}_{p}}^{\operatorname{sm}}[t]\otimes\phi_{n})

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}.\operatorname{log}_{p}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\operatorname{ord}_{p}}^{\operatorname{sm}}[t]\otimes\phi_{n})

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p},p\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbbm{1}}=-2\xi_{n}.

In particular, by Lemma
4.2.3
, we see that in
\operatorname{St}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s})
, we have

\displaystyle u_{0}\cdot\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\operatorname{log}_{p}}^{\operatorname{sm}}[t]\otimes\phi_{n})

\displaystyle=2\psi_{p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times},\operatorname{log}_{p}}=2\psi_{\log_{p}}.

For sufficiently large
n
, by bilinearity we thus have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ev}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}^{\operatorname{big}}\Big(\Upsilon_{\log_{p}}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mu_{s})\Big)

\displaystyle=-2\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}(\mu_{s},-\psi_{\log_{p}})\equiv-2\mathscr{L}(\mu,\log_{p})\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\varpi^{s}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ev}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{s}}^{\operatorname{big}}\Big(\Upsilon_{\operatorname{ord}_{p}}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mu_{s})\Big)

\displaystyle=-2\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}(\mu_{s},u_{0}\cdot\xi_{n})\equiv-2\mathscr{P}(\mu)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\varpi^{s}),

where the top line is (
4.7.7
), and the bottom follows from reducing Lemma
4.3.3
modulo
\varpi^{s}
. Combining with (
4.7.8
) thus gives

-2p^{k}\mathscr{L}(\mu,\operatorname{log}_{p})\equiv-2p^{k}\mathcal{L}_{\mu}\cdot\mathscr{P}(\mu)\pmod{\varpi^{s}}.

As this holds for all
s\geqslant 1
, we deduce
-2p^{k}\mathscr{L}(\mu,\log_{p})=-2p^{k}\mathcal{L}_{\mu}\cdot\mathscr{P}(\mu)
in
\mathcal{O}_{L}
, hence conclude.
∎

Remark 4.7.9
.

Attached to
\mathscr{L}(\mu)
is an analytic function
L(\mu,-):\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathcal{O}_{L}
defined by
L(\mu,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\langle x\rangle^{s}\ d\mathscr{L}(\mu,x)
, where
\langle-\rangle\colon\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to 1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is the natural map. Then
\mathscr{L}(\mu,\log_{p})=\tfrac{d}{ds}L(\mu,s)|_{s=0}
.

5.
p
-adic
L
-functions and
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for GL(3)

The theory developed so far has been for abstract
p
-arithmetic cohomology. We now introduce our specific automorphic representations
\pi
of interest, recall the
p
-adic
L
-functions of
\pi
and describe their exceptional zeros, recap the construction of the two
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
\pi
, and finally prove a duality formula for these
\mathcal{L}
-invariants.

5.1.
Automorphic representations

Let
\pi
be a unitary regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation of
G(\mathbb{A})
, which has central character
\widehat{\omega}_{\pi}
for some Dirichlet character
\omega_{\pi}
. Let
\pi=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
denote its restricted tensor product decomposition over all places of
\mathbb{Q}
.

Assumption 5.1.1
.

We assume that the weight of
\pi
is trivial and that
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg, i.e., there exists a
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant isomorphism
\pi_{p}\cong\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
(which we implicitly fix for the rest of the article). In particular, this implies that
\omega_{\pi}
is finite-order of prime-to-
p
conductor. We moreover assume
\omega_{\pi}(-1)=1
(else
L(\pi,s)
has no critical values).

Let
L(\pi,s)
denote the standard
L
-function associated with
\pi
; by our assumptions, the critical values of this
L
-function are at
s=0
and
s=1
.

Finally, let
\psi\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
denote the standard additive character, and let
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi)
denote the Whittaker model associated with
\psi
and
\pi
. This decomposes as
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi)=\bigotimes_{v}^{\prime}\mathcal{W}_{\psi_{v}}(\pi_{v})
.

5.2.
The
p
-adic
L
-functions of
\pi

We recap the results of
[
LW21
]
, which construct
p
-adic
L
-functions for
\pi
. Note that
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
is
B
-ordinary, hence both
P_{1}
-ordinary and
P_{2}
-ordinary; see
[
LW21
, Example 2.13]
. Let

\displaystyle\operatorname{Crit}^{-}_{p}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\{(\eta,0):\eta\text{ even Dirichlet character of $p$-power conductor}\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Crit}^{+}_{p}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\{(\eta,1):\eta\text{ even Dirichlet character of $p$-power conductor}\}.

Let
e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty},s)=e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty}\times\eta_{\infty},s)
and
e_{p}^{-}(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},s)
denote the (non-zero) modified
L
-factors at
\infty
and
p
for
(\eta,0)\in\operatorname{Crit}^{-}_{p}(\pi)
, as in
[
LW21
, Definitions 2.6, 2.16]
, where
\eta
is an even Dirichlet character of
p
-power conductor. For
\eta=1
, similar to Example 2.21
op. cit
. (noting
\alpha_{p}
there is
p^{-1}
) we have

e_{p}^{-}(\pi_{p},s)=(1-p^{s})\cdot(1-p^{-1-s})^{-1}=(1-p^{s})\cdot L(\pi_{p},s).

Also let
e_{\infty}^{+}(\pi_{\infty},s)=e_{\infty}^{+}(\pi_{\infty}\times\eta_{\infty},s)
and
e_{p}^{+}(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},s)
be the analogues for the right-half; for
\eta=1
one has

e_{p}^{+}(\pi_{p},s)=\varepsilon(\sigma_{p},s)^{-1}\cdot L(\sigma_{p},1-s)^{-1}\cdot L(\sigma_{p},s)=-p^{s}(1-p^{s-1})\cdot L(\pi_{p},s),

where
\sigma_{p}
is the twist of the Steinberg representation for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
by
|\cdot|^{1/2}
. Here we are using the fact that, if
\operatorname{St}_{\operatorname{GL}_{d}}(\mathbb{C})
denotes the Steinberg representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, then

(5.2.1)

\varepsilon(\operatorname{St}_{\operatorname{GL}_{d}}(\mathbb{C}),s)=(-1)^{d-1}p^{(d-1)(1/2-s)}

with respect to
\psi
and the standard choice of Haar measure (see
[
Wed08
, (3.2.3), (4.3.4)]
).

As
\pi_{p}
is self-dual, we have a ‘functional equation’
e^{+}(\pi_{p},1-s)=\gamma(\pi_{p},s)\cdot e^{-}(\pi_{p},s).
Here
\varepsilon(-)
and
\gamma(-)
are the usual
\varepsilon
- and
\gamma
-factors for
\pi_{p}
, defined with respect to the additive character
\psi
.

Theorem 5.2.2

(Loeffler–Williams)
.

There exist measures
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{\pm}(\pi)\in\mathcal{O}_{L}[\![\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}]\!]
such that:

(i)

For all
(\eta,0)\in\operatorname{Crit}^{-}_{p}(\pi)
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\eta^{-1}(x)\cdot d\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi)=e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty},0)\cdot e_{p}^{-}(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},0)\cdot\frac{L^{(p)}(\pi\times\eta,0)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{-}}.

(i)

For all
(\eta,1)\in\operatorname{Crit}^{+}_{p}(\pi)
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\eta^{-1}(x)\cdot d\mathscr{L}_{p}^{+}(\pi)=e_{\infty}^{+}(\pi_{\infty},1)\cdot e_{p}^{+}(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},1)\cdot\frac{L^{(p)}(\pi\times\eta,1)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{+}}.

Here the period
\Omega_{\pi}^{-}
is as defined in
[
LW21
, (9.1)]
, and
\Omega_{\pi}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Omega_{\pi^{\vee}}^{-}
. As an artifact of the construction,
\Omega_{\pi}^{\pm}
involves a fixed auxiliary choice
\eta_{2}
of Dirichlet character of prime-to-
p
conductor as in §
3.5
; we assume that
\eta_{2}\omega_{\pi}
is not congruent modulo
p
to any character of
p
-power conductor.

Since
\pi_{p}
is self-dual,
\pi^{\vee}
also satisfies Assumption
5.1.1
; and by
[
LW21
, §10]
, we have a
p
-adic functional equation. To explain this, let
\iota
be the involution on measures induced by precomposition with the map
x\mapsto x^{-1}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, let
N_{\pi}^{(p)}
be the prime-to-
p
part of the conductor of
\pi
, let
[N]
be the Dirac measure given by evaluation at
N
, and let
\varepsilon(\pi_{f}^{\vee,p},s)
be the finite prime-to-
p
part of the epsilon factor of
\pi^{\vee}
. Then

(5.2.3)

\mathscr{L}_{p}^{+}(\pi)=\varepsilon\big(\pi_{f}^{\vee,p},0\big)^{-1}\cdot\iota\Big([N_{\pi}^{(p)}]\cdot\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi^{\vee})\Big).

Remark 5.2.4
.

We have normalised
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{+}
slightly differently to
[
LW21
]
, omitting the
\operatorname{tw}_{1}
so as to give a uniform treatment of exceptional zeros. In particular our
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{+}
is not the one from
[
LW21
, Conj. B]
, which has an exceptional zero at the character
x\mapsto x
(rather than the trivial character).

Remark 5.2.5
.

The measure
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi)
exists for any
P_{1}
-nearly-ordinary
\pi
, whilst
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{+}(\pi)
exists for any
P_{2}
-ordinary
\pi
. In particular these two
p
-adic
L
-functions correspond exactly to the two maximal standard parabolics in
\GL_{3}
. This observation provides a conceptual connection between a
p
-adic
L
-function and its corresponding
\mathcal{L}
-invariant.

For
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, let
\langle-\rangle^{s}=\exp_{p}(s\cdot\log_{p}(\langle-\rangle))\colon\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
, where
\langle-\rangle
is projection to
1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(via the splitting given by the Teichmüller character). Define functions
L_{p}^{\pm}(\pi,-):\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to L
by

L_{p}^{-}(\pi,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\langle x\rangle^{s}\cdot d\mathscr{L}_{p}^{\pm}(\pi),\qquad L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\langle x\rangle^{s-1}\cdot d\mathscr{L}_{p}^{\pm}(\pi).

From the descriptions of
e_{p}^{\pm}
above, the following is immediate.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

We have
e_{p}^{-}(\pi_{p},0)=e_{p}^{+}(\pi_{p},1)=0
. In particular,
L_{p}^{-}(\pi,0)=L_{p}^{+}(\pi,1)=0
.

5.3.
Automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
G

We now recall Gehrmann’s automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
\pi
, one for each simple root/maximal standard parabolic of
\GL_{3}
. These are subspaces of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)
. We then prove a duality between these two
\mathcal{L}
-invariants.

5.3.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{L}
-invariants

Recall in §
2
that, for any
\lambda\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},R)
, we constructed an extension

\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}_{1,\lambda}\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R))

of the Steinberg representation for
P_{1}
, represented by a cocycle
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}\in\mathbb{Z}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R))
. Reversing the roles of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, to
\lambda
we can similarly define
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}_{2,\lambda}\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\bullet}(R))
with associated cocycle
6
6

6

As before, this cocycle depends on a choice that we will not yet specify. In Lemma
5.3.10
, we make an explicit choice of
c_{2,\lambda}^{\bullet}
. Regardless, its class in cohomology is independent of this choice, so the
\mathcal{L}
-invariants here are well-defined.

c_{2,\lambda}^{\bullet}\in Z^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\bullet}(R))
.

Definition 5.3.1
.

(i)

Let
\Delta_{i,\lambda}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\operatorname{St}_{3-i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)))
denote the cohomology class represented by the cocycle

x\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}f\mapsto\operatorname{pr}(c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[x]\otimes f)&\text{ if }i=1\\
f\mapsto\operatorname{pr}(f\otimes c_{2,\lambda}^{\bullet}[x])&\text{ if }i=2.\end{array}\right.

(ii)

Let

\Upsilon^{\operatorname{la}}_{i,\lambda}\colon\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))\to\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}_{3-i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*}))

denote the morphism
\mu\mapsto\mu\smile\Delta^{\operatorname{la}}_{i,\lambda}
using the natural map

\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*})\otimes_{L}\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}_{3-i}(L),\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L))\to\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}_{3-i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*}).

Remark 5.3.2
.

This recovers
\Delta_{1,\lambda}
from (
2.4.4
). We used
\Upsilon_{1,\lambda}^{\operatorname{la}}
in §
4.7
, where it was denoted
\Upsilon_{\lambda}^{\operatorname{la}}
.

Now let
\pi
be any automorphic representation satisfying Assumption
5.1.1
; we define
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
\pi
by considering how
\Upsilon_{i,\lambda}^{\operatorname{la}}
acts on the
\pi^{p}
-part of cohomology, as we now define.

Definition 5.3.3
.

Let
K^{p}\subset G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
be a sufficiently small compact open subgroup, and
\mathbb{T}=C_{c}(K^{p}\backslash G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})/K^{p},L)
the prime-to-
p
Hecke algebra of level
K^{p}
. Let
L
be any characteristic zero field for which the Whittaker model
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi)
has a model
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi,L)
over
L
. For any
L[G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-representation
M
, let

\operatorname{H}^{i}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M)\right)[\pi^{p}]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},L)^{K^{p}},\operatorname{H}^{i}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M)\right)\right).

Note that
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{la}}_{i,\lambda}
is prime-to-
p
Hecke equivariant, so in particular induces a map

\Upsilon_{i,\lambda}^{\operatorname{la}}[\pi]:\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))[\pi^{p}]\to\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}_{3-i}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*}))[\pi^{p}].

By Proposition
6.1.4
, we can view
\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*}))[\pi^{p}]
as a subspace of the module
\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))[\pi^{p}]
. We let
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{cts}}_{i,\lambda}[\pi]
denote the restriction of
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{la}}_{i,\lambda}[\pi]
to this submodule.

Definition 5.3.4
.

We define

\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)

to be the subspace of
\lambda\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)
such that
\Upsilon_{i,\lambda}^{\operatorname{cts}}[\pi]=0
.

Remark 5.3.5
.

The appearance of
3-i
in
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{la}}_{i,\lambda}
is an artifact of our conventions. If we identify
i
with the standard simple root
e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
(i.e.,
i=1
corresponds to
(1,-1,0)
and
i=2
corresponds to
(0,1,-1)
), then the parabolics
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
would be denoted
P_{\{2\}}
and
P_{\{1\}}
in
[
Geh21
]
. The shift
3-i
is included so that indexing in
\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\operatorname{Aut}}(\pi)
matches with that
op.cit.
.

We can recover
\mathcal{L}
-invariants as scalars via:

Proposition 5.3.6

(c.f.,
[
Geh21
, Proposition 3.14]
)
.

The subspace
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)
has codimension one (and hence is one-dimensional), and
\operatorname{ord}_{p}\not\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
.

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*})\right)[\pi^{p}]
is one-dimensional by Lemma
6.1.1
, Proposition
6.1.2
, and Proposition
6.1.4
below. Then
[
Geh21
, Proposition 3.14]
(in combination with Proposition
2.4.5
) implies that
\operatorname{ord}_{p}\not\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
, and the morphism
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{cts}}_{i,\operatorname{ord}_{p}}[\pi]
is an isomorphism. This proves the claim. Note that, although we are working with the “compactly-supported” version of
p
-arithmetic cohomology, the cited results still apply, as justified in
[
Geh21
, §3.6]
.
∎

Definition 5.3.7
.

The
automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant

\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,i}\in L
is the unique quantity such that

\operatorname{log}_{p}-\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,i}\cdot\operatorname{ord}_{p}\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi).

5.3.2.
Duality between
\mathcal{L}
-invariants

We have the following relation between automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
\pi
and
\pi^{\vee}
. Its proof will occupy all of §
5.3.2
.

Proposition 5.3.8
.

Let
\pi
be an automorphic representation satisfying Assumption
5.1.1
. Then
\pi^{\vee}
satisfies Assumption
5.1.1
and

\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{2}(\pi)=\mathbb{L}_{1}^{\operatorname{Aut}}(\pi^{\vee}).

Consider the involution
\vartheta\colon G\to G
given by
g^{\vartheta}={{}^{t}g^{-1}}
and assume, without loss of generality, that
K^{p}
is stable under
\vartheta
. This involution induces a natural involution
\vartheta\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
, where
\mathbb{T}
denotes the prime-to-
p
Hecke algebra of level
K^{p}
.

Away from
p
, we have the following (see
[
JPSS83
, §2]
).

Lemma 5.3.9
.

We have an isomorphism

\theta\colon\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},L)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{W}_{\psi^{-1}}((\pi_{f}^{\vee})^{p},L)

given by
f\mapsto[g\mapsto f(w_{0}g^{\vartheta})]
, where
w_{0}
denotes the longest Weyl element of
G
. This isomorphism is semilinear for the action of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
with respect to the involution
\vartheta
.

At
p
, one can verify analogously that we have an isomorphism

\theta\colon\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\bullet}(R)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\bullet}(R)

induced from
f\mapsto[g\mapsto f(w_{0}g^{\vartheta})]
(where
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and
f\colon\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R
). This
R
-linear isomorphism is semilinear for the action of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via the involution
\vartheta
.

Lemma 5.3.10
.

Let
c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}\in Z^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}_{1}(R))
be the cocycle representing
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}_{1,\lambda}
, and let
c_{2,\lambda}^{\bullet}\in Z^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\bullet}(R))
be the cocycle

c_{2,\lambda}^{\bullet}[x]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\theta(c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[x^{\vartheta}]),\quad\quad x\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Then
c_{2,\lambda}^{\bullet}
represents the extension
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}_{2,\lambda}
.

Proof.

This is a direct computation involving the explicit description of the cocycles as

c^{\bullet}_{i,\lambda}[x](g)=(\lambda\circ v_{i}\circ s_{i})(gx)-(\lambda\circ v_{i}\circ s_{i})(g)

where
v_{2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b&\\
c&d&\\
&&e\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\operatorname{det}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{-1}e
, and
s_{i}\colon G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\overline{P}_{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a continuous, left
\overline{P}_{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant map. More precisely, once we have fixed a choice of
s_{1}
, we can take
s_{2}(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=w_{0}s_{1}(w_{0}g^{\vartheta})^{\vartheta}w_{0}^{-1}
. Note that
v_{2}(w_{0}p^{\vartheta}w_{0}^{-1})=v_{1}(p)
for
p\in\overline{P}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, hence we have
(v_{2}\circ s_{2})(g)=(v_{1}\circ s_{1})(w_{0}g^{\vartheta})
. Then we compute:

c_{1,\lambda}^{\bullet}[x^{\vartheta}](w_{0}g^{\vartheta})=(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s_{1})(w_{0}(gx)^{\vartheta})-(\lambda\circ v_{1}\circ s_{1})(w_{0}g^{\vartheta})=(\lambda\circ v_{2}\circ s_{2})(gx)-(\lambda\circ v_{2}\circ s_{2})(g)

and the lemma follows.
∎

We now give the analogous relationship between the classes
\Delta_{i,\lambda}^{\bullet}
used in the definition of
\mathcal{L}
-invariants. Similarly to above, we have a natural
R
-linear isomorphism

\theta\colon\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)

induced from
f\mapsto[g\mapsto f(w_{0}g^{\vartheta})]
which is semilinear for the action of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via
\vartheta
. Similarly, we have a
\vartheta
-semilinear isomorphism

\Theta\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R))

given by
\Theta(-)=\theta\circ(-)\circ\theta
. It is simple to check that
\Delta^{\bullet}_{1,\lambda}
is mapped to
\Delta^{\bullet}_{2,\lambda}
under the natural map

\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)))\to\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{sm}}(R),\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(R)))

given by
c\mapsto[x\mapsto\Theta(c[x^{\vartheta}])]
.

Recall an element
\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M)
is described as

\xi[d]\colon\pi_{0}(G(\mathbb{R}))\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})/K^{p}\to M

where
d\in D_{G}
. For
M=\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}
or
M_{i}=\operatorname{St}_{i}(L)^{*}
, we consider the following isomorphism:

\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M_{(2)})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M_{(1)})

given by

\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(\xi)[d]([g_{\infty}],g^{p})=\theta^{*}(\xi[d^{\vartheta}]([g^{\vartheta}_{\infty}],(g^{p})^{\vartheta}))

which is semilinear for the action of
G(\mathbb{Q})
via
\vartheta
. Here the notation
M_{(i)}
means we either omit or include the subscript in both the source and target. Note that
\vartheta
indeed induces a natural semilinear involution on
D_{G}
because
\vartheta
sends proper maximal parabolics to proper maximal parabolics (recall the definition of
D_{G}
in
[
Geh21
, §3.6]
for example).

We have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{H}^{1}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon^{\operatorname{la}}_{1,\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon^{\operatorname{la}}_{2,\lambda}}

where the vertical maps are induced from
\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}
(the right-hand vertical map sends a cocycle
c
to
x\mapsto\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(c[x^{\vartheta}])
). The vertical maps are isomorphisms and
\vartheta
-semilinear for the action of
\mathbb{T}
.

We are now in a position to prove the main result of this subsection.

Proof of Proposition
5.3.8
.

From the previous section, it suffices to show that

\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))

induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))[(\pi^{\vee})^{p}]\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))[\pi^{p}],

and similarly for the continuous version and the right-hand vertical map in the above commutative diagram. But the isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi^{-1}}((\pi^{\vee}_{f})^{p},L),\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},L),\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))

is given by
(-)\mapsto\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}\circ(-)\circ\theta
. ∎

6.
The automorphic exceptional zero formula

Let
\pi
be an automorphic representation satisfying Assumption
5.1.1
. We now prove our first main result: the automorphic exceptional zero formula for
\pi
.

6.1.
Cohomology classes for
\pi

We now attach
p
-arithmetic cohomology classes to cusp forms in the Whittaker model for
\pi
. Let
K^{p}
be any sufficiently small prime-to-
p
level.

Lemma 6.1.1
.

Let
K_{\infty}^{\circ}\subset G(\mathbb{R})
denote the connected component of the identity of the maximal compact-mod-centre subgroup of
G(\mathbb{R})
. We have an isomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty}^{\circ};\pi_{\infty})\otimes\pi_{p}^{\operatorname{Iw}}\cong\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\mathbb{C})\right)[\pi^{p}],

and both sides are one-dimensional over
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Proposition
3.2.6
yields a Hecke-equivariant isomorphism between the
\mathrm{H}^{0}(-)
in the right-hand side and
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{G,K^{p}\operatorname{Iw}},\mathbb{C})
. By e.g.
[
Clo90
]
, we have

\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{G,K^{p}\operatorname{Iw}},\mathbb{C})[\pi^{p}]\cong\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{cusp}}^{2}(X_{G,K^{p}\operatorname{Iw}},\mathbb{C})[\pi^{p}].

Moreover considering the standard description of cuspidal cohomology, and by strong multiplicity one, we have

\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{cusp}}^{2}(X_{G,K^{p}\operatorname{Iw}},\mathbb{C})[\pi^{p}]\cong\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty}^{\circ};\pi_{\infty})\otimes\pi_{p}^{\operatorname{Iw}}\otimes(\pi_{f}^{p})^{K^{p}}[\pi^{p}].

Now
(\pi_{f}^{p})^{K^{p}}[\pi^{p}]
is a line, generated by the inverse of any intertwining into the Whittaker model. Choosing one, and chaining together the isomorphisms above, yields the claimed isomorphism.

The final statement follows since both constituents of the tensor product are 1-dimensional.
∎

Now:

•

let
\zeta_{\infty}\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty}^{\circ};\pi_{\infty})
denote a fixed generator; and

•

let
\varphi_{p}\in\pi_{p}
denote the vector which is a scalar multiple of the indicator function
\operatorname{ch}(\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\operatorname{Iw})\in\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
under the isomorphism in Assumption
5.1.1
, and satisfies
W_{p}(1)=W_{\varphi_{p}}(1)=1
.

Under the isomorphism in Lemma
6.1.1
, this yields an element
\phi\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\mathbb{C}))[\pi^{p}]
, that is, a Hecke equivariant map

\phi\colon\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi^{p}_{f})^{K^{p}}\to\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\mathbb{C})\right).

Let
E
be the Hecke field of
\pi
, which is a number field. We then have an
E
-rational structure
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f},E)\subset\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f})
, and we let
\Theta_{\pi}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the Whittaker period as in
[
LW21
, §3.3.1]
; this is well-defined up to
E^{\times}
, and

\Theta_{\pi}^{-1}\cdot\phi\Big(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},E)^{K^{p}}\Big)\subset\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}E)).

If
L
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
containing
E
, then we can naturally consider the image of
\Theta_{\pi}^{-1}\cdot\phi
(on the
E
-rational Whittaker functions) with
L
-coefficients.

Recall the map
\rho_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{0}
from §
3.3
. As
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg, we have the following key result:

Proposition 6.1.2
.

Post-composition with
\rho_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{0}
induces an isomorphism

(6.1.3)

\rho_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{0}:\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*})\right)[\pi^{p}]\to\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}L)\right)[\pi^{p}].

Proof.

This is
[
Geh21
, Proposition 3.6(b)]
, using that
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg.
∎

Proposition 6.1.4
.

The natural maps
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*}\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*}
induce morphisms

(6.1.5)

\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*})\right)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*})\right),

and the composition is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This follows from (the compactly-supported version of)
[
Geh21
, Proposition 3.11]
.
∎

Combining these propositions with the discussion above, for
\bullet\in\{\operatorname{sm},\operatorname{cts}\}
we have

\dim_{L}\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(L)^{*})\right)[\pi^{p}]=1,

and we may lift
\Theta_{\pi}^{-1}\cdot\phi
uniquely to a Hecke-equivariant function

\phi^{\bullet}:\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},E)^{K^{p}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{0}\left(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\bullet}(L)^{*})\right).

(This depends on
\Theta_{\pi}
, but we suppress this). We let
\phi^{\operatorname{la}}
denote the composition of
\phi^{\operatorname{cts}}
with the first map in (
6.1.5
).

6.2.
Local zeta integrals and test data

As input to the map
\phi^{\operatorname{cts}}
, we take test data for the following local zeta integrals.

Definition 6.2.1
.

For any Schwartz function
\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{2})
, smooth character
\chi\colon\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and
s\in\mathbb{C}
, we let
W_{\Phi_{v}}(-;\chi,s)
denote the (
\GL_{2}
) Whittaker function as in
[
LW21
, §8.1]
. For
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
,
W\in\mathcal{W}_{\psi_{v}}(\pi_{v})
,
\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{2})
and smooth characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, we define

Z(W,\Phi_{v};\chi_{1},\chi_{2},s_{1},s_{2})=\int_{\left(N_{2}\backslash\operatorname{GL}_{2}\right)(\mathbb{Q}_{v})}W(\iota(g,1))W_{\Phi_{v}}(g;\chi_{2},s_{2})\chi_{1}(\operatorname{det}g)|\operatorname{det}g|^{s_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}dg.

This factorises as

Z(W,\Phi_{v};\chi_{1},\chi_{2},s_{1},s_{2})=\widetilde{Z}(W,\Phi_{v};\chi_{1},\chi_{2},s_{1},s_{2})\cdot L(\pi\times\chi_{1},s_{1}+s_{2}-1/2)L(\pi\times\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1},s_{1}-s_{2}+1/2)

where
\widetilde{Z}(W,\Phi_{v};\chi_{1},\chi_{2},s_{1},s_{2})
is entire in both complex variables (see
[
LW21
, Theorem 8.2]
).

Test data 6.2.2
.

We fix the following local test data:

•

If
v\nmid p\infty
, by
[
JPSS83
]
there exists a finite set
I_{v}
and sets
(W_{v,i})_{i\in I_{v}}\subset\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{v},E)
and
(\Phi_{v,i})_{i\in I_{v}}\subset{}_{c}\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{2},\mathbb{Z})
such that

\sum_{i}\widetilde{Z}(W_{v,i},\Phi_{v};1,\omega_{\pi,v}\eta_{2,v},s_{1},s_{2})=1.

•

If
v\nmid p\infty
and
\pi_{v}
and
\eta_{2,v}
are unramified, we may take
I_{v}=\{1\}
a singleton, and take
W_{v,1}
and
\Phi_{v,1}
to be the normalised spherical data.

•

If
v=\infty
, we let
\Phi_{\infty}(x,y)=2^{-1}(x+iy)^{2}\operatorname{exp}(-\pi(x^{2}+y^{2}))\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

•

If
v=p
, we let
\varphi_{p}\in\pi_{p}
denote the vector which is a scalar multiple of the indicator function
\operatorname{ch}(\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\operatorname{Iw})\in\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
under the isomorphism in Assumption
5.1.1
, and satisfies
W_{p}(1)=W_{\varphi_{p}}(1)=1
. We let
\Phi_{p}=\operatorname{ch}(p^{m}\mathbb{Z}_{p},1+p^{m}\mathbb{Z}_{p})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2})
for an integer
m\geqslant 1
(to be specified later).

We suppose
K^{p}
fixes all of this test data. Let
I=\prod I_{v}
, a finite set (as all but finitely many
I_{v}
are singletons). For each
i=(i_{v})\in I
we then obtain an element
W^{p}_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\otimes_{v\nmid p\infty}W_{v,i_{v}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},E)^{K^{p}}
and a prime-to-
p
Schwartz function
\Phi^{(p)}_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\otimes_{v}\Phi_{v,i_{v}}
.

For each
i\in I
, let
\mu^{\operatorname{cts}}_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\phi^{\operatorname{cts}}(W^{p}_{i})\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*}))
. Let
\mu_{i}
be its image under the natural map
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*}\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)^{*}
, and likewise
\mu_{i}^{\operatorname{la}}
under
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*}\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}
. Note in all cases
\mu_{i}^{\bullet}=\phi^{\bullet}(W_{i}^{p})
.

Similarly to
[
LW21
, §3.3.3]
, we may rescale
\Theta_{\pi}
to ensure that

\mu^{\operatorname{cts}}_{i}\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathcal{O}_{L})^{*})),

hence also
\mu_{i}
, is integral. Then for each
i\in I
, we have a measure
\mathscr{L}(\mu_{i},-,\Phi^{(p)}_{i})
and a period
\mathscr{P}(\mu_{i},\Phi^{(p)}_{i})
as constructed in §
4.2
and §
4.3
respectively. Note we now emphasise the dependence on the prime-to-
p
Schwartz function, which we dropped from previous notation.

We combine this data into a single measure and and single period attached to
\pi
:

Definition 6.2.3
.

•

Let
\mathscr{L}(\pi,-)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{i\in I}\mathscr{L}(\mu_{i},-,\Phi_{i}^{(p)})\in\mathcal{O}_{L}[\![\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}]\!]
.

•

Let
\mathscr{P}(\pi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{i\in I}\mathscr{P}(\mu_{i},\Phi_{i}^{(p)})\in\mathcal{O}_{L}
.

6.3.
Abstract vs. automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants

The following relates the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for
\pi
to the abstract
\mathcal{L}
-invariants used in §
4.7
. We maintain the notation
\mu_{i}^{\bullet}
for
i\in I
from §
6.2
.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

For each
i\in I
, we have
[\Upsilon^{\operatorname{la}}_{\log_{p}-\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}\operatorname{ord}_{p}}(\mu_{i}^{\operatorname{la}})]=0
.

Proof.

For the first, note by Definitions
5.3.4
and
5.3.7
that
\Upsilon_{\log_{p}-\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}\operatorname{ord}_{p}}^{\operatorname{la}}[\pi]
is zero on the generator
\phi^{\operatorname{cts}}\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{cts}}(L)^{*}))[\pi^{p}]
, hence zero on
\phi^{\operatorname{la}}
. As this map is induced by post-composition with
\Upsilon_{\log_{p}-\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}\operatorname{ord}_{p}}^{\operatorname{la}}
, it follows that

\Big[\Upsilon_{\log_{p}-\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}\operatorname{ord}_{p}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\phi^{\operatorname{la}}(W^{p}))\Big]=0\qquad\forall W^{p}\in\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},E)^{K^{p}}.

The result follows since
\mu_{i}^{\operatorname{la}}=\phi^{\operatorname{la}}(W_{i}^{p})
.
∎

We immediately deduce the following exceptional zero formula:

Proposition 6.3.2
.

We have

\mathscr{L}(\pi,\log_{p})=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}\cdot\mathscr{P}(\pi).

Proof.

The lemma allows us to apply Theorem
4.7.6
to
\mu_{i}^{\operatorname{cts}}
with
\mathcal{L}_{\mu_{i}}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}
, which yields

\mathscr{L}(\mu_{i},\log_{p},\Phi^{(p)}_{i})=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}\cdot\mathscr{P}(\mu_{i},\Phi^{(p)}_{i}).

Taking the sum over
i
yields the claimed expression.
∎

6.4.
Comparison of measures

We now directly compare the measure
\mathscr{L}(\pi)
to the
p
-adic
L
-function
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi)
from
[
LW21
]
(as described in Theorem
5.2.2
). Let
dh
denote the Tamagawa measure for
H
. We have the following comparison.

Proposition 6.4.1
.

There exists
C\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\mathscr{L}(\pi,-)=p^{C}\cdot\Big(c^{2}-(\eta_{2}\omega_{\pi})(c)^{-1}[c^{-1}]\Big)\cdot\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H};dh)^{-1}\cdot\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi,-).

Proof.

Let
i\in I
. Combining Propositions
6.1.2
and
3.2.6
(ii), attached to
\mu_{i}
is a class
\phi_{i}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{G,K^{p}\operatorname{Iw}},L)
. There exists
7
7

7

The factor of
p^{C}
accounts for any difference between the integral structures chosen in the present paper and in
[
LW21
]
, which we do not attempt (or need) to carefully compare.

C\in\mathbb{Z}
such that each
p^{C}\phi_{i}
has coefficients in
\mathcal{O}_{L}
. Then
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi)
is constructed using the tame test data
\{p^{C}\phi_{i},\Phi^{(p)}_{i}\}_{i}
, as explained in §9
op. cit
. The construction there also uses the same choices at infinity and
p
, so to compare the measures
\mathscr{L}(\pi)
and
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi)
, it suffices to compare the methods of construction. The compatibility of the two constructions was described in the proof of Proposition
4.2.7
.

To explain the additional terms, note when constructing
\mathscr{L}(\pi)
we have not included the volume term from
[
LW21
, Prop. 6.11]
, hence the appearance of
\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H};dh)
here. Finally, the construction of
\mathscr{L}(\pi,-)
uses
c
-smoothed Eisenstein series, differing from the non-
c
-smoothed version by the stated factor in
c
, as explained in
[
LW21
, §6.3]
.
∎

For
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, let
\mathscr{L}(\pi,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\langle x\rangle^{s}\cdot d\mathscr{L}(\pi)
. The decomposition in Proposition
6.4.1
can be evaluated multiplicatively on any character, hence on
\langle x\rangle^{s}
, so

\mathscr{L}(\pi,s)=p^{C}\cdot\left(c^{2}-(\eta_{2}\omega_{\pi})(c)^{-1}c^{-s}\right)\cdot\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H};dh)^{-1}\cdot L_{p}^{-}(\pi,s).

Corollary 6.4.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\pi,\log_{p})

\displaystyle=\left.\tfrac{d}{ds}\mathscr{L}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=p^{C}\cdot\Big(c^{2}-(\eta_{2}\omega_{\pi})(c)^{-1})\Big)\cdot\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H};dh)^{-1}\cdot\left.\tfrac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{-}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=0}.

Proof.

As
\langle x\rangle^{s}=\operatorname{exp}_{p}(s\cdot\log_{p}(\langle x\rangle))
, we see the first derivative at
s=0
is computed by integrating
\log_{p}(x)=\log_{p}(\langle x\rangle)
. The second equality is then the chain rule, recalling that
L_{p}(\pi,0)=0
.
∎

6.5.
Computation of the period

The goal of this section is to prove the following:

Proposition 6.5.1
.

We have

\mathscr{P}(\pi)=p^{C}(c^{2}-(\eta_{2}\omega_{\pi})(c)^{-1})\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H};dh)^{-1}\cdot e_{\infty}(\pi,0)\cdot\frac{L(\pi,0)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{-}}.

We will prove this by first relating the summands
\mathscr{P}(\mu_{i},\Phi^{(p)}_{i})
to a Rankin–Selberg automorphic period, and then computing this period via local zeta integrals. From now on, we let
m\geqslant 1
be an arbitrary integer, and take
\Phi_{p}=\Phi_{p}^{m}
.

Let
\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}
and
\{\delta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\delta_{5}^{\prime}\}
denote bases of
\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{2}/\mathfrak{K}_{2,\infty}^{\circ}\right)^{\vee}
and
\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{3}/\mathfrak{K}_{3,\infty}^{\circ}\right)^{\vee}
respectively, satisfying the compatibility as in
[
LW21
, §7.2.3]
. By the presentation of Lie algebra cohomology using the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex, we can view

\operatorname{H}^{2}\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{3},K_{3,\infty}^{\circ};\pi_{\infty}\right)\subset\bigwedge{\!}^{2}\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{3}/\mathfrak{K}_{3,\infty}^{\circ}\right)^{\vee}\otimes\pi_{\infty}.

Write

\zeta_{\infty}=\sum_{1\leqslant r<s\leqslant 5}[\delta^{\prime}_{r}\wedge\delta^{\prime}_{s}]\otimes\varphi_{\infty,r,s}

where
\zeta_{\infty}
is the fixed generator of this one-dimensional cohomology group, and set
\varphi_{\infty}=\varphi_{\infty,1,3}-\varphi_{\infty,2,3}\in\pi_{\infty}
. Recall
\phi_{p}\in\pi_{p}^{\operatorname{Iw}}
from §
6.1
, and let
\varphi^{p}_{i}
be the pullback of
W^{p}_{i}
under our fixed Whittaker intertwining. We have the following:

Lemma 6.5.2
.

One has

\mathscr{P}(\mu_{i},\Phi^{(p)}_{i})=p^{C}\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H}U_{1}(p^{m});dh)^{-1}\Theta_{\pi}^{-1}\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\varphi_{i}(\iota(h)u_{0})\cdot{{}_{c}\mathcal{E}}^{2}_{\Phi_{i}^{(p)}\Phi_{p}}(\operatorname{pr}_{1}(h))\cdot\widehat{\eta}_{2}(\nu_{2}(h))dh

where
\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{\infty}\otimes\varphi_{p}\otimes\varphi_{p}^{i}
and
\nu_{2}:H\to\GL_{1}
is projection to the second factor.

Proof.

Again we compare to
[
LW21
]
. As in the proof of Proposition
6.4.1
, attached to
\mu_{i}
is a class
p^{C}\phi_{i}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{G,K^{p}\operatorname{Iw}},\mathcal{O}_{L})
. In the definition of
\mathscr{P}(\mu_{i},\Phi^{(p)}_{i})
, note that
\Psi_{\operatorname{Iw},m}(\mu_{i})
is simply the restriction of
u_{0}\cdot p^{C}\phi_{i}
from
G
to
H
. Then
\mathscr{P}(\mu_{\varphi_{f}})
is the pairing of this restriction with an Eisenstein class, namely, the pullback version of the pairing in
[
LW21
, §7.2]
. The result now follows from exactly the same reasoning as §7.2-7.3
op. cit
.
∎

We now relate this to a Rankin–Selberg period, which in turn can be related to a product of local zeta integrals. For ease of notation set
C^{\prime}=c^{2}-(\eta_{2}\omega_{\pi})(c^{-1})
.

Lemma 6.5.3
.

One has

\displaystyle\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}}

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(\iota(h)u_{0})\cdot{{}_{c}\mathcal{E}}^{2}_{\Phi_{i}^{(p)}\Phi_{p}}(\operatorname{pr}_{1}(h))\cdot\widehat{\eta}_{2}(\nu_{2}(h))dh

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}\cdot Z(u_{0}\cdot W_{p},\Phi_{p};1,\omega_{\pi,p}\eta_{2,p},1/2,0)\cdot\prod_{v\neq p}Z(W_{v,i_{v}},\Phi_{v,i_{v}};1,\omega_{\pi,v}\eta_{2,v},1/2,0).

Proof.

This follows from
[
LW21
, §7.3]

mutatis mutandis
. The factor
C^{\prime}
arises from comparing the
c
-smoothed Eisenstein series with the non-
c
-smoothed version.
∎

Corollary 6.5.4
.

After possibly rescaling
\zeta_{\infty}
, one has

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}(\pi)=C^{\prime}p^{C}\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H}U_{1}(p^{m});dh)^{-1}

\displaystyle\cdot Z(u_{0}\cdot W_{p},\Phi_{p};1,\omega_{\pi,p}\eta_{2,p},1/2,0)

\displaystyle\times e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi,0)\cdot\frac{L^{(p)}(\pi,0)\cdot L^{(p)}(\pi\times(\omega_{\pi}\eta_{2})^{-1},1)}{\Theta_{\pi}}.

Proof.

Summing over
i\in I
, we find from Assumption
6.2.2
that all of the finite prime-to-
p
zeta integrals reduce to the claimed
L
-values. The factor at infinity is computed in
[
LW21
, §9.5]
.
∎

From now on, we assume that
\zeta_{\infty}
is normalised so that the conclusion of Corollary
6.5.4
holds. We now compute the local zeta integral at
p
.

Lemma 6.5.5
.

Set
\chi_{2}=\omega_{\pi,p}\eta_{2,p}
. With notation as in Lemma
6.5.3
, one has

Z(u_{0}\cdot W_{p},\Phi_{p};1,\omega_{\pi,p}\eta_{2,p},1/2,0)=\frac{1}{p^{2m}(1-p^{-2})}L(\pi_{p},0)\frac{\mathcal{E}_{0}\cdot L(\pi_{p}\times\chi_{2}^{-1},1)}{\varepsilon(\pi_{p}\times\chi_{2}^{-1},1)}

for
m\geqslant 1
sufficiently large, where
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\frac{L(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{2,p},0)}{L(\pi_{p}^{\vee}\times\chi_{2},0)}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is a
p
-adic unit.

Proof.

We follow (and freely use the notation from)
[
LW21
, Theorem 8.7]
. We first note that

Z(u_{0}\cdot W_{p},\Phi_{p};1,\chi_{2},1/2,0)=\gamma(\pi_{p}\times\chi_{2}^{-1},1)^{-1}\cdot Y(u_{0}\cdot W_{p},\Phi_{p};1,\chi_{2},1/2,0)

by
[
LW21
, Theorem 8.4]
. For
m\geqslant 1
sufficiently large, we have

Y(u_{0}\cdot W_{p},\Phi_{p};1,\chi_{2},1/2,0)=\frac{1}{p^{2m}(1-p^{-2})}y(u_{0}\cdot W_{p};1,\chi_{2};1/2,0)(1)

so it suffices to compute

y(u_{0}\cdot W_{p};1,\chi_{2};s_{1},s_{2})(1)=\int_{(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times})^{2}}W_{p}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&&\\
&1&\\
&&y^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)|x|^{s_{1}+s_{2}-3/2}|y|^{s_{2}-s_{1}-1/2}\chi_{2}(y)d^{\times}xd^{\times}y

where
W_{p}
is the Iwahori fixed vector satisfying
W_{p}(1)=1
. By
[
LW21
, Proposition 2.25]
, we have

\displaystyle y(u_{0}\cdot W_{p};1,\chi_{2};s_{1},s_{2})(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n\geqslant 0}\omega_{\pi,p}(p^{-n})W_{p}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{m+n}&&\\
&p^{n}&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)p^{-m(s_{1}+s_{2}-3/2)}p^{-n(s_{2}-s_{1}-1/2)}\chi_{2}(p^{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n\geqslant 0}\eta_{2,p}(p^{n})W_{p}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{m+n}&&\\
&p^{n}&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)p^{-m(s_{1}+s_{2}-3/2)}p^{-n(s_{2}-s_{1}-1/2)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n\geqslant 0}\eta_{2,p}(p^{n})W_{\sigma}^{\operatorname{new}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)p^{-m(s_{1}+s_{2}+1/2)}p^{-n(s_{2}-s_{1}+1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{m\geqslant 0}p^{-m(s_{1}+s_{2}+1/2)}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\eta_{2,p}(p^{n})W_{\sigma}^{\operatorname{new}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{n}&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)p^{-n(s_{2}-s_{1}+1)}\right)

Here
W_{\sigma}^{\operatorname{new}}
is the normalised new vector for
\sigma=\operatorname{St}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})\otimes|\cdot|^{-1/2}
, where
\operatorname{St}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
denotes the Steinberg representation for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Using the standard formula for local
L
-factors in terms of Whittaker new vectors
[
Gel75
, Prop. 6.17]
, we have

\displaystyle y(u_{0}\cdot W_{p};1,\chi_{2};s_{1},s_{2})(1)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{p}(s_{1}+s_{2}+1/2)\cdot L(\operatorname{St}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{GL}_{2}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\eta_{2,p},s_{2}-s_{1}+1)

\displaystyle=L(|\cdot|_{p},s_{1}+s_{2}-1/2)\cdot L(|\cdot|_{p}\otimes\eta_{2,p},s_{2}-s_{1}+1/2)

\displaystyle=L(\pi_{p},s_{1}+s_{2}-1/2)\cdot L(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{2,p},s_{2}-s_{1}+1/2).

We now use the fact that

\gamma(\pi_{p}\times\chi_{2}^{-1},1)^{-1}=\frac{L(\pi_{p}\times\chi_{2}^{-1},1)}{\varepsilon(\pi_{p}\times\chi_{2}^{-1},1)L(\pi_{p}^{\vee}\times\chi_{2},0)}.

The last part follows from
[
LW21
, Lemma 8.8]
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.5.1
.

Combining Lemmas
6.5.2
,
6.5.3
, and
6.5.5
, and using the definition of
\Omega_{\pi}^{-}
from
[
LW21
, (9.1)]
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}(\pi)=p^{C}C^{\prime}\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H}U_{1}(p^{m});dh)^{-1}\frac{1}{p^{2m}(1-p^{-2})}\cdot e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi,0)\cdot\frac{L(\pi,0)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{-}}.

Now we note that the volume of
U_{1}(p^{m})
satisfies

\operatorname{vol}(U_{1}(p^{m});dh)^{-1}=[\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):\left(\begin{smallmatrix}*&*\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\pmod{p^{m}}]=p^{m}(1-p^{-1})\cdot p^{m}(1+p^{-1})=p^{2m}(1-p^{-2}).

∎

6.6.
The exceptional zero formulas

The following is the first main theorem of this article. It is the direct analogue of Conjecture
1.1.2
using the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant.

Theorem 6.6.1

(The left-half exceptional zero formula)
.

One has

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{-}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=0}=e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty},0)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}\cdot\frac{L(\pi,0)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{-}}.

Proof.

Choose
c
such that
c^{2}-(\omega_{\pi}\eta_{2})(c^{-1})\neq 0
(which is always possible because we are assuming that
\omega_{\pi}\eta_{2}
is not congruent modulo
p
to any character of
p
-power conductor). The result now follows by combining Proposition
6.3.2
with Corollary
6.4.2
and Proposition
6.5.1
, which yields the claimed formula multiplied on both sides by the (non-zero) factor
p^{C}\cdot(c^{2}-(\omega_{\pi}\eta_{2})(c^{-1}))\cdot\operatorname{vol}(K^{p}_{H};\operatorname{dh})^{-1}
. Dividing through gives the result.
∎

We also have an analogue for the other
p
-adic
L
-function for
\pi
.

Theorem 6.6.2

(The right-half exceptional zero formula)
.

One has

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=1}=-p\cdot e_{\infty}^{+}(\pi_{\infty},1)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,2}\cdot\frac{L(\pi,1)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{+}}.

Proof.

From the
p
-adic functional equation (
5.2.3
), we have

L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)=\varepsilon\big(\pi_{f}^{\vee,p},0\big)^{-1}\cdot\langle N_{\pi}^{(p)}\rangle^{1-s}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\langle x\rangle^{1-s}\cdot d\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi^{\vee}).

Differentiating and evaluating at
s=1
, the chain rule gives two terms; but one of them contains
\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\mathbbm{1}\cdot d\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi)
, which vanishes (via the exceptional zero). We are left with

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=1}=\varepsilon\big(\pi_{f}^{\vee,p},0\big)^{-1}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}-\log_{p}(x)\cdot d\mathscr{L}_{p}^{-}(\pi^{\vee}).

Bringing out the
-1
and applying the left-half exceptional zero formula to
\pi^{\vee}
, we obtain

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=1}=-\varepsilon(\pi_{f}^{\vee,p},0)^{-1}\cdot e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty}^{\vee},0)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi^{\vee},1}\cdot\frac{L(\pi^{\vee},0)}{\Omega_{\pi^{\vee}}^{-}}.

We have functional equations
e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty}^{\vee},0)L(\pi^{\vee},0)=\varepsilon(\pi_{f}^{\vee},0)\cdot e_{\infty}^{+}(\pi_{\infty},1)L(\pi,1)
(from
[
Coa89
, (6)]
),
\Omega_{\pi^{\vee}}^{-}=\Omega_{\pi}^{+}
(by definition), and
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{1,\pi^{\vee}}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,2}
(Proposition
5.3.8
). Combining all of this yields

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=1}=-\varepsilon(\pi_{p}^{\vee},0)\cdot e_{\infty}^{+}(\pi_{\infty},1)\cdot\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,2}\cdot\frac{L(\pi,1)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{+}}.

We conclude as
\varepsilon(\pi_{p}^{\vee},0)=\varepsilon(\operatorname{St}_{\GL_{3}}(\mathbb{C}),0)=p
by (
5.2.1
).
∎

6.7.
Relation with completed cohomology

We note that we have the following relation between
\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\operatorname{Aut}}(\pi)
and completed cohomology of the locally symmetric space for
G
. Let
X_{G,K^{p}K_{p}}
denote the locally symmetric space for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{3}
of level
K^{p}K_{p}
. We define the compactly supported completed cohomology to be:

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{*}_{c}\left(X_{G,K^{p}},L\right)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\varprojlim_{n}\varinjlim_{K_{p}}\operatorname{H}^{*}_{c}\left(X_{G,K^{p}K_{p}},\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varpi^{n}\right)\right)[1/\varpi]

which carries commuting actions of
\mathbb{T}
and
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Let

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{2}_{c}\left(X_{G,K^{p}},L\right)[\pi^{p}]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\pi_{f}^{p},L)^{K^{p}},\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{2}_{c}\left(X_{G,K^{p}},L\right)\right)

denote the prime-to-
p
isotypic piece. Recall from Proposition
2.4.5
that any continuous homomorphism
\lambda\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to L
gives rise to an extension (in the category of admissible locally analytic representations)

0\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)\to\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda_{\lambda}}\to\operatorname{St}_{2}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)\to 0.

Let us denote this by
\mathcal{C}_{1,\lambda}=\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda_{\lambda}}
to indicate the dependence on
i=1
. By reversing the roles of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, we also obtain an extension
\mathcal{C}_{2,\lambda}
of
\operatorname{St}_{1}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)
by
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)
in exactly the same way.

Proposition 6.7.1
.

One has a unique up to scalar
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant embedding

(6.7.2)

\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(L)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{2}_{c}\left(X_{G,K^{p}},L\right)[\pi^{p}].

Moreover, the embedding (
6.7.2
) extends to a
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant embedding

\mathcal{C}_{i,\lambda}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{2}_{c}\left(X_{G,K^{p}},L\right)[\pi^{p}]

if and only if
\lambda\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Geh21
, §5]
since the group
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
is “relatively definite”.
∎

Part II
Local-global compatibility and Fontaine–Mazur
\mathcal{L}
-invariants

In this second part of the article, we now discuss the exceptional zero formulae involving the Fontaine–Mazur
\mathcal{L}
-invariants. More precisely, we prove that the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants coincide with the Fontaine–Mazur
\mathcal{L}
-invariants (under some additional assumptions if
\pi
is not essentially self-dual). We have two approaches to this. The first only applies under the assumption that
\pi
is essentially self-dual – in this case, we can compare with the work of Rosso
[
Ros16
,
Ros15
]
. The second is to prove this comparison between
\mathcal{L}
-invariants if
\pi
is not necessarily essentially self-dual (which occupies the majority of Part
II
). The key input is to prove that the family of Galois representations associated with
\pi
over the
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
-eigenvariety admits a triangulation in families, which amounts to establishing local-global compatibility results extending
[
ACC+23
]
. We can then apply the method of Gehrmann–Rosso
[
GR22
]
.

7.
The symmetric square of a weight two modular form

Let
f
be a cuspidal newform of weight
2
and level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
and nebentypus
\varepsilon_{f}
, where
N
is prime to
p
. We assume that
\varepsilon_{f}(p)=1
(otherwise the symmetric square
p
-adic
L
-function of
f
will have no exceptional zeros). Let
\pi
denote the unitary normalisation of the automorphic representation of
G(\mathbb{A})
associated with the symmetric square lift
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f
, as constructed in
[
GJ78
]
. It satisfies the following properties:

•

\pi
is a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
, which satisfies
\pi^{\vee}\cong\pi\otimes\varepsilon_{f}^{-1}
, and is cohomological for the trivial representation.

•

The local component at
p
satisfies
\pi_{p}\cong\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
(because
\varepsilon_{f}(p)=1
).

•

Let
\rho_{f}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
denote the
p
-adic Galois representation associated with
f
, and set
\rho_{\pi}=\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\rho_{f}
. Then

L(\pi,s)=L(\rho_{\pi},s+1)

for
s\in\mathbb{C}
.

•

The restriction
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is semi-stable, and there exists a basis
\{v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}\}
of the associated filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module
D\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{st}}(\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}})
such that

\varphi v_{i}=p^{i}v_{i},\quad Nv_{i}=v_{i-1},\quad\quad i=0,1,2,

with the convention that
v_{-1}=0
.

Indeed, for the first bullet point see, for example,
[
NT21
, Theorem A]
noting that
f
cannot have CM because
p
divides its conductor exactly once. The remaining bullet points follow from known local-global compatibility results for essentially self-dual regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A})
and
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
(see, e.g.,
[
LW12
,
Car12
,
BLGGT14a
]
and the references therein, noting that the trivial weight for
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
is Shin-regular).

The jumps in the Hodge filtration
\operatorname{Fil}^{i}D
on
D
are at
i=0,1,2
, and as
D
is weakly admissible, there exist unique quantities
\mathcal{L}_{1,\pi}^{\operatorname{FM}},\mathcal{L}_{2,\pi}^{\operatorname{FM}}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
such that
v_{1}-\mathcal{L}_{1,\pi}^{\operatorname{FM}}v_{0}
(resp.
v_{2}-\mathcal{L}_{2,\pi}^{\operatorname{FM}}v_{1}
) is a basis of
\operatorname{Fil}^{1}D/\operatorname{Fil}^{2}D
(resp.
(\operatorname{Fil}^{2}D+\langle v_{0}\rangle)/\langle v_{0}\rangle
). Since
\rho_{\pi}^{\vee}\cong\rho_{\pi}(2)\otimes\varepsilon_{f}^{-1}
, one can easily check that
\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}^{\operatorname{FM}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{L}_{1,\pi}^{\operatorname{FM}}=\mathcal{L}_{2,\pi}^{\operatorname{FM}}
. We call
\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}^{\operatorname{FM}}
the
Fontaine–Mazur
\mathcal{L}
-invariant
associated with
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f
.

Remark 7.1
.

One can easily verify directly from the definitions that
\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}^{\operatorname{FM}}=\mathcal{L}_{f}^{\operatorname{FM}}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{f}^{\operatorname{FM}}
denotes the Fontaine–Mazur
\mathcal{L}
-invariant associated with
\rho_{f}
(c.f.,
[
Mok12
, Remark 4.2]
).

On the other hand, since
\pi^{\vee}\cong\pi\otimes\varepsilon_{f}^{-1}
, we have
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,2}
(see §
5.3.2
and
[
Geh21
, §3.4]
). We refer to this quantity as the automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant associated with
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f
. We obtain the following theorem relating the Fontaine–Mazur and automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants:

Theorem 7.2
.

With notation as above, one has
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}
.

Proof.

We will give two proofs of this theorem, with the first under more restrictive hypotheses.

Suppose first that
N
is square-free and
\varepsilon_{f}
is trivial. Under these assumptions, Rosso
[
Ros16
,
Ros15
]
constructs a
p
-adic measure
\mathscr{L}_{p}^{\operatorname{imp}}(\pi,-)
on
1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
which satisfies: for any Dirichlet character
\eta
of
p
-power conductor factoring through
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to 1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, one has

\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{imp}}_{p}(\pi,\eta)=C_{2,\eta^{-1}}\cdot E_{1}(2,\eta^{-1})\cdot\frac{L(\pi\times\eta,0)}{\pi\cdot W^{\prime}(f)\cdot\langle f,f\rangle_{\operatorname{Pet}}}

where the
\pi
in the denominator is the real number
3.14\cdots
, and

•

C_{2,\eta}=G(\eta)N_{f}^{-1}2^{-5/2}
,

•

E_{1}(2,\eta)=(1-\eta_{0}(p))
where
\eta_{0}
is the primitive character associated with
\eta
,

•

W^{\prime}(f)
is the prime-to-
p
part of the root number associated with
f
,

•

\langle f,f\rangle_{\operatorname{Pet}}
is the Petersson inner product of
f
with itself.

We first relate this with the
p
-adic
L
-function in
[
LW21
]
. More precisely, noting that
e^{-}_{p}(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},0)=G(\eta^{-1})E_{1}(2,\eta^{-1})L(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},0)
, we have

\mathscr{L}_{p}^{\operatorname{imp}}(\pi,\eta)=(N_{f}2^{5/2}W^{\prime}(f))^{-1}e^{-}_{p}(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},0)\cdot\frac{L(\pi\times\eta,0)}{L(\pi_{p}\times\eta_{p},0)\cdot\pi\langle f,f\rangle_{\operatorname{Pet}}}.

Set
Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(N_{f}2^{5/2}W^{\prime}(f))e^{-}_{\infty}(\pi_{\infty},0)\pi\langle f,f\rangle_{\operatorname{Pet}}(\Omega_{\pi}^{-})^{-1}\neq 0
. Then, since this quantity is independent of
\eta
(factoring through
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow 1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
), we have

\mathscr{L}_{\pi}^{-}|_{1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=Q\cdot\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{imp}}(\pi,-)

by comparing interpolation formulae and using Weierstrass preparation. The theorem now follows from combining
[
Ros15
, Theorem 1.3]
and Theorem
6.6.1
with the fact that
L(\pi,0)\neq 0
.

For the alternative proof, we now drop the assumption that
N
is square-free and
\varepsilon_{f}
is trivial. In this case, the theorem now follows from §
8.2.3
below.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

We note that, in
[
Ros16
,
Ros15
]
, an exceptional zero formula is obtained for general
N
at the expense of replacing the
p
-adic
L
-function with one interpolating values of the (complex) imprimitive
L
-function. In this generality, it does not seem possible to employ a similar strategy as in first proof of Theorem
7.2
to show that the Fontaine–Mazur and automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants coincide, since the imprimitive
L
-function can vanish at
s=0
for non-square-free
N
. This is why we use a different strategy (which also works for non-self-dual automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
) to obtain the equality of
\mathcal{L}
-invariants for general
N
.

Remark 7.4
.

Set
L_{p}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=L_{p}^{-}(\pi,s)
,
L(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=L(\pi,s)
and
\Omega(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty},0)^{-1}\cdot\Omega_{\pi}^{-}
. As a consequence of the
second
proof of Theorem
7.2
and Theorem
6.6.1
, we obtain the exceptional zero formula

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f,s)\right|_{s=0}=\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}^{\operatorname{FM}}\cdot\frac{L(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f,0)}{\Omega(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f)}

as predicted by Greenberg
[
Gre94
]
(see also
[
Ben11
]
). When
N
is square-free and
\varepsilon_{f}
trivial, this has of course already been proven in
[
Ros15
]
; however for general
N
and
\varepsilon_{f}
, this result appears to be new (to the best of the authors’ knowledge).

Remark 7.5
.

If
\xi
is a Dirichlet character of prime-to-
p
conductor with
\xi(p)=1
, then
\mathcal{L}_{\pi,i}^{\operatorname{Aut}}=\mathcal{L}_{\pi\otimes\xi,i}^{\operatorname{Aut}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\pi,i}^{\operatorname{FM}}=\mathcal{L}_{\pi\otimes\xi,i}^{\operatorname{FM}}
. Thus Remark
1.1.4
in the introduction follows from Theorem
7.2
.

8.
The Benois–Colmez–Greenberg–Stevens formulae

In this section, we extend Theorem
7.2
to automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
which are not necessary essentially self-dual, under certain assumptions on the existence of infinitesimal deformations of triangulations (Assumptions
8.2.1
and
8.3.5
). In particular, these assumptions can be verified in the self-dual setting, leading to the second proof of Theorem
7.2
.

8.1.
Families

Let
\mathcal{W}
denote the adic space over
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
whose
(R,R^{+})
-points parameterise pairs of continuous characters
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})
with
\kappa_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\to R^{\times}
. The space
\mathcal{W}
is isomorphic to the disjoint union of
2
-dimensional open discs; in particular, let
\mathcal{W}_{0}
denote the connected component containing the trivial character. Then
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is the adic generic fibre of
\operatorname{Spf}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![T_{1},T_{2}]\!]
with the universal character
\kappa^{\operatorname{univ}}=(\kappa_{1}^{\operatorname{univ}},\kappa_{2}^{\operatorname{univ}})
satisfying
\kappa_{i}^{\operatorname{univ}}=1
on the roots of unity
\mu_{p-1}
(if
p
odd) or
\mu_{2}
(if
p=2
), and

\kappa_{i}^{\operatorname{univ}}(v)=1+T_{i},\;\;\;\quad(i=1,2).

Here
v\in 1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a fixed topological generator with
\log_{p}(v)=1
; thus
\kappa_{i}^{\operatorname{univ}}(x)=(1+T_{i})^{\log_{p}(x)}
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. We can view any point
\kappa\in\mathcal{W}(R,R^{+})
as a character of
T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
by the formula
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\kappa_{1}(z_{1}z_{3}^{-1})\kappa_{2}(z_{2}z_{3}^{-1})
. We will use the same notation if there is no confusion.

Remark 8.1.1
.

In this normalisation, the one-dimensional “pure weight space”, supporting cuspidal cohomology, corresponds to the closed subspace
\mathcal{W}^{\operatorname{pure}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\kappa^{\operatorname{univ}}_{2}=1\}\subset\mathcal{W}
.

Let
\pi
be as in §
5.1
. We now explain how to deform
\pi
in a
p
-adic family. We note that, since the defect of the group
G=\operatorname{GL}_{3}
is
1
, we can only deform
\pi
in a family over a one-dimensional locus in
\mathcal{W}
(not necessarily the pure weight locus), and in general this family is not expected to have a Zariski-dense set of classical specialisations.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathcal{W}_{0}
denote the (affinoid) disc of radius
p^{-\rho}
centered at the trivial character, for some large
\rho\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
(which we will frequently increase as needed). Let
r=r_{\Omega}
denote the radius of analyticity of the universal character of
\Omega
. We define
D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega}
to be the space of
r
-analytic distributions (over
\Omega
) on the Iwahori subgroup of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. More precisely, let

A^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega}=\{f\colon\operatorname{Iw}\to\mathcal{O}(\Omega):f\text{ is }r\text{-analytic},f(b\cdot-)=\kappa_{\Omega}(b)\cdot f(-)\;\forall b\in\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap\operatorname{Iw}\}

and let
D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega}
denote the continuous dual of
A^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega}
. Let
T^{+}
(resp.
T^{-}
) be the submonoid of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
of elements which have non-negative valuation when paired with positive (resp. negative) roots. The space
A^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega}
carries actions of
\operatorname{Iw}
and
T^{-}
, and
D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega}
carries actions of
\operatorname{Iw}
and
T^{+}
, defined for our normalisations in
[
BW21
, §3.4]
. Note the actions of
T^{\pm}
and
\operatorname{Iw}
agree on the intersections.

Let
\mathcal{K}^{p}\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
be a compact open subgroup such that
(\pi^{p}_{f})^{\mathcal{K}^{p}}
is one-dimensional. We let
K^{p}\subset\mathcal{K}^{p}
denote a sufficiently small normal compact open subgroup, and we fix a finite set
S
of primes containing
p
and such that
K^{p}=K^{S}K_{S\backslash\{p\}}
with
K_{S\backslash\{p\}}\subset G(\mathbb{Q}_{S\backslash\{p\}})
and
K^{S}=\prod_{\ell\not\in S}G(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
. Let
K=K^{p}\operatorname{Iw}
to ease notation, and let
X_{G,K}
denote the locally symmetric space associated with
G
of level
K
. Let
\mathbf{T}^{S}=C^{\infty}_{c}(G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})/\!/G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S}),\mathbb{Z})
denote the abstract spherical Hecke algebra at primes not in
S
(with respect to the standard choices of Haar measures).

As
(\pi_{f}^{S})^{K^{S}}
is a line,
\mathbf{T}^{S}
acts on
(\pi_{f}^{p})^{K^{p}}
via an algebra homomorphism
\alpha^{S}\colon\mathbf{T}^{S}\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
\alpha_{p}\colon\mathbb{Z}[T^{+}]\to\mathbb{C}
denote the algebra homomorphism satisfying

U_{t}\cdot\varphi_{\operatorname{Iw}}=\alpha_{p}(t)\varphi_{\operatorname{Iw}}

for
\varphi_{\operatorname{Iw}}\in\pi_{p}\cong\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
any non-zero vector fixed by
\operatorname{Iw}
, where
U_{t}
is the Hecke operator associated with
t
. Both
\alpha^{S}
and
\alpha_{p}
are definable over a number field, and hence a finite extension
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We note that
\alpha_{p}
is in fact the trivial character.

Set
\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}=\mathbf{T}^{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[T^{+}]
, and let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}_{L}
denote the kernel of
\alpha^{S}\otimes\alpha_{p}
. We base-change everything to
L
, but omit this from the notation. Finally if
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
is a prime ideal, we let
\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\Omega
denote the Zariski closed subspace cut out by
\mathfrak{p}
.

Proposition 8.1.2
.

With notation as above, we have:

(1)

\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\{1\}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is zero if
i\neq 2,3
and one-dimensional over
L
for
i=2,3
.

(2)

For
\rho
sufficiently large, there exists a height one prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
contained in
(T_{1},T_{2})
, and an algebra homomorphism

\underline{\alpha}^{S}\otimes\underline{\alpha}_{p}\colon\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)/\mathfrak{p}}\to\mathcal{O}(\Omega)/\mathfrak{p}=\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}})

which specialises to
\alpha^{S}\otimes\alpha_{p}
at the trivial character, with the following property: the localised cohomology group
\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{I}
is zero if
i\neq 2,3
, and free of rank one over
\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}})
if
i=2,3
, where
I
denotes the kernel of
\underline{\alpha}^{S}\otimes\underline{\alpha}_{p}
. Moreover

\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}})}L\cong\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\{1\}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

for all
i
, where the tensor product is over the trivial character.

(3)

One has
\underline{\alpha}_{p}(T^{+})\subset\mathcal{O}^{+}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}})^{\times}
and (after possibly increasing
\rho
)
\underline{\alpha}_{p,1}\underline{\alpha}_{p,2}\underline{\alpha}_{p,3}=1
, where
\underline{\alpha}_{p,j}
denotes the character on the
(j,j)
-th element of the diagonal matrix (note
\underline{\alpha}_{p}
extends naturally to a character of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
).

Proof.

Cuspidal cohomology for
\GL(3)
is supported in degrees 2 and 3 by
[
FS98
, Proposition 4.1]
and Matsushima’s formula; part (1) then follows from Stevens’ control theorem in this setting (see, e.g.,
[
BW21
, Thm. 4.4]
).

For part (2), one can apply
[
HT17
, Thm. 4.9]
(noting that the compactly-supported version follows from the same proof). More precisely, since the non-abelian Leopoldt conjecture is known when the defect of the group is
1

[
Han17
, Thm. 1.1.5]
, one has
\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
if
i\neq 3
, and
\operatorname{H}^{3}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong\mathcal{O}(\Omega)_{\{1\}}/J
for a principal ideal
J
generated by a non-zero-divisor. One can now choose a height one prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
contained in
(T_{1},T_{2})
, which contains
J
after localising, and the Tor spectral sequence implies that:

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{3}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{O}(\Omega)_{\{1\}}/\mathfrak{p}=\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\{1\}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{2}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Tor}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)_{\{1\}}}_{1}(\operatorname{H}^{3}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}},\mathcal{O}(\Omega)_{\{1\}}/\mathfrak{p})\cong\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\{1\}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{j}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Tor}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)_{\{1\}}}_{3-j}(\operatorname{H}^{3}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}},\mathcal{O}(\Omega)_{\{1\}}/\mathfrak{p})=0\quad(j\neq 2,3)

where the last isomorphism in the second line follows from the fact that
J
is generated by a non-zero-divisor (and
J\subset\mathfrak{p}
). Via a standard rigid delocalisation process (as in
[
BDJ22
, Lem. 2.10]
), we deduce both the existence of the character
\underline{\alpha}^{S}\otimes\underline{\alpha}_{p}
, and that
\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{I}
is free of rank one over
\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}})
. The final statement of (2) follows from the Tor spectral sequence relating
\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\{1\}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Finally, to see (3), note that
\mathbb{Z}[T^{+}]
acts by integral units by ordinarity, giving the first statement. For the second, note
\underline{\alpha}_{p,1}\underline{\alpha}_{p,2}\underline{\alpha}_{p,3}
is the character of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
describing the action of the centre
Z(\GL_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
on
\operatorname{H}^{i}_{c}(X_{G,K},D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\operatorname{ord},\mathcal{K}^{p}/K^{p}}_{I}
. As we have restricted to the 2-dimensional ‘null’ weight space, the centre acts by a finite-order character at each point, and trivially at
\mathfrak{m}
(as
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg). Thus, by shrinking if necessary, we can assume it acts trivially at
I
.
∎

8.1.1.
The essentially self-dual case

We can say slightly more if
\pi
is essentially self-dual. In this case,
\pi
is a twisted symmetric square lift of a weight
2
cuspidal newform
f
of level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
and nebentypus
\varepsilon_{f}
satisfying
\varepsilon_{f}(p)=1
(as in §
7
). Then
f
is necessarily ordinary at
p
– let
\mathbf{f}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}(\mathbf{f})q^{n}
denote the (unique) Hida family passing through
f
defined over a small open neighbourhood of the trivial weight
8
8

8

Our convention is that the specialisation of
\mathbf{f}
at an integer weight
k\geqslant 0
in the one-dimensional weight space for
\GL_{2}
is a weight
k+2
modular form.
in the (one-dimensional)
\GL_{2}
weight space.

By the rigidity of eigenvarieties and
p
-adic Langlands functoriality
[
JN19b
, §3.2]
and
[
Han17
, §5.4]
, and since
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{f}}(p)=1
, we see that one may take
\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}
contained in the pure weight locus
\mathcal{W}^{\operatorname{pure}}
and

\underline{\alpha}_{p}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&&\\
&1&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\underline{\alpha}_{p}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&&\\
&p&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=a_{p}(\mathbf{f})^{2}.

8.2.
The automorphic Benois–Colmez–Greenberg–Stevens formula

With notation as in §
8.1
, set
\Sigma=\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}
. The tangent space
T_{1}(\Sigma)
of
\Sigma
at the origin is at least one-dimensional over
L
. We assume the following weaker version of the Calegari–Mazur conjecture (see
[
CM09
]
):

Assumption 8.2.1
.

Let
T_{1}(\mathcal{W})
denote the tangent space of
\mathcal{W}
at the identity, and let
PT_{1}(\mathcal{W})
denote the space of lines in
T_{1}(\mathcal{W})
. One has a natural map

(8.2.2)

T_{1}(\Sigma)\backslash\{0\}\to T_{1}(\mathcal{W})\backslash\{0\}\to PT_{1}(\mathcal{W}).

We assume that:

(A1)

There exists an element
0\neq v\in T_{1}(\Sigma)
which maps to the line
[(v_{1},v_{2})]\in PT_{1}(\mathcal{W})
such that
[(v_{1},v_{2})]\neq[(1,1)]
.

(A2)

There exists an element
0\neq v\in T_{1}(\Sigma)
which maps to the line
[(v_{1},v_{2})]\in PT_{1}(\mathcal{W})
such that
[(v_{1},v_{2})]\neq[(2,-1)]
.

Note that, automatically, either (A1) or (A2) holds.

Remark 8.2.3
.

For
i=1,2
, assumption (Ai) is precisely the property of admitting “non-
s_{i}
-infinitesimal deformations” as in §
1.2.2
.

Remark 8.2.4
.

The papers of Ash–Pollack–Stevens
[
APS08
]
and Calegari–Mazur
[
CM09
]
study classical
p
-adic families for
\GL(3)
and Bianchi modular forms respectively. They both conjecture that non-essentially-self-dual representations do not vary in classical
p
-adic families. Calegari–Mazur go further, predicting that such representations never vary infinitesimally along a rational line. The analogue of this stronger conjecture should also hold for
\GL(3)
; and this would say that if
\pi
is non-essentially-self-dual, then the image of (
8.2.2
) does not contain a line of the form
[(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})]
with
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Assumptions (A1) and (A2) would both be implied by this analogue.

Remark 8.2.5
.

If
\pi
is essentially self-dual, then Assumption
8.2.1
automatically holds because
\Sigma
is contained in the pure weight locus
\mathcal{W}^{\operatorname{pure}}
(so
(1,0)
is a basis of
T_{1}(\Sigma)
).

Let
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in T_{1}(\Sigma)
be a non-zero tangent vector. We will consider the first-order infinitesimal behaviour of the family in the direction of this tangent vector. Let
L[\epsilon]
denote the ring of dual numbers, and note we have a natural map
\mathcal{O}(\Sigma)\to L[\epsilon]
given by
T_{1}\mapsto v_{1}\epsilon
and
T_{2}\mapsto v_{2}\epsilon
; in particular,
T_{i}^{2}\mapsto 0
. As
\kappa_{i}^{\operatorname{univ}}(x)=(1+T_{i})^{\log_{p}(x)}
, the universal character over
L[\epsilon]
is then given by

(8.2.6)

\kappa_{i,\epsilon}(x)=1+\operatorname{log}_{p}(x)v_{i}\epsilon

for
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Set
\kappa_{3,\epsilon}=\kappa_{1,\epsilon}^{-1}\kappa_{2,\epsilon}^{-1}
.

Definition 8.2.7
.

For
i=1,2,3
, let
\delta_{i,v}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to L[\epsilon]^{\times}
denote the unique character such that
\delta_{i,v}|_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}=\kappa_{i,\varepsilon}
and
\delta_{i,v}(p)=\underline{\alpha}_{p,i}(p)
(specialised in this first-order neighbourhood). Note
\delta_{i,v}
is trivial modulo
\epsilon
.

8.2.1.
Koszul resolutions

One of the key ingredients in proving the relation between the automorphic and Galois
\mathcal{L}
-invariants is the locally analytic Koszul resolution of Kohlhasse–Schraen, which we now recall. For this we introduce some notation. Let
R
be a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-affinoid algebra, and let
\chi\colon\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R^{\times}
be a locally analytic character. Let
\chi_{0}\colon\operatorname{Iw}\cap\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R^{\times}
denote its restriction, which we suppose is
r_{\chi_{0}}
-analytic.

Recall that, for
r\geqslant r_{\chi_{0}}
, we let

A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}}=\{f\colon\operatorname{Iw}\to R:f\text{ is }r\text{-analytic},f(b\cdot-)=\chi_{0}(b)\cdot f(-)\;\forall b\in\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap\operatorname{Iw}\}

denote the
r
-analytic induction, which comes equipped with an action of
\operatorname{Iw}
and
T^{-}\subset T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. For any
t\in T^{-}
, let
U_{t}\colon G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\operatorname{End}_{R}(A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})
denote the unique bi-
\operatorname{Iw}
-equivariant morphism satisfying:
\operatorname{supp}(U_{t})=\operatorname{Iw}\cdot t\cdot\operatorname{Iw}
, and
U_{t}(t)=t\star-
(where
\star
denotes the action of
T^{-}
on
A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}}
). This gives rise to an endomorphism of
\operatorname{c-Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})
which we also denote by
U_{t}
.

We let

\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)=\left\{f\colon G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to R\text{ locally analytic }:f(b\cdot-)=\chi(b)f(-)\text{ for all }b\in\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\right\}

which is a representation of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. One has a natural
\operatorname{Iw}
-equivariant map
A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}}\to\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)
given by extending a function on the open cell
\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cdot\operatorname{Iw}
by zero. This induces a
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant map

(8.2.8)

\operatorname{c-Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})\to\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi).

Let
t_{1}=t=\operatorname{diag}(p,1,1)
and
t_{2}=\operatorname{diag}(p,p,1)
, so
t_{1}^{-1},t_{2}^{-1}\in T^{-}
. For
i=1,2
, we let
y_{i}=U_{t_{i}^{-1}}-\chi(t_{i})
denote the corresponding endomorphism of
