\operatorname{c-Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})
. The Koszul complex is

\Lambda^{\bullet}_{R}(R^{\oplus 2})\otimes_{R}\operatorname{c-Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}}),

concentrated in degrees
[-2,0]
, with differentials
d_{k}:\Lambda^{k}\otimes(-)\to\Lambda^{k-1}\otimes(-)
given by

d_{2}(e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\otimes f)=e_{2}\otimes y_{1}(f)-e_{1}\otimes y_{2}(f),\qquad d_{1}(e_{i}\otimes f)=y_{i}(f).

Here
e_{1},e_{2}
denotes the standard basis of
R^{\oplus 2}
.

Lemma 8.2.9

(c.f.,
[
KS12
, Thm. 2.5]
and
[
GR22
, Thm. 3.4]
)
.

With notation as above, suppose that
\chi
is trivial on the centre of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Then the augmented Koszul complex

\Lambda^{\bullet}_{R}(R^{\oplus 2})\otimes_{R}\operatorname{c-Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})\rightarrow\mathrm{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)\rightarrow 0

is exact.

Proof.

Note that, by the hypothesis on
\chi
, we can identify the induction
\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)
as an induction from the lower-triangular Borel in
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}
. Similarly, we can view
A_{\chi_{0}}^{r\mathrm{-an}}
as an induction to the image of
\operatorname{Iw}
in
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
. Thus, the lemma is a consequence of the same result for
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}
proved by Kohlhasse and Schraen
[
KS12
, Thm. 2.5]
. We note that in
loc.cit.
, we are free to arrange
\overline{B}
to be the choice of Borel and
\operatorname{Iw}
to be the choice of Iwahori subgroup.
∎

Let
D^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}}
denote the continuous
R
-linear dual of
A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}}
. Let
M^{\bullet}
denote the following complex of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-representations

\Lambda^{\bullet}_{R}(R^{\oplus 2})\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(D^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})

concentrated in degrees
[0,2]
, with differentials given by the dual of
d_{k}
above (identifying
M^{k}
with the continuous dual of
\Lambda^{k}_{R}(R^{\oplus 2})\otimes_{R}\operatorname{c-Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(A^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})
). Note that the dual of
d_{1}
is given by

d^{*}_{1}(\mu)=e_{1}\otimes z_{1}(\mu)+e_{2}\otimes z_{2}(\mu),

where
z_{i}=U_{t_{i}}-\chi(t_{i})
(resp.
U_{t_{i}}
) denotes the dual of
y_{i}
(resp.
U_{t_{i}^{-1}}
). From Lemma
8.2.9
we obtain:

Corollary 8.2.10
.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension and set
R=L
or
R=L[\epsilon]
with
\epsilon^{2}=0
. With notation and assumptions as in Lemma
8.2.9
, we obtain a resolution

0\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\mathrm{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)^{\ast})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M^{\bullet})

as
G(\mathbb{Q})
-representations. In particular
\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)^{*}))
is identified with the submodule

\operatorname{ker}(U_{t_{1}}-\chi(t_{1}))\cap\operatorname{ker}(U_{t_{2}}-\chi(t_{2}))\subset\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}D^{r\operatorname{-an}}_{\chi_{0}})).

Proof.

Note that we have a resolution as
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-representations

0\to\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)^{*}\to M^{\bullet}.

Indeed this follows from the same argument using the Hahn–Banach theorem as in the proof of
[
GR22
, Prop. 3.9]
; there exactness is only discussed for coefficients in a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, but the extension to
R=L[\epsilon]
is immediate. Applying
\mathcal{A}_{G,c}
to this resolution preserves exactness;
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_{G},-)
is exact, since
D_{G}
is a projective
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

Truncating this resolution to the short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)^{*})\to\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M^{0})\to\operatorname{Im}(d_{1}^{*})\to 0
, and taking the long exact sequence in
G(\mathbb{Q})
-cohomology, we see
\mathrm{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)^{*}))
is identified with the kernel of

\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M^{0}))\xrightarrow{d_{1}^{*}}\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(M^{1})),

so the last part follows from the explicit description of
d_{1}^{*}
above.
∎

Remark 8.2.11
.

It is likely that a version of Corollary
8.2.10
holds for general affinoid algebras
R
. Since we only need the result when
R=L
or
R=L[\epsilon]
, we restrict to this case in order to avoid any complications with the failure of the Hahn–Banach theorem over general affinoid algebras.

8.2.2.
Automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants via infinitesimal deformations

We have the following result:

Proposition 8.2.12
.

Let
\partial\delta_{i,v}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to L
denote the unique continuous homomorphism satisfying
\delta_{i,v}=1+\partial\delta_{i,v}\epsilon
. Then:

(1)

\partial\delta_{1,v}\neq\partial\delta_{2,v}
if Assumption
8.2.1
(A1) holds, and
\partial\delta_{2,v}\neq\partial\delta_{3,v}
if Assumption
8.2.1
(A2) holds.

(2)

One has

\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v}\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{1}(\pi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\partial\delta_{2,v}-\partial\delta_{3,v}\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{2}(\pi).

In particular, if Assumption
8.2.1
(A1) (resp. Assumption
8.2.1
(A2)) holds, then
\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v}
is a basis of
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{1}(\pi)
(resp.
\partial\delta_{2,v}-\partial\delta_{3,v}
is a basis of
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{2}(\pi)
).

Proof.

Note
\left(\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v}\right)|_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}=(v_{1}-v_{2})\operatorname{log}_{p}
and
\left(\partial\delta_{2,v}-\partial\delta_{3,v}\right)|_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}=(v_{1}+2v_{2})\operatorname{log}_{p}
. Part (1) now follows from Assumption
8.2.1
. We now prove part (2) following
[
GR22
]
.

Let
\chi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\delta_{1,v}\delta_{2,v}^{-1}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to L[\varepsilon]^{\times}
and set
\lambda=\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v}
. Therefore,
\chi=1+\lambda\cdot\epsilon
. We extend
\chi
to
\overline{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
by
\chi(b)=\chi(t_{1}^{-1}t_{2})
where
b=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t_{1}&&\\
\ast&t_{2}&\\
\ast&\ast&t_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and we consider the locally analytic induction
\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)
introduced in §
8.2.1
(with
R=L[\epsilon]
). Let
r\geqslant r_{\chi_{0}}
, and take
\mathcal{K}^{p}
to be the prime-to-
p
level in the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(-)
. Then we have a commutative diagram:

(8.2.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(\chi)^{*}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}D^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{\chi_{0}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{I}_{\overline{B}}^{\operatorname{la}}(L)^{*}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}D^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{1}))}}

where both vertical maps are specialisation mod
\epsilon
, and the horizontal maps are induced from (
8.2.8
). Let
I^{\prime}
denote the kernel
\underline{\alpha}^{S}
in
\mathbf{T}_{L[\epsilon]}^{S}
and let
\mathfrak{m}
denote the kernel of
\alpha^{S}\otimes\alpha_{p}
. From Corollary
8.2.10
and Proposition
8.1.2
we deduce that for the cohomology in degree
0
, the composition of the top horizontal map and the right-hand vertical map in (
8.2.13
) localised at
I^{\prime}
has image which contains
\operatorname{H}^{0}(G(\mathbb{Q}),\mathcal{A}_{G,c}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{Iw}}^{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}D^{r\mathrm{-an}}_{1}))_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{ord}}
. Here we use the analogue of Proposition
3.2.6
with non-trivial coefficients to compare
p
-arithmetic and compactly-supported cohomology (as in
[
Geh21
, §3.6]
).

Additionally, by Corollary
8.2.10
, the bottom horizontal map is injective on cohomology in degree
0
with image the space where
U_{t_{1}}
and
U_{t_{2}}
act trivially. Combining both of these properties, we see that the hypotheses in
[
GR22
, Proposition 2.4]
hold, and we conclude by applying
loc.cit.
(noting that the compactly-supported version follows from exactly the same proof).

One uses a similar argument for
\partial\delta_{2,v}-\partial\delta_{3,v}
, and we leave the details to the reader.
∎

8.2.3.
Proof of Theorem
7.2

Assume that we are in the essentially self-dual setting of §
8.1.1
. Then we may take
\Sigma=\Omega_{\mathfrak{p}}
to be a small disc around the origin in the pure weight line
\mathcal{W}^{\operatorname{pure}}
, with universal character
(\kappa,1)
for
\kappa(x)=\operatorname{exp}(k\operatorname{log}(x))
for all
x\in 1+2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
k\in\mathcal{O}(\Sigma)
with
|k|\leqslant p^{-\rho}
. View
a_{p}=a_{p}(\mathbf{f})
as a rigid analytic function in the variable
k
and set
v=(1,0)
. Then we calculate that

•

(\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v})|_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}=\operatorname{log}_{p}
, and

•

recalling
\underline{\alpha}_{p,1}(p)=a_{p}(\mathbf{f})^{2}
and noting that
\underline{\alpha}_{p,2}(p)=1
, we have

\partial\delta_{1,v}(p)-\partial\delta_{2,v}(p)=\partial\underline{\alpha}_{p,1}(p)=\partial a_{p}(\mathbf{f})^{2}=2\left.\tfrac{da_{p}}{dk}\right|_{k=0}.

Combining, we see

\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v}=\operatorname{log}_{p}+2\left.\tfrac{da_{p}}{dk}\right|_{k=0}\cdot\operatorname{ord}_{p}.

By Remark
8.2.5
and Proposition
8.2.12
, we see that
\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v}
is a basis of
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{1}(\pi)
; thus

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}^{\operatorname{Aut}}=\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\pi,1}=-2\left.\tfrac{da_{p}}{dk}\right|_{k=0}=\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}f}^{\operatorname{FM}},

yielding Theorem
7.2
. The last equality here is
[
Hid04
]
(see also
[
Mok12
]
). ∎

8.3.
The Galois Benois–Colmez–Greenberg–Stevens formula

We now consider the Galois side. Suppose that
p\geqslant 7
and let
\omega\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
denote the
p
-adic cyclotomic character (i.e.,
\omega(p)=1
and
\omega(x)=x
for
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
). Let
\rho_{\pi}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{3}(L)
denote the
p
-adic Galois representation associated with
\pi
.

Assumption 8.3.1
.

We assume that the mod
p
residual representation
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
is absolutely irreducible and decomposed generic. By the latter condition, we mean that there exists an odd prime
\ell\neq p
such that:
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
is unramified at
\ell
, and neither
\ell
nor
\ell^{-1}
is an eigenvalue of
\operatorname{Ad}\overline{\rho}_{\pi}(\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell})\in\operatorname{GL}_{9}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
.

By
[
ACC+23
, Corollary 5.5.2]
(see Theorem
9.2.6
), we have

\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&*_{1}&*\\
&\omega^{-1}&*_{2}\\
&&\omega^{-2}\end{array}\right)

for certain extension classes
*_{i}\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},L(1))
. We also make the following assumption:

Assumption 8.3.2
.

Suppose that
*_{1}
and
*_{2}
are non-split extensions.

Remark 8.3.3
.

Whether
*_{i}
is split or non-split should be closely related to whether
\pi
has CM or not. Since
\pi_{p}
is assumed to be the Steinberg representation,
\pi
cannot have CM and we therefore expect at least one of
*_{1}
or
*_{2}
to be non-split. Furthermore,
\pi_{p}
being Steinberg should correspond to maximal rank monodromy at
p
on
\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{st}}(\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}})
, so we expect the stronger property that
*_{1}
and
*_{2}
are non-crystalline. Unfortunately, even understanding when the local Galois representation at
p
associated with a modular form is split or non-split is still an open problem.

In the introduction, this assumption is built into the ‘Steinberg-at-
p
’ assumption on
\rho_{\pi}
.

Definition 8.3.4
.

For
i=1,2
, we denote by
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{i}(\pi)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cts}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},L)\cong\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},L)
the one-dimensional subspace given by the orthogonal complement of
\langle*_{i}\rangle\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},L(1))
under Tate local duality. If
*_{i}
is non-crystalline, we let
\mathcal{L}_{\pi,i}^{\operatorname{FM}}\in L
denote the unique element such that
\operatorname{log}_{p}-\mathcal{L}_{\pi,i}^{\operatorname{FM}}\operatorname{ord}_{p}
is a basis of
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{i}(\pi)
.

8.3.1.
Families of trianguline representations

We have the following theorem, which follows from the results in §
9
. Its proof will be postponed until §
9.8
.

Theorem 8.3.5
.

There exists a rank
3
trianguline
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module
D_{L[\epsilon]}
over
L[\epsilon]
with parameters

\{\delta_{1,v},\delta_{2,v}\omega^{-1},\delta_{3,v}\omega^{-2}\}

(with notation as in Definition
8.2.7
). Moreover, the specialisation of
D_{L[\epsilon]}
mod
\epsilon
corresponds, via
p
-adic Hodge theory, to
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
.

8.3.2.
Equality of
\mathcal{L}
-invariants

We now prove an equality of automorphic and Fontaine–Mazur
\mathcal{L}
-invariants without assuming any self-duality.

Theorem 8.3.6
.

Suppose that Assumptions
8.3.1
and
8.3.2
hold. Then at least one of
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{1}(\pi)=\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{1}(\pi)
or
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{2}(\pi)=\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{2}(\pi)
holds. More precisely, for
\operatorname{i}\in\{1,2\}
, if Assumption
8.2.1
(A
\operatorname{i}
) holds then
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{\operatorname{i}}(\pi)=\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\operatorname{i}}(\pi)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.2.12
, it suffices to show
\partial\delta_{1,v}-\partial\delta_{2,v}\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{1}(\pi)
or
\partial\delta_{2,v}-\partial\delta_{3,v}\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{FM}}_{2}(\pi)
. This follows from the Benois–Colmez–Greenberg–Stevens formula
[
Ben11
]
,
[
Din19
, Thm. 3.4]
.
∎

As a consequence, we obtain the following by combining this with Theorems
6.6.1
and
6.6.2
.

Corollary 8.3.7
.

Suppose that Assumptions
8.3.1
and
8.3.2
hold. If Assumption
8.2.1
(A1) holds, then we obtain the exceptional zero formula:

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{-}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=0}=e_{\infty}^{-}(\pi_{\infty},0)\cdot\mathcal{L}_{\pi,1}^{\operatorname{FM}}\cdot\frac{L(\pi,0)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{-}}.

If Assumption
8.2.1
(A2) holds, then we obtain the exceptional zero formula:

\left.\frac{d}{ds}L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)\right|_{s=1}=-p\cdot e_{\infty}^{+}(\pi_{\infty},1)\cdot\mathcal{L}_{\pi,2}^{\operatorname{FM}}\cdot\frac{L(\pi,1)}{\Omega_{\pi}^{+}}.

In particular, the exceptional zero conjecture of Greenberg–Benois holds for at least one of
L_{p}^{-}(\pi,s)
or
L_{p}^{+}(\pi,s)
under Assumptions
8.3.1
and
8.3.2
.

Remark 8.3.8
.

Note that the statement in the above corollary is indeed well-defined because
\operatorname{ord}_{p}\not\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
(Proposition
5.3.6
), hence
*_{i}
is non-crystalline if
\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\operatorname{FM}}(\pi)=\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{Aut}}_{i}(\pi)
.

9.
Local-global compatibility at
\ell=p

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
8.3.5
. In order to do this, we will extend the local-global compatibility results in the ordinary case in
[
ACC+23
, §5]
to cuspidal automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{A})
, with the main result being Theorem
9.7.1
. In fact, there is nothing special about
\operatorname{GL}_{3}
in this section, and we obtain local-global compatibility results for cuspidal automorphic representations of
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A})
, for any
n\geqslant 2
. Since we are working in a more general setting, the notation in this section therefore differs slightly from the rest of the article.

9.1.
Notation and conventions

Let
n\geqslant 2
be an integer.
9
9

9

Almost everything in this section also applies with
n=1
; however we exclude this case since the main result (Theorem
9.7.1
) becomes trivial.
Throughout this section, we fix the following notation and conventions.

9.1.1.
The groups

Let
\widetilde{G}=\operatorname{GSp}_{2n}
denote the general symplectic group in
2n
-variables where, for any
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
R
, one has

\widetilde{G}(R)=\left\{x\in\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(R):\begin{array}[]{c}x^{t}Jx=s(x)J\\
\text{ for some }s(x)\in R^{\times}\end{array}\right\},\quad\quad J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&&&&1\\
&&&&\iddots&\\
&&&1&&\\
&&-1&&&\\
&\iddots&&&&\\
-1&&&&&\end{smallmatrix}\right).

We let
P\subset\widetilde{G}
denote the upper-triangular Siegel parabolic subgroup, with Levi subgroup
M\cong\GL_{n}\times\operatorname{GL}_{1}
and unipotent radical
N
. Let
T\subset\widetilde{G}
denote the standard diagonal torus, which we will describe as elements of the form

\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n},st_{n}^{-1},\dots,st_{1}^{-1})\in T.

We will almost always denote such an element of the torus by
(t_{1},\dots,t_{n};s)
. The identification
M\cong\GL_{n}\times\operatorname{GL}_{1}
takes the element
(t_{1},\dots,t_{n};s)\in T
to
\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\times s
; we can therefore identify
T
with the maximal torus in
M
. We will also use the notation
T_{\GL_{n}}
for the standard diagonal torus in
\GL_{n}
.

Let
T^{+}\subset T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(resp.
T_{M}^{+}\subset T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
) denote the submonoid of elements which satisfy
v_{p}\langle t,\alpha\rangle\geqslant 0
for every positive root
\alpha
in
\widetilde{G}
(resp. every positive root in
M
). Of course, one has
T^{+}\subset T_{M}^{+}
. Let
W_{\mathscr{G}}
denote the Weyl group of a split reductive group
\mathscr{G}
, and let
w_{0}^{\mathscr{G}}
denote the longest Weyl element. Let
{{}^{M}W}
denote the set of minimal length representatives for
W_{M}\backslash W_{\widetilde{G}}
. This set has size
2^{n}
and the lengths of the elements range from
0
to
d=n(n+1)/2
(exhausting this list of possibilities). We note that, for any
w\in{{}^{M}W}
, one has
wT^{+}w^{-1}\subset T_{M}^{+}
.

If we view
W_{\widetilde{G}}
as the subgroup of
S_{2n}
of permutations
w
which satisfy
w(i)+w(2n+1-i)=2n+1
, then we have the following explicit description of the elements of
{{}^{M}W}
.

Lemma 9.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
be a subset, which we write as
\mathcal{B}=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\}
with
b_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant b_{l}
. Let
\mathcal{B}^{c}=\{1,\dots,n\}-\mathcal{B}=\{b_{1}^{\prime},\dots,b_{n-l}^{\prime}\}
, with
b_{1}^{\prime}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant b_{n-l}^{\prime}
. We let
w_{\mathcal{B}}\in S_{2n}
denote the permutation which satisfies

w_{\mathcal{B}}(c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}n+i&\text{ if }c=b_{i}\in{\mathcal{B}}\\
i&\text{ if }c=b_{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{c}\end{array}\right.

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and
w_{\mathcal{B}}(c)=2n+1-w_{\mathcal{B}}(2n+1-c)
for
n+1\leqslant c\leqslant 2n
. By convention, we let
w_{\varnothing}
denote the identity, and
w_{\{1,\dots,n\}}=w_{0}^{M}w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}
. Then

{{}^{M}W}=\{w_{\mathcal{B}}\in S_{2n}:{\mathcal{B}}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}\}

and
l(w_{\mathcal{B}})=\sum_{b\in{\mathcal{B}}}(n+1-b)
.

Proof.

The Weyl group
W_{\widetilde{G}}
is described as those permutations
w
in
S_{2n}
which satisfy
w(i)+w(2n+1-i)=2n+1
. Furthermore, the set
{{}^{M}W}
is characterised by the condition that
\Phi_{M}^{+}\subset w\Phi^{+}
where
\Phi_{M}^{+}
and
\Phi^{+}
denote the positive roots in
M
and
\widetilde{G}
respectively. Equivalently, the set
{{}^{M}W}
consists of the permutations
w\in W_{\widetilde{G}}
which satisfy

w^{-1}(1)<\cdots<w^{-1}(n).

This amounts to the description in the statement of the lemma.
∎

9.1.2.
Algebraic representations

Let
X^{*}(T)
denote the group of algebraic characters of
T
. We will denote such a character by
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n};\lambda_{0})
, where
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
are such that

\lambda(t_{1},\dots,t_{n};s)=t_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots t_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}s^{\lambda_{0}}.

The weight
\lambda
is
\widetilde{G}
-dominant (denoted
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
) if
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{n}\geqslant 0
, and it is
M
-dominant (denoted
X^{*}(T)^{+}_{M}
) if
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{n}
. We will often write the group law on characters additively. We let
\rho\in X^{*}(T)_{\mathbb{Q}}
denote the half-sum of the positive roots of
\widetilde{G}
, and for any
w\in W_{\widetilde{G}}
and
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
, we let

\lambda_{w}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=w\star\lambda\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=w\cdot(\lambda+\rho)-\rho

denote the shifted Weyl action.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension, and let
\mathcal{O}
be its ring of integers. For any
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
, we let

V_{\widetilde{G},\lambda}=V_{\widetilde{G},\lambda,\mathcal{O}}=\{f\colon\widetilde{G}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}:f(-\cdot b)=(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\lambda)(b^{-1})\cdot f(-)\text{ for all }b\in B\}

where
B\subset\widetilde{G}
denotes the upper-triangular Borel subgroup. This is an algebraic representation of
\widetilde{G}(\mathcal{O})
of highest weight
\lambda
, where the action is given by left-translation by the inverse. We equip this representation with an action of
T^{+}
by the formula
(t\cdot f)(-)=f(t^{-1}\cdot-\cdot\langle t\rangle)
, where
\langle(t_{1},\dots,t_{n};s)\rangle=(|t_{1}|_{p}^{-1},\dots,|t_{n}|_{p}^{-1};|s|_{p}^{-1})
. Here the
p
-adic valuation is normalised so that
|p|_{p}=p^{-1}
. If
t\in T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, then this action agrees with the usual action given by the left-translation by the inverse. We make similar definitions for the groups
M
and
\GL_{n}
(which carry actions of
T_{M}^{+}
and
T_{\GL_{n}}^{+}
respectively), and often drop the group from the notation when it is clear from the context.

We note that we will sometimes consider
V_{\widetilde{G},\lambda,L}=V_{\widetilde{G},\lambda}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}L
, but we will
only ever
view this as a representation of
\widetilde{G}(\mathcal{O})
and
T^{+}
as above (and not as a representation of
\widetilde{G}(L)
in the usual way). This will mean that, when we consider the cohomology of the locally symmetric space associated with
\widetilde{G}
with coefficients in the local system
V_{\widetilde{G},\lambda,L}
, the action of the Hecke operators at
p
will automatically be optimally normalised.

9.1.3.
Hecke algebras

Let
S
be a finite set of primes containing
p
, and fix Haar measures on
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})
,
M(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})
,
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})
such that the volumes of
\widetilde{G}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S})
,
M(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S})
,
\GL_{n}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S})
are
1
. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=C_{c}^{\infty}(\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})/\!/\widetilde{G}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S}),\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{S}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=C_{c}^{\infty}(M(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})/\!/M(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S}),\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{S}_{\GL_{n}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=C_{c}^{\infty}(\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})/\!/\GL_{n}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S}),\mathcal{O})

denote the spherical Hecke algebras over
\mathcal{O}
for the primes away from
S
where the convolution product is with respect to the above fixed Haar measures. We have an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra homomorphism (the unnormalised, or twisted, Satake morphism)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{T}^{S}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[m\mapsto\int_{N(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})}f(mn)dn\right]

given by restricting to
P
and integrating over the fibres of
P\to M
(here,
dn
is the Haar measure on
N(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S})
normalised so that
N(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S})
has volume
1
).

We let
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}[T^{+}]
,
\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}=\mathbf{T}^{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}[T_{M}^{+}]
, and
\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}_{\GL_{n}}=\mathbf{T}^{S}_{\GL_{n}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}[T_{\GL_{n}}^{+}]
. If
w\in{{}^{M}W}
, we will let
\mathcal{S}_{w}\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}\to\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
which coincides with
\mathcal{S}
on the first factor, and is induced by the map
t\mapsto wtw^{-1}
on the second factor. For any
t\in T^{+}
, we let
\widetilde{U}_{t}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
denote the element corresponding to the class
[t]\in\mathcal{O}[T^{+}]
. Similarly, for
t\in T_{M}^{+}
(resp.
t\in T_{\GL_{n}}^{+}
), we let
U_{t}\in\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
(resp.
\mathbf{T}_{\GL_{n}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
) denote the element corresponding to the class
[t]\in\mathcal{O}[T_{M}^{+}]
(resp.
[t]\in\mathcal{O}[T_{\GL_{n}}^{+}]
). To simplify notation, for any integer
i=0,1,\dots,n
, we let
\widetilde{U}_{i}=\widetilde{U}_{t_{i}}
and
U_{i}=U_{t_{i}}
, where

t_{i}=(p,\dots,p,1,\dots,1;1)\in T^{+}

with
i
lots of
p
(if we want to view
t\in T^{+}_{\GL_{n}}
, we simply forget the last
\operatorname{GL}_{1}
-entry).

For any element
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and
i=1,\dots,n
, we let
\widetilde{\langle u\rangle}_{i}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord},\times}
and
\langle u\rangle_{i}\in\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord},\times}
(or
\langle u\rangle_{i}\in\mathbf{T}_{\GL_{n}}^{S,\operatorname{ord},\times}
) denote the element corresponding to the class

[(1,\dots,1,u,1,\dots,1;1)]\in\mathcal{O}[T^{+}]

where
u
is in the
i
-th place. In the following definition, we let
\operatorname{Art}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{ab}}
denote the Artin reciprocity map, normalised so that
p
is sent to a geometric Frobenius.

Definition 9.1.2
.

Let
\omega\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
denote the
p
-adic cyclotomic character.

(1)

Let
R
be any
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
-algebra in which the operators
\widetilde{U}_{t}
(
t\in T^{+}
) become invertible. Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n};\lambda_{0})\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
. For
i=1,\dots,2n+1
, we let
\psi_{\lambda,i}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to R^{\times}
denote the unique continuous character satisfying:

\psi_{\lambda,i}(\operatorname{Art}(u))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\omega^{n+1-i}(\operatorname{Art}(u))u^{(w_{0}^{M}\lambda)_{n+1-i}}\widetilde{\langle u\rangle}_{n+1-i}^{-1}&\text{ if }1\leqslant i\leqslant n\\
1&\text{ if }i=n+1\\
\omega^{n+1-i}(\operatorname{Art}(u))u^{-(w_{0}^{M}\lambda)_{i-(n+1)}}\widetilde{\langle u\rangle}_{i-(n+1)}&\text{ if }n+2\leqslant i\leqslant 2n+1\end{array}\right.

for
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and

\psi_{\lambda,i}(\operatorname{Art}(p))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\omega^{n+1-i}(\operatorname{Art}(p))\widetilde{U}_{{n-i}}\widetilde{U}_{{n+1-i}}^{-1}&\text{ if }1\leqslant i\leqslant n\\
1&\text{ if }i=n+1\\
\omega^{n+1-i}(\operatorname{Art}(p))\widetilde{U}_{{i-(n+1)}}\widetilde{U}^{-1}_{{i-(n+2)}}&\text{ if }n+2\leqslant i\leqslant 2n+1\end{array}\right..

Here
(w_{0}^{M}\lambda)_{i}=\lambda_{n+1-i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

(2)

Let
R
be any
\mathbf{T}_{\GL_{n}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
-algebra in which the operators
U_{t}
(
t\in T_{\GL_{n}}^{+}
) become invertible. Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in X^{*}(T_{\GL_{n}})^{+}
. For
i=1,\dots,n
, we let
\chi_{\lambda,i}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to R^{\times}
denote the unique continuous character satisfying:

\chi_{\lambda,i}(\operatorname{Art}(u))=\omega^{1-i}(\operatorname{Art}(u))u^{-(w_{0}^{\GL_{n}}\lambda)_{i}}\langle u\rangle_{i}

for
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and

\chi_{\lambda,i}(\operatorname{Art}(p))=\omega^{1-i}(\operatorname{Art}(p))U_{i}U_{{i-1}}^{-1}.

Here
(w_{0}^{\GL_{n}}\lambda)_{i}=\lambda_{n+1-i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

In (2), if
\lambda
is the trivial character, then we often drop it from the notation and simply write
\chi_{i}
.

Finally, if
C
is a complex of
\mathcal{O}
-modules (resp.
L
-modules) on which

\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
, etc. acts, then we write
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(C)
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}(C)
, etc. for the image of
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{L}^{S},\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{L}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
) etc. in the endomorphism algebra of
C
. If we view
C
as an object in the derived category
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{O})
of
\mathcal{O}
-modules, then we will consider the image of the Hecke algebra in the endomorphism algebra
\operatorname{End}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{O})}(C)
.

9.1.4.
Dual groups and Hecke polynomials

Recall that the dual group of
\widetilde{G}=\operatorname{GSp}_{2n}
can (and will) be identified with
\operatorname{GSpin}_{2n+1}
(see, e.g.,
[
KS23
]
for the definition of the general spin group). We will identify
T
with the standard maximal torus in
\operatorname{GSpin}_{2n+1}
. We will consider the Hecke polynomial associated with the standard representation
r_{\operatorname{GSpin},\operatorname{std}}\colon\operatorname{GSpin}_{2n+1}\to\operatorname{SO}_{2n+1}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}
.

Definition 9.1.3
.

Let
\ell\not\in S
. For
i=0,1,\dots,2n+1
, let
\widetilde{T}_{\ell,i}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})/\!/\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),\mathcal{O})
denote the Hecke operator which corresponds to
\operatorname{tr}(\wedge^{i}r_{\operatorname{GSpin},\operatorname{std}})
under the
normalised
Satake isomorphism.
10
10

10

Note that
\widetilde{T}_{\ell,i}
does indeed have coefficients in
\mathcal{O}
and not just
\mathcal{O}[\ell^{\pm 1/2}]
. This is because the half-sum of the positive roots for
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}
is integral (it is
(n,n-1,\dots,1)
). In particular,
\ell^{n+1-i}\widetilde{T}_{\ell,i}=\ell^{n+1-i}\widetilde{T}_{\ell,2n+1-i}
is the Hecke operator corresponding to
(\ell,\dots,\ell,1,\dots,1;1)\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
for
i=1,\dots,n
, and
\widetilde{T}_{\ell,0}=\widetilde{T}_{\ell,2n+1}
is the identity.
We define:

\widetilde{H}_{\ell}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\widetilde{T}_{\ell,1}X+\cdots+(-1)^{i}\widetilde{T}_{\ell,i}X^{i}+\cdots-\widetilde{T}_{\ell,2n+1}X^{2n+1}

which is a polynomial with coefficients in
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
.

We will also need the standard Hecke polynomials for
\GL_{n}
. Let
r_{\GL_{n},\operatorname{std}}\colon\GL_{n}\times\operatorname{GL}_{1}\to\GL_{n}
denote the projection to the first factor.

Definition 9.1.4
.

Let
\ell\notin S
. For
i=0,1,\dots,n
, let
T_{\ell,i}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})/\!/M(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),\mathcal{O}[\ell^{\pm 1/2}])
denote the Hecke operator which corresponds to
\operatorname{tr}(\wedge^{i}r_{\GL_{n},\operatorname{std}})
under the normalised Satake isomorphism. We define:

H_{\ell}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\ell^{(n-1)/2}T_{\ell,1}X+\cdots+(-1)^{i}\ell^{i(n-1)/2}T_{\ell,i}X^{i}+\cdots+(-1)^{n}\ell^{n(n-1)/2}T_{\ell,n}X^{n}

which is a polynomial with coefficients in
\mathbf{T}^{S}
. We also let
H_{\ell}(X)
denote the image of the polynomial under the natural map
\mathbf{T}^{S}[X]\to\mathbf{T}^{S}_{\GL_{n}}[X]
(given by projecting to the
\GL_{n}
factor in
M
). We also let

H^{\vee}_{\ell}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\ell^{-(n-1)/2}T_{\ell,n}^{-1}T_{\ell,n-1}X+\cdots+(-1)^{i}\ell^{-i(n-1)/2}T_{\ell,n}^{-1}T_{\ell,n-i}X^{i}+\cdots+(-1)^{n}\ell^{-n(n-1)/2}T_{\ell,n}^{-1}X^{n}

which is a polynomial in
\mathbf{T}^{S}[X]
(note that
T_{\ell,n}
is invertible in
\mathbf{T}^{S}
).

We note the following key identity:

(9.1.5)

\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{H}_{\ell}(X))=(1-X)\cdot H_{\ell}(\ell\cdot X)\cdot H^{\vee}_{\ell}(\ell^{-1}\cdot X)

which can can be proven by decomposing
\wedge^{i}r_{\operatorname{GSpin},\operatorname{std}}
as a representation of
\widehat{M}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}_{2n+1}
and comparing the normalised Satake isomorphisms for
\widetilde{G}
and
M
(c.f., the discussion just before
[
Sch15
, Corollary 5.2.6]
, noting that our normalisations differ from
loc.cit.
by
\ell^{\pm 1}
).

9.1.5.
Locally symmetric spaces

For
\mathscr{G}\in\{\widetilde{G},M,\GL_{n}\}
and
K\subset\mathscr{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
a neat compact open subgroup, we let
X_{\mathscr{G},K}
denote the locally symmetric space associated with
\mathscr{G}
of level
K
. Note that the (real) dimensional of
X_{\mathscr{G},K}
is
n(n+1)=2d
(resp.
n(n+1)/2-1=d-1
) if
\mathscr{G}=\widetilde{G}
(resp.
\mathscr{G}\in\{M,\GL_{n}\}
).

We consider the following compact open subgroups in the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-points of these groups. For
c\geqslant b\geqslant 0
with
c\geqslant 1
, we let

\widetilde{\operatorname{Iw}}(b,c)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{x\in\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):x\text{ mod }p^{c}\in B(\mathbb{Z}/p^{c}\mathbb{Z}),x\text{ mod }p^{b}\in U(\mathbb{Z}/p^{b}\mathbb{Z})\}

recalling
B
is the upper-triangular Borel in
\widetilde{G}
, and where
U\subset B
denotes its unipotent radical. We also define

\operatorname{Iw}(b,c)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{x\in M(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):x\text{ mod }p^{c}\in B_{M}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{c}\mathbb{Z}),x\text{ mod }p^{b}\in U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{b}\mathbb{Z})\}

where
B_{M}
denotes the upper-triangular Borel subgroup in
M
with unipotent radical
U_{M}
. We also write
\operatorname{Iw}(b,c)
for the image of this group under the natural map
M(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

9.1.6.
Borel–Serre compactifications

For an integer
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
, let
\mathbf{j}
denote a partition
j=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{a}
. Let
Q_{\mathbf{j}}\subset\widetilde{G}
denote the standard parabolic subgroup of
\widetilde{G}
with Levi

M_{\mathbf{j}}\cong\operatorname{GL}_{j_{1}}\times\cdots\times\operatorname{GL}_{j_{a}}\times\operatorname{GSp}_{2(n-j)},

where our convention is that
\operatorname{GSp}_{0}=\operatorname{GL}_{1}
. Let

\mathfrak{X}_{\widetilde{G}}=\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\left(\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\times X_{\widetilde{G}}\right)

where
X_{\widetilde{G}}
denotes the Hermitian symmetric domain for the standard Shimura datum associated with
\widetilde{G}=\operatorname{GSp}_{2n}
. Let
\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G}}
denote the Borel–Serre compactification of
X_{\widetilde{G}}
, and let
X_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}=e({Q_{\mathbf{j}}})
denote the boundary component corresponding to the parabolic
{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}
(see
[
NT16
, §3.1.2]
). Set

\mathfrak{X}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={Q_{\mathbf{j}}}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\left({Q_{\mathbf{j}}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\times X_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}\right),\quad\quad\operatorname{Ind}_{{Q_{\mathbf{j}}^{\infty}}}^{\widetilde{G}^{\infty}}\mathfrak{X}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={Q_{\mathbf{j}}}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\left(\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\times X_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}\right).

We also let
\mathfrak{X}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}=M_{\mathbf{j}}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(M_{\mathbf{j}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\times X_{M_{\mathbf{j}}})
, where
X_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}
denotes the symmetric space associated with
M_{\mathbf{j}}
. If we let
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{G}}
denote the Borel–Serre compactification of
\mathfrak{X}_{\widetilde{G}}
then, as explained in
[
NT16
, §3.1.2]
, one has a
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-equivariant locally closed embedding

\operatorname{Ind}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}^{\infty}}^{\widetilde{G}^{\infty}}\mathfrak{X}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}\hookrightarrow\partial\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{G}}

where
\partial\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{G}}=\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{G}}-\mathfrak{X}_{\widetilde{G}}
denotes the boundary. If
Q_{\mathbf{j}}
is a maximal proper parabolic (i.e.,
j_{1}=j
), we also have an open immersion
\mathfrak{X}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}\hookrightarrow\partial\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{G}}
.

Let
\widetilde{K}\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a sufficiently small compact open subgroup, and let
\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}}
denote the boundary of the Borel–Serre compatification of
X_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}}=\mathfrak{X}_{\widetilde{G}}/\widetilde{K}
. Then we have a locally closed embedding

X^{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\widetilde{K}}:=\operatorname{Ind}^{\widetilde{G}^{\infty}}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}^{\infty}}\mathfrak{X}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}/\widetilde{K}\hookrightarrow\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}}

which is an open immersion if
Q_{\mathbf{j}}
is maximal proper.

9.2.
Galois representations

We now recall the existence of Galois representations associated with characteristic zero and characteristic
p
cohomology classes following
[
Art13
]
and
[
Sch15
]
. These Galois representations all satisfy local-global compatibility for
\ell\not\in S
, as we make precise. In characteristic zero, we prove local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
for
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A})
in Theorem
9.2.4
, and recall it for
\GL_{n}
in Theorem
9.2.6
. In characteristic
p
, local-global compatibility for
\GL_{n}
at
\ell=p
(when the Galois representation is absolutely irreducible and decomposed generic) is one of our main results, and we do not prove it until Proposition
9.6.12
(see Remark
9.6.18
).

9.2.1.
Characteristic zero results

In this section, we fix an identification
\mathbb{C}\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, but omit this from the notation. Let
\mathscr{G}
denote either
\widetilde{G}
or
\GL_{n}
, let
\mathscr{T}\subset\mathscr{B}\subset\mathscr{G}
be the diagonal torus and upper-triangular Borel, and let
\mathscr{T}^{+}
be
T^{+}
or
T^{+}_{\GL_{n}}
(if
\mathscr{G}
is
\widetilde{G}
or
\GL_{n}
respectively). let
\Pi
be a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathscr{G}(\mathbb{A})
of weight
-w_{0}^{\mathscr{G}}\lambda
, with
\lambda\in X^{*}(\mathscr{T})^{+}
.

Definition 9.2.1
.

Let
J_{\mathscr{B}}(\Pi_{p})
denote the (normalised) Jacquet module of
\Pi_{p}
. We say
\Pi_{p}
is
ordinary
if there exists a one-dimensional subquotient of
J_{\mathscr{B}}(\Pi_{p})
given by a smooth character
\chi\colon\mathscr{T}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
satisfying

v_{p}(\chi(t))=v_{p}\left(\Big(\delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{1/2}\cdot w_{0}^{\mathscr{G}}\lambda\Big)(t)\right)

for all
t\in\mathscr{T}^{+}
(via the identification
\mathbb{C}\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
above).

Remark 9.2.2
.

Note that, if
\Pi_{p}
is ordinary, it is isomorphic to a subquotient of the normalised induction
\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\mathscr{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\chi
. Furthermore, let
\mathscr{I}(b,b)
be
\widetilde{\operatorname{Iw}}(b,b)
(if
\mathscr{G}=\widetilde{G}
) or
\operatorname{Iw}(b,b)
(if
\mathscr{G}=\GL_{n}
); then this ordinarity condition is equivalent to the existence of an integer
b\geqslant 1
and a non-zero element
v\in\Pi_{p}^{\mathscr{I}(b,b)}
which satisfies

[\mathscr{I}(b,b)\cdot t_{i}\cdot\mathscr{I}(b,b)]\cdot v=\alpha_{i}v

for all
i=0,1,\dots,n
, where
\alpha_{i}
has
p
-adic valuation equal to that of
(w_{0}^{\mathscr{G}}\lambda)(t_{i})
. Here
[\mathscr{I}(b,b)\cdot t_{i}\cdot\mathscr{I}(b,b)]
is the Hecke operator associated with the characteristic function of
\mathscr{I}(b,b)\cdot t_{i}\cdot\mathscr{I}(b,b)
. Note in particular that for
\mathscr{G}=\GL_{n}
, Definition
9.2.1
agrees with
[
Ger19
, Def. 5.3]
.

Definition 9.2.3
.

With notation as in Remark
9.2.2
, we define
(\Pi_{p}^{\mathscr{I}(b,b)})^{\operatorname{ord}}\subset\Pi_{p}^{\mathscr{I}(b,b)}
to be the maximal subspace stable under
[\mathscr{I}(b,b)\cdot t_{i}\cdot\mathscr{I}(b,b)]
for all
i=0,1,\dots,n
, and such that any eigenvalue for
[\mathscr{I}(b,b)\cdot t_{i}\cdot\mathscr{I}(b,b)]
has valuation equal to the
p
-adic valuation of
(w_{0}^{\mathscr{G}}\lambda)(t_{i})
. We define the
ordinary part

\Pi_{p}^{\operatorname{ord}}
of
\Pi_{p}
to be

\Pi_{p}^{\mathrm{ord}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigcup_{b\geqslant 1}(\Pi_{p}^{\mathscr{I}(b,b)})^{\operatorname{ord}}.

If
\Pi_{p}
is ordinary as in Definition
9.2.1
, then
\Pi_{p}^{\operatorname{ord}}
is one-dimensional, else it is zero (as
\lambda
is dominant; for
\GL_{n}
, this is
[
Ger19
, Lemma 5.4(2)]
, and for
\widetilde{G}
, the same idea works).

We first consider local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
for cuspidal automorphic representations of
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A})
.

Theorem 9.2.4
.

Let
\Pi
be a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation of
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A})
of weight
-w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\lambda
, with
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n};\lambda_{0})\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
, and sufficiently regular in the sense that
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}\geqslant 1
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
and
\lambda_{n}\geqslant 1
(so, in particular,
w\cdot\lambda\neq\lambda
for all
w\in W_{\widetilde{G}}
). Let
S
denote a finite set of primes containing
p
and all primes where
\Pi
is ramified. Then there exists a semisimple continuous Galois representation
\rho_{\Pi}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
satisfying the property that: for every prime
\ell\not\in S
,
\rho_{\Pi}
is unramified at
\ell
and one has

\operatorname{det}(1-\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}X|\rho_{\Pi})=\Theta^{S}_{\Pi}(\widetilde{H}_{\ell}(X))

where
\Theta^{S}_{\Pi}\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}^{S}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
denotes the eigencharacter for the action of the spherical Hecke algebra away from
S
acting on
(\Pi_{f}^{S})^{\widetilde{G}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S})}
.

Moreover, suppose that
\Pi_{p}
is ordinary (in the sense of Definition
9.2.1
) and let
\Theta_{\Pi,p}\colon\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}[T^{+}]\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
denote the eigencharacter for the action of the normalised
U_{p}
-Hecke operators acting on
\Pi_{p}^{\operatorname{ord}}
. Let
\psi_{\lambda,i}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
denote the characters as in Definition
9.1.2
associated with
\Theta_{\Pi}^{S}\otimes\Theta_{\Pi,p}
. Then:

\rho_{\Pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\psi_{\lambda,1}&*&*&*\\
&\psi_{\lambda,2}&*&*\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&\psi_{\lambda,2n+1}\end{array}\right),

that is, we have local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
.

We expect this result is well-known to experts in the field, but as we were unable to find a proof in the literature, we include one here.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, suppose that
\Pi
is unitary. Let
\Pi_{0}\subset\Pi|_{\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{A})}
be any (unitary) irreducible constituent of the restriction to an automorphic representation of
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{A})
. It is enough to establish the claim for
\Pi_{0}
since the standard representation of
\operatorname{GSpin}_{2n+1}={{}^{L}\operatorname{GSp}_{2n}}
factors through
\operatorname{SO}_{2n+1}={{}^{L}\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}}
.

By the work of Arthur
[
Art13
]
(as explained in
[
Sch15
, Theorem 5.1.2]
for example, although note that
loc.cit.
is for scalar weight),
\Pi_{0}
appears in the global
A
-packet associated with an elliptic
A
-parameter
\psi
; so there is a partition
2n+1=l_{1}N_{1}+\cdots+l_{r}N_{r}
and self-dual cuspidal automorphic representations
\Sigma_{i}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{A})
, such that:

•

For any
\ell\not\in S
, the representations
\Sigma_{i}
are unramified at
\ell
, and

\eta\varphi_{\Pi_{0,\ell}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\left(\varphi_{\Sigma_{i,\ell}}|\cdot|^{(1-l_{i})/2}\oplus\varphi_{\Sigma_{i,\ell}}|\cdot|^{(3-l_{i})/2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\varphi_{\Sigma_{i,\ell}}|\cdot|^{(l_{i}-1)/2}\right)

where
\varphi_{\Pi_{0,\ell}}
(resp.
\varphi_{\Sigma_{i,\ell}}
) denotes the unramified Langlands parameter associated with
\Pi_{0,\ell}
(resp.
\Sigma_{i,\ell}
), and
\eta\colon{{}^{L}\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}}\to{{}^{L}\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}}
is the map of dual groups given by the standard representation of
\operatorname{SO}_{2n+1}
.

•

The representation
\sigma_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Sigma_{i}\otimes|\!|\cdot|\!|^{(N_{i}-l_{i})/2}
is regular algebraic of weight
-w_{0}^{\operatorname{GL}_{N_{i}}}\mu_{i}
, and the union

\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}\{(w_{0}^{\operatorname{GL}_{N_{i}}}\mu_{i})_{j}+j-k:j=1,\dots,N_{i}\text{ and }k=1,\dots,l_{i}\}

is equal to

\{-\lambda_{1}-n,-\lambda_{2}-n+1,\dots,-\lambda_{n}-1,0,\lambda_{n}+1,\dots,\lambda_{2}+n-1,\lambda_{1}+n\}

as multisets.
11
11

11

Note that, by regular algebraic, we mean regular
C
-algebraic (see
[
BG14
]
).
Set
\xi_{i,k}^{(j)}=(w_{0}^{\operatorname{GL}_{N_{i}}}\mu_{i})_{j}+j-k
.

•

Since
\lambda
is sufficiently regular, by the preceding bullet point we must have
l_{i}=1
for all
i=1,\dots,r
. In particular,
\psi
is a generic
A
-parameter. For a finite prime
\ell
, let
\psi_{\ell}
denote the local
A
-parameter at
\ell
associated with
\psi
. By
[
Car12
, Theorem 1.2]
,
\Sigma_{i,\ell}
is tempered
12
12

12

To see this, choose an imaginary quadratic number field
F/\mathbb{Q}
in which
\ell
splits and such that
\Sigma_{i}
does not have CM by
F
. Then the quadratic base-change
\operatorname{BC}_{F}(\Sigma_{i})
of
\Sigma_{i}
to an automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is cuspidal, regular algebraic, and conjugate self-dual, and satisfies
\operatorname{BC}_{F}(\Sigma_{i})_{\lambda}\cong\Sigma_{i,\ell}
for any prime
\lambda|\ell
.
hence
\psi_{\ell}
is a tempered, generic
A
-parameter (or equivalently, a tempered
L
-parameter).

Every
\sigma_{i}
is a cuspidal essentially self-dual automorphic representation, so by the work of many people (see
[
CH13
,
BLGGT14b
]
for example), there exists a semisimple continuous representation

\rho_{\sigma_{i}}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{N_{i}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})

such that for every
\ell\not\in S
, one has

L_{\ell}(\rho_{\sigma_{i}},s)=L(\varphi_{\sigma_{i,\ell}|\cdot|^{(1-N_{i})/2}},s).

Furthermore,
\rho_{\sigma_{i}}
is de Rham at
p
with Hodge–Tate weights
\{\xi_{i,1}^{(j)}:j=1,\dots,N_{i}\}
. We then define

\rho_{\Pi_{0}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\left(\rho_{\sigma_{i}}\oplus\rho_{\sigma_{i}}(-1)\oplus\cdots\oplus\rho_{\sigma_{i}}(1-l_{i})\right)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\rho_{\sigma_{i}},

which satisfies the desired local-global compatibility at
\ell\not\in S
by the first bullet point above.

Suppose now that
\Pi_{p}
is ordinary. By Remark
9.2.2
, and as
\lambda
is sufficiently regular, Tadić’s irreducibility criterion
[
Tad94
, Thm. 7.9]
implies that
\Pi_{p}\cong\operatorname{Ind}_{B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\chi
is the normalised induction of a smooth character
\chi=(\chi_{1},...,\chi_{n};\chi_{0})
(as in Definition
9.2.1
). By Mackey theory,

\Pi_{p}|_{\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\cong I_{B^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\chi^{\prime}\cong\Pi_{0,p},

where
B^{\prime}
is the standard Borel in
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}
, and
\chi^{\prime}=(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{n})
is the corresponding character of the torus
T^{\prime}=T\cap B^{\prime}
. Here the second isomorphism follows from irreducibility of the middle induction (which is
[
Tad94
, Thm. 7.1]
; see also
[
Taï16
, Thm. 2.2.4]
).

The representation
\Pi_{0,p}
lies in the local
A
-packet associated with the tempered
L
-parameter
\psi_{p}
, hence
\Pi_{0,p}
is tempered (see
[
Art13
, Theorem 1.5.1(b)]
). Thus the characters
\chi_{i}
are unitary. Since
\Pi_{0,p}
lies in a unique
A
-packet (see
loc.cit.
), we must have that
\psi_{p}
is equivalent to the
A
-parameter

\psi_{p}=\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{n}\oplus\mathbf{1}\oplus\chi_{n}^{-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{1}^{-1},

where
\chi_{i}\colon W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\times\operatorname{SU}(2)\times\operatorname{SU}(2)\to\operatorname{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
which is trivial on the
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-factors and coincides with
\chi_{i}
on the
W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-factor. If we let

\pi=\Sigma_{1}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus\Sigma_{r}

denote the global base-change of
\Pi_{0}
to an automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{A})
, then we see that
\pi_{p}\cong I_{B_{\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{n}\oplus\mathbf{1}\oplus\chi_{n}^{-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{1}^{-1})
. We note that
\pi_{p}\cong\Sigma_{1,p}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus\Sigma_{r,p}
. As
\Pi_{p}
is ordinary, one may easily check that
\pi_{p}
is also ordinary, and that this can only happen if
\Sigma_{i,p}
are also ordinary, for all
i=1,\dots,r
.

Since
\lambda
is sufficiently regular, so is
\mu_{i}
and the Galois representation
\rho_{\sigma_{i}}
satisfies local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
(see
[
BLGGT14a
]
), i.e.,

\rho_{\sigma_{i}}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\chi_{\mu_{i},1}&*&*&*\\
&\chi_{\mu_{i},2}&*&*\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&\chi_{\mu_{i},N_{i}}\end{array}\right)

where
\chi_{\mu_{i},j}
are the characters from Definition
9.1.2
associated with the action of the Hecke algebra at
p
acting on
\sigma_{i,p}
. We note that the collection
\{\chi_{\mu_{i},j}:i=1,\dots,r\text{ and }j=1,\dots,N_{i}\}
coincides with the collection
\{\psi_{\lambda,j}:j=1,\dots,2n+1\}
, and by noting that the Hodge–Tate weights of
\chi_{\mu_{i},1},\dots,\chi_{\mu_{i},N_{i}}
have to be in increasing order, one can see that the filtration on

\rho_{\Pi_{0}}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\rho_{\sigma_{i}}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}

can be reordered so that the local-global compatibility property in the theorem holds.
∎

A key ingredient in the above proof was the known local-global compatibility results for regular algebraic, essentially self-dual, cuspidal automorphic representations of general linear groups. We will also need similar results without the essentially self-dual condition, as found in
[
ACC+23
, §5.5]
. For this, first recall the following definition from
[
ACC+23
, Def. 4.3.1]
(c.f.
[
CS17
, Def. 1.9]
, although note that the condition in
loc.cit.
is slightly stronger than what we need).

Definition 9.2.5
.

Let
\rho\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
be a continuous Galois representation which is unramified outside
S
. We say that
\rho
is
decomposed generic at a prime
\ell\notin S
if the eigenvalues of
\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}
, denoted
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}
, satisfy

\beta_{i}\beta_{j}^{-1}\neq\ell\text{ for all }i\neq j.

We say
\rho
is
decomposed generic
if there exists such a prime
\ell
(note that if there exists one such prime, then there exist infinitely many by the Chebotarev density theorem).

Theorem 9.2.6
.

Let
\Pi
be a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic representation of
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A})
of weight
-w_{0}^{\GL_{n}}\lambda
, with
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in X^{*}(T_{\GL_{n}})^{+}
. Let
S
denote a finite set of primes containing
p
and all primes where
\Pi
is ramified. Then there exists a continuous semisimple Galois representation
\rho_{\Pi}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{n}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that: for every
\ell\not\in S
,
\rho_{\Pi}
is unramified at
\ell
and one has

\operatorname{det}(1-\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}X|\rho_{\Pi})=\Theta_{\Pi}^{S}(H_{\ell}(X))

where
\Theta^{S}_{\Pi}\colon\mathbf{T}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}^{S}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
denotes the eigencharacter for the action of the spherical Hecke algebra away from
S
acting on
(\Pi_{f}^{S})^{\GL_{n}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S})}
.

Moreover, suppose that:

•

\Pi_{p}
is ordinary, and

•

the residual representation
\overline{\rho}_{\Pi}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
is decomposed generic and irreducible.

Then one has

\rho_{\Pi}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\chi_{\lambda,1}&*&*&*\\
&\chi_{\lambda,2}&*&*\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&\chi_{\lambda,n}\end{array}\right)

where
\chi_{\lambda,i}
denotes the characters associated with
\Theta_{\Pi}^{S}\otimes\Theta_{\Pi,p}
as in Definition
9.1.2
, with
\Theta_{\Pi,p}\colon\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}[T^{+}_{\GL_{n}}]\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
denoting the eigencharacter for the action of the normalised
U_{p}
-operators acting on
\Pi_{p}^{\mathrm{ord}}
.

Proof.

The existence of the Galois representation follows from
[
Sch15
]
(or
[
HLTT16
]
), and the local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
follows from
[
ACC+23
, Corollary 5.5.2]
(by making a suitable quadratic base-change to an imaginary quadratic field
F/\mathbb{Q}
in which
p
splits).
∎

9.2.2.
Characteristic
p
results

We now recall the construction of Galois representations associated with torsion classes appearing in
[
Sch15
]
.

Theorem 9.2.7

(Scholze)
.

Let
k=\mathcal{O}/\varpi
, and let
\mathfrak{n}
be a maximal ideal in the (prime-to-
S
) Hecke algebra
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\left(\operatorname{H}^{*}(X_{\widetilde{G},K},k)\right)
(resp.
\mathbf{T}^{S}\left(H^{*}(X_{\GL_{n},K},k\right)
) for some sufficiently small compact open subgroup
K\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
(resp.
K\subset\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
). There exists a continuous semisimple Galois representation

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2n+1}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\quad\text{ (resp. }\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\text{ ) }

such that for all
\ell\not\in S
, we have
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}}
is unramified at
\ell
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}(1-\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}X|\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{H}_{\ell}(X))

\displaystyle\text{ (resp. }\operatorname{det}(1-\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}X|\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{\mathfrak{n}}(H_{\ell}(X))\text{ )}.

Here
\Theta_{\mathfrak{n}}\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
(resp.
\Theta_{\mathfrak{n}}\colon\mathbf{T}^{S}\to\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
) denotes the homomorphism with kernel
\mathfrak{n}
.

Proof.

For the standard representation for general symplectic groups, the existence is explained in the proof of
[
Sch15
, Corollary 5.2.6]
(and relies on Arthur’s endscopic classification for symplectic groups
[
Art13
]
). The existence for general linear groups again follows from
[
Sch15
]
(which, in fact, uses the existence for general symplectic groups as input).
∎

9.3.
Vanishing results and boundary cohomology

We now collect together key cohomological results; firstly, a vanishing result of Yang and Zhu in characteristic
p
, and secondly, an analysis of boundary cohomology that will allow the transfer of classes from
\widetilde{G}
to
M
via a degree-shifting argument.

9.3.1.
Vanishing results

Recall that
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is a finite extension with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
and unformiser
\varpi
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbf{T}^{S}
be a maximal ideal with residue field
k=\mathcal{O}/\varpi
, and let
\Theta\colon\mathbf{T}^{S}\twoheadrightarrow k
denote the corresponding
\mathcal{O}
-algebra morphism with kernel
\mathfrak{m}
.

Assumption 9.3.1
.

We assume that there exists a continuous semisimple Galois representation

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{n}(k)

such that for every finite prime
\ell\not\in S
, the Galois representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is unramified at
\ell
and one has

\operatorname{det}(1-\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}X|\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})=\Theta(H_{\ell}(X)).

We assume that
\mathfrak{m}
is non-Eisenstein, i.e.,
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible.

Let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}=S^{*}(\mathfrak{m})
denote the pullback of
\mathfrak{m}
under the unnormalised Satake morphism. We set

\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\vee}(1)\oplus\mathbf{1}\oplus\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}(-1)

where
V(j)
is shorthand for
V\otimes\omega^{j}
, and
\mathbf{1}
denotes the trivial representation. By (
9.1.5
), we see that
\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is a continuous semisimple Galois representation, which is unramified outside
S
and satisfies
\operatorname{det}(1-\operatorname{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}X|\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}})=(\Theta\circ\mathcal{S})(\widetilde{H}_{\ell}(X))
for
\ell\notin S
.

We will use the following vanishing result.

Theorem 9.3.2

(Yang–Zhu
[
YZ25
]
)
.

Suppose that
p>2n
. Let
K\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a neat compact open subgroup of the form
K=K^{S}K_{S\backslash\{p\}}K_{p}
with
K^{S}=\widetilde{G}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{S})
,
K_{S\backslash\{p\}}\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{S\backslash\{p\}})
, and
K_{p}\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
. Suppose that
\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is decomposed generic at an odd prime
\ell\not\in S
. Then for all
m\geqslant 1
,

\operatorname{H}^{i}\left(X_{\widetilde{G},K},V_{\lambda}/\varpi^{m}\right)_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0

if
i<n(n+1)/2
, and

\operatorname{H}_{c}^{i}\left(X_{\widetilde{G},K},V_{\lambda}/\varpi^{m}\right)_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0

if
i>n(n+1)/2
.

Proof.

By deepening
K_{p}
and using the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence, it suffices to prove the theorem when
\lambda
is trivial. But now we note that
\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
being decomposed generic at
\ell
implies that the
L
-parameter
W_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}\to{{}^{L}T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
corresponding to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
is generic in the sense of
[
YZ25
, Def. 1.1]
(note that
\operatorname{GSpin}_{2n+1}
is a central extension of
\operatorname{SO}_{2n+1}
). Applying
[
YZ25
, Thm. 1.5]
yields the result for
m=1
, noting that
2n
is the Coxeter number of
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}
. To conclude for general
m
, we argue inductively using the long exact sequence associated with
0\to V_{\lambda}/\varpi^{m-1}\to V_{\lambda}/\varpi^{m}\to V_{\lambda}/\varpi\to 0
.
∎

Remark 9.3.3
.

We note that in the special case of
\operatorname{GSp}_{4}
, the above vanishing results were originally obtained by Hamann–Lee
[
HL23
]
under some additional hypotheses. We are primarily interested in the group
\operatorname{GSp}_{6}
in this article, which is not covered by their work.

9.3.2.
Boundary cohomology

As a consequence of the existence of Galois representations above, we obtain certain vanishing results of the boundary cohomology of the Borel–Serre compactification of
\widetilde{G}
. Let
\widetilde{K}\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a sufficiently small compact open subgroup hyperspecial outside
S
.

Proposition 9.3.4
.

Recall the ideal
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
from §
9.3.1
.

(1)

If
Q_{\mathbf{j}}\neq P
, then
R\Gamma_{c}(X^{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\widetilde{K}},\mathcal{O})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=R\Gamma(X^{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\widetilde{K}},\mathcal{O})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0
.

(2)

One has a natural
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
-equivariant quasi-isomorphism

R\Gamma(X_{\widetilde{K}}^{P},\mathcal{O})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}R\Gamma(\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}},\mathcal{O})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is very similar to that of
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 2.4.2]
. By Poincaré duality and the fact that
R\Gamma(X^{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\widetilde{K}},\mathcal{O})
is a perfect complex, it suffices to show

R\Gamma(X^{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\widetilde{K}},k)_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0,

i.e. that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
doesn’t descend to a maximal ideal of
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(H^{*}(X^{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\widetilde{K}},k))
. Suppose for contradiction that it does. We may freely increase the level
\widetilde{K}
at primes in
S
such that we have a decomposition

\widetilde{K}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}=\widetilde{K}\cap Q_{\mathbf{j}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})=(\widetilde{K}\cap N_{\mathbf{j}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))\cdot\widetilde{K}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}},

where
\widetilde{K}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}
is the projection of
\widetilde{K}_{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}
to the Levi, and
N_{\mathbf{j}}\subset Q_{\mathbf{j}}
is the unipotent radical.

In this proof, we will denote the Hecke algebra associated with
M_{\mathbf{j}}
by
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{j}}^{S}
. We note that we have an unnormalised Satake morphism

\mathcal{S}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{j}}^{S}

defined in the exact same way as in §
9.1.3
(restricting to
Q_{\mathbf{j}}
and integrating along the fibres of the map
Q_{\mathbf{j}}\to M_{\mathbf{j}}
). We also have the following identity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}(\widetilde{H}_{\ell}(X))=\widetilde{H}^{(2(n-j))}_{\ell}(X)\cdot H^{(j_{1})}_{\ell}(\ell^{n+1-j_{1}}X)\cdot H^{(j_{2})}_{\ell}(\ell^{n+1-j_{1}-j_{2}}X)\cdots H^{(j_{a})}_{\ell}(\ell^{n+1-j}X)\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot H^{(j_{a}),\vee}_{\ell}(\ell^{j-1-n}X)\cdots H^{(j_{2}),\vee}_{\ell}(\ell^{j_{1}+j_{2}-1-n}X)\cdot H^{(j_{1}),\vee}_{\ell}(\ell^{j_{1}-1-n}X)

analogous to (
9.1.5
), where
\widetilde{H}^{(2(n-j))}_{\ell}(X)
,
H_{\ell}^{(j_{i})}(X)
and
H_{\ell}^{(j_{i}),\vee}(X)
denote the versions of the Hecke polynomials in Definition
9.1.3
and Definition
9.1.4
respectively for the groups
\operatorname{GSp}_{2(n-j)}
and
\operatorname{GL}_{j_{i}}
.

By a similar argument as in
[
NT16
, Theorem 4.2]
(and possibly increasing the level
\widetilde{K}
further at primes in
S
combined with
[
NT16
, Lemma 4.3]
), as
R\Gamma(X^{Q_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\widetilde{K}},k)_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\neq 0
one has
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathcal{S}^{*}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}(\mathfrak{n})
for some ideal
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{j}}^{S}(\operatorname{H}^{*}(X_{M_{\mathbf{j}},\widetilde{K}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}},k))
. By deepening the level further, we may assume that
\widetilde{K}_{M_{\mathbf{j}}}
can be written as a product

K_{1}\times\cdots\times K_{a}\times K_{0}

where
K_{i}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{j_{i}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
(
i=1,\dots,a
) and
K_{0}\subset\operatorname{GSp}_{2(n-j)}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. By the Künneth formula, the ideal
\mathfrak{n}
can be written as the product

\mathfrak{n}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{n}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathfrak{n}_{a}\otimes\mathfrak{n}_{0}

for
\mathfrak{n}_{i}\subset\mathbf{T}^{S}(\operatorname{H}^{*}(X_{\operatorname{GL}_{j_{i}},K_{i}},k))
(
i=1,\dots,a
) and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(\operatorname{H}^{*}(X_{\operatorname{GSp}_{2(n-j)},K_{0}},k))
.

By Theorem
9.2.7
, we have

\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\cong\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}(j_{1}-n-1)\oplus\cdots\oplus\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}}(j-n-1)\oplus\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}\oplus\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}}^{\vee}(n+1-j)\oplus\cdots\oplus\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}(n+1-j_{1})

On the other hand, we know that
\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\cong\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}(-1)\oplus\mathbf{1}\oplus\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\vee}(1)
, and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible (since
\mathfrak{m}
is non-Eisenstein). The only way we can have both of these presentations of
\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is if
a=1
and
j=n
, which is a contradiction to our assumption that
Q_{\mathbf{j}}\neq P
. This completes the proof of part (1). For part (2), we follow the same argument as in
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 2.4.2]
, namely the isomorphism arises from the excision exact triangle.
∎

9.3.3.

We now analyse the cohomology
R\Gamma(X_{\widetilde{K}}^{P},\mathcal{O})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
. Let
\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a sufficiently small compact open subgroup and suppose that

\widetilde{K}\cap P(\mathbb{A}_{f})=(\widetilde{K}\cap N(\mathbb{A}_{f}))\cdot K

for some sufficiently small compact open subgroup
K=K^{p}K_{p}\subset M(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Suppose that
\widetilde{K}
is hyperspecial outside
S
, and that
\widetilde{K}^{p}_{S}
consists of those matrices in
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{S-\{p\}})
which are congruent to the identity modulo
N_{S}
, for some integer
N_{S}
divisible only by primes in
S-\{p\}
. We let
R\Gamma(X^{P}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{sm}}
denote the (derived)
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-smooth vectors in the cohomology of
X^{P}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}}=\operatorname{Ind}_{P^{\infty}}^{G^{\infty}}\mathfrak{X}_{P}/\widetilde{K}^{p}
(as in
[
ACC+23
, §5.2.1]
).

Proposition 9.3.5
.

Let
m\geqslant 1
. Then we have a
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
-equivariant quasi-isomorphism

R\Gamma(X^{P}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{sm}}\cong\bigoplus_{P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{S-\{p\}})/\widetilde{K}^{p}_{S}}\operatorname{Ind}_{P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}R\Gamma(X_{M,K^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{sm}}

in the derived category of smooth representations of
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Here
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
acts on the right-hand side through the Satake morphism
\mathcal{S}\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to\mathbf{T}^{S}
.

Proof.

This follows from the exact same argument as in the proof of
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 5.4.1]
, noting that
\widetilde{K}^{p}_{S}
is a normal subgroup of
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{S-\{p\}})
(there is no place in the argument, nor in the citations to
[
NT16
]
, which is specific to the authors’ choice of reductive groups).
∎

9.4.
The degree shifting argument

Throughout this section, fix
m\geqslant 1
and let
\mathcal{C}
be a bounded from below complex in the derived category of smooth
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m}[M(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-modules. We will often view this as a representation of
P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via inflation. We recall (see
[
ACC+23
, §5.2.1]
) that the derived ordinary part of
\mathcal{C}
is

\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ord}}=\operatorname{ord}R\Gamma(U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\mathcal{C})=R\Gamma(U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\mathcal{C})\otimes^{L}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m}[T_{M}^{+}]}\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m}[T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]

where
U_{M}
is the unipotent of the upper-triangular Borel in
M
. Here the action of
t\in T_{M}^{+}
on
R\Gamma(U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\mathcal{C})
is through the Hecke operator

[U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cdot t\cdot U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})]\cdot v=\sum_{u\in U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/tU_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})t^{-1}}ut\cdot v.

We have a similar definition for smooth representations of
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, by first passing to derived
U(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
invariants and then localising along
T^{+}\hookrightarrow T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. The goal of this section is to compute the ordinary part of the smooth induction
\operatorname{Ind}_{P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathcal{C})
following
[
ACC+23
, §5.3]
.

We recall the following functors from
loc.cit.
. Let
w\in{{}^{M}W}
and set
S_{w}=P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cdot w\cdot B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cdot w\cdot U(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
S_{w}^{\circ}=P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cdot w\cdot U(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Set

G_{\geqslant i}=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in{{}^{M}W}\\
l(w)\geqslant i\end{subarray}}S_{w},

which is an open subset of
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Definition 9.4.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a smooth representation of
P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We define

\displaystyle I_{\geqslant i}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle=\left\{f\colon G_{\geqslant i}\to\mathcal{C}:\begin{array}[]{c}f\text{ loc. constant, compact support mod }P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\\
f(p\cdot-)=p\cdot f(-)\;\forall p\in P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle I_{w}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle=\left\{f\colon S_{w}\to\mathcal{C}:\begin{array}[]{c}f\text{ loc. constant, compact support mod }P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\\
f(p\cdot-)=p\cdot f(-)\;\forall p\in P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle I^{\circ}_{w}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle=\{f\in I_{w}(\mathcal{C}):\operatorname{supp}(f)\subset S^{\circ}_{w}\}.

The first and second are representations of
B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via right-translation. The third is a representation of
B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})^{+}=U(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cdot T^{+}\cdot U(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
via right-translation. Of course,
I_{\geqslant 0}
is the usual smooth induction from
P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
to
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, viewed as a representation of
B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. By the same argument as in the proof of
[
ACC+23
, Proposition 5.3.1]
(see, also,
[
Hau16
, §2]
), all of these functors are exact and for any smooth representation
\mathcal{C}
of
P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, we have functorial short exact sequences

0\to I_{\geqslant i+1}(\mathcal{C})\to I_{\geqslant i}(\mathcal{C})\to\oplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in{{}^{M}W}\\
l(w)=i\end{subarray}}I_{w}(\mathcal{C})\to 0.

Furthermore, by the same proof as in
[
ACC+23
, Lemma 5.3.4]
(see, also,
[
Hau16
, Lemme 3.3.1]
), we have
I^{\circ}_{w}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}}\cong I_{w}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}}
for any bounded below complex
\mathcal{C}
in the derived category of smooth representations of
P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Set
U_{w}=P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap wU(\mathbb{Z}_{p})w^{-1}
, which contains
U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, and recall that
N
denotes the unipotent radical of
P
. For a character
\chi\colon T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, let
\mathcal{O}(\chi)
denote the rank one
\mathcal{O}
-module equipped with an action of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
through the character
\chi
.

Proposition 9.4.2
.

Let
w\in{{}^{M}W}
and let
\chi_{w}\colon T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
denote the character given by

\chi_{w}(t)=\frac{\operatorname{det}(\operatorname{Ad}(wtw^{-1})|\operatorname{Lie}(N(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap wU(\mathbb{Q}_{p})w^{-1}))^{-1}}{|\operatorname{det}(\operatorname{Ad}(wtw^{-1})|\operatorname{Lie}(N(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap wU(\mathbb{Q}_{p})w^{-1}))|_{p}}.

Then:

(1)

One has a
T^{+}
-equivariant quasi-isomorphism
R\Gamma(U(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),I^{\circ}_{w}(\mathcal{C}))\cong R\Gamma(U_{w},\mathcal{C})
, where
T^{+}
acts on the right-hand side through the Hecke operator
[U_{w}\cdot(wtw^{-1})\cdot U_{w}]
.

(2)

Suppose that the action of
P(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
on
\mathcal{C}
factors through
M(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Then

I_{w}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}}\cong R\Gamma(U_{w},\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}}\cong\mathcal{O}(\chi_{w})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\tau_{w}^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ord}}[l(w)-n(n+1)/2]

where
\tau_{w}^{-1}
means the action of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is twisted through the map
t\mapsto wtw^{-1}
.

Proof.

For part (1), we follow
[
ACC+23
, Lemma 5.3.5]
, namely we just need to show that the natural map

\operatorname{H}^{0}(U(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),I^{\circ}_{w}(\mathcal{C}))\to\operatorname{H}^{0}(U_{w},\mathcal{C})

given by
f\mapsto f(w)
is equivariant for the Hecke action. But this amounts to checking

\sum_{u\in U(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/tU(\mathbb{Z}_{p})t^{-1}}f(w\cdot ut)=\sum_{v\in U_{w}/(wtw^{-1})U_{w}(wtw^{-1})^{-1}}v(wtw^{-1})\cdot f(w)

which follows from exactly the same proof as in
loc.cit.
.

For part (2), we follow
[
ACC+23
, Lemma 5.3.7]
(note that, in our notation, the roles of
U
and
N
are reversed compared to
loc.cit.
). Let
U_{w,N}=U_{w}\cap N(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. The key point is to then compute
A\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=R\Gamma(U_{w,N},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})
which is equipped with the natural action of
U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
(since
U_{w}/U_{w,N}=U_{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
) and
t\in T^{+}
via the Hecke action of
wtw^{-1}
. More precisely, we want to compute

\alpha A=\alpha R\Gamma(U_{w,N},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=R\Gamma(U_{w,N},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}[T^{+}]}\mathcal{O}[T_{M}^{+}]

where the localisation is via the map
T^{+}\to T_{M}^{+}
given by
t\mapsto wtw^{-1}
. Note that
R\Gamma(U_{w,N},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})
already carries an action of
T^{+}
via the (non-twisted) Hecke action of
t\in T^{+}
(since
tU_{w,N}t^{-1}\subset U_{w,N}
), and
\alpha A
is then the localisation along the non-twisted map
T^{+}\subset T_{M}^{+}
.

Set
d=n(n+1)/2
and
z_{p}=\operatorname{diag}(p,\dots,p,1,\dots,1)\in T^{+}
, where there are
n
lots of
p
. Then this acts on
\operatorname{H}^{i}(U_{w,N},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})
by multiplication by
p^{d-l(w)-i}
(since
U_{w,N}
has
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-rank
d-l(w)
), but applying
\alpha
inverts this action. Hence
\alpha A
is concentrated in degree
d-l(w)
, and
[
Hau16
, Proposition 3.1.8]
tells us that
\alpha\operatorname{H}^{d-l(w)}(U_{w,N},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})
is free of rank one over
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m}
with the action of
T_{M}^{+}
given by the character

t\mapsto\frac{\operatorname{det}(\operatorname{Ad}(t)|\operatorname{Lie}U_{w,N})^{-1}}{|\operatorname{det}(\operatorname{Ad}(t)|\operatorname{Lie}U_{w,N})|_{p}}.

The rest of the proposition follows as in
[
ACC+23
, Lemma 5.3.7]
.
∎

9.5.
Transfer map on Hecke algebras

We now specialise to the ordinary setting. We explain how to apply the degree shifting results in Proposition
9.4.2
to construct a map of ordinary Hecke algebras (acting faithfully on certain cohomology groups) from
\widetilde{G}
to
M
, and subsequently to
\GL_{n}
.

Let
p>2n
. Let
m\geqslant 1
and
c\geqslant b\geqslant 0
with
c\geqslant 1
, and let
\widetilde{K}
and
K
be as in §
9.3.3
with the additional condition that
\widetilde{K}_{p}=\widetilde{\operatorname{Iw}}(b,c)
and
K_{p}=\operatorname{Iw}(b,c)
. Set
d=n(n+1)/2
for brevity. Let
\widetilde{\lambda}
be a dominant weight for
\widetilde{G}
. We assume that
\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is decomposed generic. From the excision exact sequence, we obtain a Hecke-equivariant map

(9.5.1)

\operatorname{H}^{d}(X_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}},V_{\widetilde{\lambda}}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{d}(\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}},V_{\widetilde{\lambda}}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}

which is surjective by Yang–Zhu’s vanishing result
\operatorname{H}^{d+1}_{c}(X_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}},V_{\widetilde{\lambda}}/\varpi^{m})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0
(Theorem
9.3.2
). Here, the ordinary part is as usual the direct summand on which the
\widetilde{U}_{t}
-operators act invertibly.

We will construct the transfer by applying degree-shifting to
\mathcal{C}=R\Gamma(X_{M,K^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
. The next two lemmas isolate the weight contribution and relate the cohomologies of
\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}}
and
X_{M,K}
. Let
T(b)\subset T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
denote the subgroup of elements which are congruent to the identity modulo
p^{b}
, and recall that
w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}
(resp.
w_{0}^{M}
) denotes the longest Weyl element of
\widetilde{G}
(resp.
M
).

Lemma 9.5.2
.

Let
\widetilde{\lambda}
and
\lambda
be
\widetilde{G}
-dominant and
M
-dominant weights respectively, which we view as continuous characters
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
by precomposing with the natural map
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\twoheadrightarrow T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. We have a
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
-equivariant (resp.
\mathbf{T}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
-equivariant) quasi-isomorphism

\displaystyle R\Gamma(\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}},V_{\widetilde{\lambda}}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}

\displaystyle\cong R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes R\Gamma(\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}),

\displaystyle\text{(resp. }R\Gamma(X_{M,K},V_{\lambda}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}

\displaystyle\cong R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{M}\lambda)\otimes R\Gamma(X_{M,K^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}})).

Proof.

The second quasi-isomorphism is
[
ACC+23
, Props. 5.2.15, 5.2.17]
. The first is proved via the same arguments.
∎

Lemma 9.5.3
.

One has a surjective morphism

\operatorname{H}^{d}\left(R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes R\Gamma(\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}})\right)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{d}\left(R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes I_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}})\right).

This is
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
-equivariant, where the action of
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
on the target is via
\mathcal{S}\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to\mathbf{T}^{S}
and the action of
T^{+}
is induced from its action on
\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes I_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}}
.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 5.4.1]
. Let
R\Gamma_{\widetilde{K}^{p},\operatorname{sm}}
denote the derived functor of taking
\widetilde{K}^{p}
-invariants and
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-smooth vectors on representations of
\widetilde{G}^{\infty}=\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

We first claim that one has a Hecke-equivariant quasi-isomorphism:

\displaystyle(R\Gamma_{\widetilde{K}^{p},\operatorname{sm}}R\Gamma(\operatorname{Ind}_{P^{\infty}}^{\widetilde{G}^{\infty}}\mathfrak{X}_{P},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m}))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle\cong(R\Gamma_{\widetilde{K}^{p},\operatorname{sm}}R\Gamma(\partial\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{G}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m}))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{R\Gamma}(\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}^{p}},\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{sm}}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}.

Indeed, this follows from the same argument as in
loc.cit.
using Proposition
9.3.4
. The lemma now follows from Proposition
9.3.5
.
∎

Now, following the same argument as in
[
ACC+23
, Lemma 5.3.3]
, for any
i\geqslant 0
we have Hecke-equivariant short exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to\operatorname{H}^{d}

\displaystyle\left(R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes I_{\geqslant i+1}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}})\right)\to\operatorname{H}^{d}\left(R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes I_{\geqslant i}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}})\right)

(9.5.4)

\displaystyle\to\bigoplus_{l(w)=i}\operatorname{H}^{d}\left(R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes I_{w}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}})\right)\to 0.

Arguing inductively, and combining with Lemmas
9.5.2
and
9.5.3
, for each
w\in{{}^{M}}W
we obtain a
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{S,\operatorname{ord}}
-equivariant subquotient

(9.5.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{d}(\partial\overline{X}_{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K}},V_{\widetilde{\lambda}}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{H}^{d}\left(R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes I_{w}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}})\right).}}

Here the notation “
” means the target is a subquotient of the source. Now we apply degree-shifting to the image. Let
x=w_{0}^{M}ww_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\in{{}^{M}W}
and recall
\lambda_{x}=x\star\widetilde{\lambda}=x\cdot(\widetilde{\lambda}+\rho)-\rho
. Note that
l(w)=d-l(x)=n(n+1)/2-l(x)
.

Lemma 9.5.6
.

We have

\operatorname{H}^{d}\left(R\Gamma(T(b),\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\lambda})\otimes I_{w}(\mathcal{C})^{\operatorname{ord}})\right)\cong\tau_{w}^{-1}\operatorname{H}^{l(w)}(X_{M,K},V_{\lambda_{x}}/\varpi^{m})^{\operatorname{ord}}_{\mathfrak{m}}.

Proof.

Applying Proposition
9.4.2
to
\mathcal{C}
, we have
